Vortex-enhanced photovoltaic current in disordered topological materials
Pavlo Sukhachov, Penghao Zhu, Ella Banyas, Liang Z. Tan, A. Alexandradinata

Abstract
In disordered topological materials, real-space crystalline defects interplay with momentum-space wave function singularities to enhance the bulk photovoltaic current. What’s singular is the interband Berry phase, or equivalently the phase of the optical dipole matrix element, which has a vortex structure in momentum space. Such optical vorticity is guaranteed to exist in all topological materials associated with nontrivial Chern numbers. These vortices enhance electron-impurity skew scattering, which manifests as a ballistic photovoltaic current that is sensitive to (a) the topological material class, (b) the symmetry class of crystalline defects, and (c) the light polarization. This sensitivity manifests in two ways: firstly, by (a-c)-dependent frequency exponents for the photovoltaic current ∝ ω exponent \propto\omega^{\text{exponent}} in topological semimetals, with ω \omega the frequency of the light source. Secondly, by (a-c)-dependent constraints of the bulk photovoltaic tensor, which are explainable only by emergent, magnetic symmetries of time-reversal-invariant topological materials. These ideas are concretized by case studies on multifold fermions, 3D m m -order Weyl semimetals and 2D n n -order Dirac systems, which include n n -layer rhombohedral graphene, transition metal dichalcogenides, and topological surface states. Theoretical guidance is provided for a tri-pronged experimental program that combines frequency-tuned photoconductivity measurements, defect characterization and defect engineering.



I
Introduction and outline

Irrespective of a material’s symmetry, the longitudinal linear conductivity is usually degraded by an increasing concentration of crystalline defects.
29
;
10
Nonlinear optical responses can be qualitatively different: only in noncentrosymmetric materials can a dynamic electric field (associated to a light wave) drive a
bulk photovoltaic
current in quadratic response,
42
and this current can be enhanced by crystalline defects.
16
;
13
How is this possible? It is not as simple as saying that noncentrosymmetric defects (e.g., electric dipolar impurities) reduce the symmetry of the material, because centrosymmetric defects (e.g., electric monopolar impurities) can also mediate a bulk photovoltaic current, as a matter of principle.
37
Evidently, centrosymmetry-breaking of the material’s band structure must also play a role in activating the bulk photovoltaic current. But there is no centrosymmetry-breaking of the band energy-momentum relation in non-magnetic materials, because
E_{\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
is guaranteed by time-reversal symmetry. Apparently, it must be a centrosymmetry-breaking of the electron wave function:
|\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}})\neq\text{Inversion}|\psi_{-\boldsymbol{k}})
.

What geometric or topological properties of the electron wave function are relevant to the component of the bulk photovoltaic current which grows in proportionality to the concentration of crystalline defects? This paper proposes that in disordered topological materials, momentum-space wave function singularities interplay with real-space crystalline defects
to enhance the bulk photovoltaic current. These are singularities of the interband Berry connection:
\boldsymbol{A}_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}=\langle u_{v\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,i\boldsymbol{\nabla_{k}}u_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\rangle
between the low-energy valence band and the high-energy conduction band. The manifestation of a singularity is a vortex structure in the vector field
\boldsymbol{\nabla_{k}}\arg\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}
for any vector
\boldsymbol{e}
, as illustrated in Fig.
1
(a). Because
\boldsymbol{e}
can be interpreted as the polarization vector of a source-driven light wave, and
i\boldsymbol{\nabla_{k}}
corresponds in quantum mechanics to the real-space position, the aforementioned vortex structure is equivalently a
vortex
structure in the phase of the
optical
dipole matrix element.

Figure 1:
Topological band-touching
\boldsymbol{k}
-points (orange dots) are not just sources/sinks of the intraband Berry curvature but also sources/sinks of vorticity in the interband Berry phase. In panel (a), a brown-colored
optical vortex line threads two band-touching Weyl points (of opposite Weyl charge) and is wrapped by the circulating vector field:
\boldsymbol{\nabla_{k}}\arg\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{vc\boldsymbol{k}}
.
(b) For a topological semimetal, asymmetric electron-defect scattering results in the electron distribution
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\neq f_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
.
(c) An optical vortex makes the photo-excitation rate anisotropic over
\boldsymbol{k}
-space: largest at the equator, zero at the poles, as indicated by smoothly varying colors. An electron photo-excited at
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
(on the equator) can subsequently collide with a defect and scatter directly (green arrow) to
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
(at the north pole), or scatter indirectly (magenta arrow) as
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}
. The quantum interference between both scattering processes results in the asymmetric scattering of panel (b), and is fundamentally characterized by the Bargmann invariant:
\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle
, a wave function geometric characteristic that is generally non-reducible to the quantum metric or Berry curvature.

Such
optical vortices
are guaranteed to exist in all topological materials associated with nontrivial Chern numbers, including (but not exhausted by) Chern insulators,
n
-order Weyl semimetals,
31
multifold fermions with pseudospin-1 (spin-one Weyl fermion) and -3/2 (Rarita-Schwinger fermions).
19
This topological guarantee of optical vorticity follows from a theorem
83
relating the vorticity of the
inter
band Berry phase to the quantized
intra
band Berry phase, which is what a Chern number is.
Historically, topological materials have been characterized by their intraband Berry phase, which is the holonomy associated with the parallel transport of an electron within a band.
68
;
34
;
70
One main message of this paper is that an alternative history could have happened in which topological materials were first characterized by their interband Berry phase.

The second main message of this paper is worth reiterating:

\displaystyle{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Optical vortex in}\\
\text{disordered topological materials}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\rightarrow\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Asymmetric electron-defect}\\
\text{scattering}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\rightarrow\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Ballistic}\\
\text{photovoltaic current}\end{subarray}}

(1)

By ‘asymmetric’, we mean that electron-defect scattering creates an electron distribution
f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}
that is non-symmetric in inverting
\boldsymbol{k}
, with
b
being a band index, and with the difference
(f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}})
being proportional to the light intensity, as illustrated in Fig.
1
(b). The resultant photocurrent is called the

\displaystyle{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Defect-mediated ballistic}\\
\text{photovoltaic current density}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\mathbf{j}_{\text{ballistic}}=-\frac{|e|}{2\text{Volume}}\sum_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{v}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}(f_{b\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}),}

(2)

with the group velocity satisfying
\boldsymbol{v}_{b\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{v}_{b,-\boldsymbol{k}}
owing to time-reversal symmetry. The qualifier of ‘defect-mediated’ specifies the scattering mechanism behind
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
; there are other mechanisms
13
;
15
;
25
;
24
;
67
of the bulk photovoltaic current which are not the focus of this work.

As a general property of bulk photovoltaic currents,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
is distinguished phenomenologically (from conventional photovoltaic currents
74
) by its sensitivity to the polarization of the driving light wave, which is encoded in the photovoltaic tensor,
B^{\text{de}}
, defined by

\displaystyle{j_{\text{ballistic}}^{a}=\sum_{bc}B^{\text{de}}_{abc}{\cal E}^{b}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}\overline{{\cal E}^{c}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}};\;\;\;\;a,b,c\in\{x,y,z\},}

(3)

with
\boldsymbol{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}
being an electric wave of wavevector
\boldsymbol{q}
and frequency
\omega
.
A bulk photovoltaic theory of disordered topological materials must therefore
interconnect (a) the polarization sensitivity of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
to (b) the symmetry class of defects and (c) the topological class of the underlying pristine material
. This interconnection forms the
central theme
for the rest of the paper, and is explored in two related ways:

(i) Firstly, because topological semimetals have a topologically-enforced zero band gap, they are uniquely fingerprinted by the low-frequency bulk photovoltaic response which has a power law form:
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}\propto\omega^{\text{exponent}}
. It will turn out that this frequency exponent depends on the factors (a-c) of our
central theme
. A representative result for
n
-order Weyl semimetals is given in Fig.
2
and will be greatly generalized.

Figure 2:
The frequency exponent depends on the order
n
of
n
-Weyl fermions, the light polarization (linear polarization vector parallel or orthogonal to the rotation axis), and the impurity type (electric dipole moment parallel or orthogonal to the rotation axis).

(ii) Secondly, the low-frequency bulk photovoltaic response (of disordered topological materials) is determined by low-energy effective Hamiltonians whose point-group symmetries are determined by the
(b) the symmetry class of defects and (c) the topological class of the underlying pristine material
. We have systematically determined what these symmetries are for Dirac-Weyl topological systems and for point defects (
\equiv

impurities
) with electric monopole and/or dipole moments. These symmetries are encoded as constraints on the photovoltaic tensor (e.g.,
B^{\text{de}}_{abc}=0
or
B^{\text{de}}_{abc}=\pm B^{\text{de}}_{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}
) which determines the
(a) polarization-dependence of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
.

As a representative illustration, consider
n
-order Dirac fermions characterized by a Berry phase of
n\pi
and materialized by
n
-layer rhombohedral graphene, or
n
-
graphene
in short.
41
The symmetry of the low-energy effective Hamiltonian
41
(of pristine
n
-graphene) is the magnetic point group
\bar{3}^{\prime}m
, which contains
C_{3v}
as a subgroup and additionally magnetic symmetries like
TS_{6}\equiv
(time reversal) composed with (improper six-fold reflection). Magnetic symmetries like
TS_{6}
impose symmetry constraints on the photovoltaic tensor (
B^{\text{de}}
) that we formalize into a symmetry theorem, newly presented here. The symmetry of disordered
n
-graphene can be lower than that of
\bar{3}^{\prime}m
depending on the symmetry class of crystalline defects (e.g., monopolar vs dipolar impurity), and depending on whether a Dirac mass is generated externally by an out-of-plane displacement field or generated internally by a correlation-induced, band-energy gap. A lower symmetry than
\bar{3}^{\prime}m
relaxes some of the symmetry constraints on
B^{\text{de}}
, which thus affects the polarization dependence of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
. We see (in
n
-graphene) the tight interconnection of ideas encapsulated in our
central theme
, which we will elaborate on and generalize to other classes of topological materials.

Here is an outline of our paper.

Sec.
II
: Optical vortices in topological semimetals and Dirac-Weyl materials

•

II.1
: A Chern-vorticity theorem relates the quantized intraband Berry phase to the vorticity of the interband Berry phase.

•

II.2
: This theorem is applied to uncover the structure of optical vorticity in 3D topological semimetals associated with nontrivial Chern numbers, including isotropic Weyl fermions, anisotropic
n
-Weyl fermions and multi-fold fermions.

•

II.3
: The structure of optical vorticity is also uncovered for 2D Dirac systems associated with nontrivial Chern numbers, including Chern insulators, transition metal chalcogenides and topological surface states.

•

II.4
: To compactly represent the
\boldsymbol{k}
-location of optical vortices, we introduce the
director field
, a wave-function-geometric vector field over
\boldsymbol{k}
-space. An optical vortex is shown to be dual to a skyrmion in the director field.

Sec.
III
: Ballistic photovoltaic current in disordered Dirac-Weyl materials

•

III.1
: An argument is presented for the enhancement of the ballistic photovoltaic current by an interplay between momentum-space optical vortices and real-space crystalline defects.

•

III.2
: Focusing on impurities with an electric dipolar component, we formulate impurity-dependent power laws which determine how the frequency exponent [in
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}\propto\omega^{\text{exponent}}
] depends on the dipole orientation, light polarization, and topological class of the Dirac-Weyl material.

•

III.3
: Because dipolar impurities are lower in symmetry than electric monopolar impurities, the monopolar-impurity-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
is nontrivial only in a smaller class of symmetry-reduced Dirac-Weyl materials. To determine the boundary of this ‘smaller class’, we perform a symmetry classification of the low-energy effective Hamiltonians (of Dirac-Weyl materials); knowing their symmetry is tantamount to knowing the symmetry constraints on the photovoltaic tensor, by application of symmetry theorems formulated in the next section.

Sec.
IV
: Symmetry theorems for bulk photovoltaic response tensors

•

IV.1
: Theorems are established which constrain the mechanism-agnostic bulk photovoltaic tensor (inclusive of shift and ballistic contributions) based on the point-group symmetries of general Hamiltonians – effective or not. Emphasis is placed on magnetic point-group symmetries of the form: (time reversal) composed with (spatial transformation), which emerge in the low-energy effective Hamiltonians of the previous subsection.

•

IV.2
: Additional theorems are established which constrain the mechanism-specific bulk photovoltaic tensor associated to defect-mediated skew scattering.

Sec.
V
: Discussion, generalization, outlook

•

A high-level, broad-perspective summary of results is presented. Generalizations are discussed, with an outlook for future theoretical and experimental progress. In particular, the notion of optical vorticity is extended to broader classes of topological materials and even to non-topological materials.

•

Additional vortex-enhanced photovoltaic effects are discussed, including more mechanisms of the bulk photovoltaic effect and also the surface photovoltaic effect.

•

A case is made for bulk photovoltaics as an experimental probe of correlated insulating phases, with an application to rhombohedral
n
-graphene.

•

Theoretical guidance is provided for a tri-pronged experimental program that combines frequency-tuned photoconductivity measurements, defect characterization and defect engineering.

The rest of the paper are appendices targeted to the technically-oriented audience and separately outlined above App.
A
.

Sec.
II.4
and Sec.
IV
are mathematically-oriented and written to be self-contained; a physically-oriented reader can skip these subsections without losing sight of the logical thread running through the paper.

II
Optical vortices in topological semimetals and Dirac-Weyl materials

II.1
Optical vortices and the Chern-vorticity theorem

Because the interband Berry connection
\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}
is a complex number with two real parameters, the zeros of
\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}
have co-dimension two, meaning they generically form lines in the 3D Brillouin zone and points in the 2D Brillouin zone; such lines and points are collectively referred as
vortex centers
. The interband Berry phase
(\arg\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}})
winds around any infinitesimally small loop (
\partial\text{vortex}
) encircling a vortex center; this winding number is referred to as the
vorticity
, and it can be calculated as the circulation of the photonic shift vector:

\displaystyle{\text{Vorticity}\;=\oint_{\partial\text{vortex}}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\tfrac{d\boldsymbol{k}}{2\pi};\;\;\;\;\text{Photonic shift vector}\;\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=&\;-\boldsymbol{\nabla_{k}}\arg\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{bb^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}+\boldsymbol{A}_{bb\boldsymbol{k}}-\boldsymbol{A}_{b^{\prime}b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(4)

The last two terms involving the intraband Berry connection have been added so that the shift vector is gauge invariant.
1
1

1

If a gauge is chosen so that
\boldsymbol{A}_{cc}-\boldsymbol{A}_{vv}
is smoothly defined in the neighborhood of the vortex point, then the line integral of
\boldsymbol{A}_{cc}-\boldsymbol{A}_{vv}
vanishes
\partial vortex
is infinitesimally small, and what remains is the winding number of the interband Berry phase.

Throughout this work, we focus on a subcategory of topological semimetals whose energy bands are either energy-nondegenerate at generic
\boldsymbol{k}
, or doubly-degenerate at generic
\boldsymbol{k}
due to the spin-orbit interaction being negligible.
2
2

2

This subcategory excludes the 3D Dirac semimetals.
For such semimetals, the existence of optical vortices is guaranteed by the Chern-vorticity theorem
83
, which colloquially states: wherever there is a quantized intraband Berry phase, there is also a quantized interband Berry phase. More precisely, for any pair of bands indexed by
b
and
b^{\prime}
, the

\displaystyle{\text{Chern-vorticity theorem:}\;\;\;\;C_{b}-C_{b^{\prime}}=\text{Vor}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{b^{\prime}b}=\sum_{vortex}\oint_{\partial\text{vortex}}\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{b^{\prime}b\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\tfrac{d\boldsymbol{k}}{2\pi},}

(5)

relates the
interband Chern number
(
C_{b}-C_{b^{\prime}}
) to the
interband optical vorticity

(\text{Vor}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{b^{\prime}b})
, which is
the net circulation (of the shift vector) over all
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex points in a closed 2D
\boldsymbol{k}
-manifold, which can be a 2D Brillouin zone, or a two-toroidal/spherical cut of the 3D Brillouin zone. This theorem is
proven in App.
B.1
. In the application to 3D topological semimetals, the closed 2D
\boldsymbol{k}
-manifold is a sphere (enclosing the topological band touching) characterized by a nontrivial interband Chern number, thus guaranteeing optical vorticity by the theorem, as the next Section [Sec.
II.2
] elucidates.

II.2
Optical vorticity of 3D topological semimetals

II.2.1
Isotropic Weyl fermions

An isotropic Weyl fermion has a two-band effective Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle{H^{i,w}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=d_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma};\;\;\;\;d_{\boldsymbol{k}}=v\boldsymbol{k}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\sigma_{3}),}

(6)

which is the dot product of a
Hamiltonian vector
(
\boldsymbol{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
) with the vector
\boldsymbol{\sigma}
of Pauli matrices. Throughout, we set
\hbar=1
.

For a
\boldsymbol{k}
-surface surrounding a Weyl band-touching point, the Chern numbers of the conduction and valence bands satisfy:
C_{c}=-1=-C_{v}
,
implying (via the Chern-vorticity theorem) that
\text{Vor}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv}=2
for any light polarization
\boldsymbol{e}
, complex or not. Because this conclusion holds for an arbitrarily small radius of the
\boldsymbol{k}
-surface, it must be that at least one vortex line connects to (or emerges from) the Weyl point.

Where is this line exactly? The
\boldsymbol{k}
-location of this line must also be a zero of the
optical affinity
defined as

\displaystyle{\text{Optical affinity}:\;\;\;\;\text{Aff}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{e}}\equiv|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}.}

(7)

If
\boldsymbol{e}
is viewed as the light polarization vector, then the optical affinity is essentially the absolute-valued square of the dipole matrix element and is a measure of the optical absorption.
3
3

3

This is generally true for bulk 3D materials, and remains true for 2D materials if
\boldsymbol{e}
lies in the plane of the 2D material. Conversely, if
\boldsymbol{e}
has an out-of-plane component (say,
e_{z}
), then the quantum-mechanical correspondence between the position
z
and
i\nabla_{k_{z}}
breaks down.

For any two-band, effective Hamiltonian of the form:

\displaystyle{H_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\delta E_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathbb{1}_{2};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}},\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}});\;\;\;\;\mathbb{1}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},}

(8)

the affinity is expressible [App.
B.2
] as

\displaystyle{\text{Aff}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{e}}=\sum_{ab}\left(g_{ab,\boldsymbol{k}}{\text{Re}\;}(e_{a}\overline{e_{b}})-\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{ab,v\boldsymbol{k}}{\text{Im}\;}(e_{a}\overline{e_{b}})\right)}

(9)

in terms of the

\text{Quantum metric tensor}\;\;\;\;g_{ab,v\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv\frac{1}{4}\partial_{a}\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\partial_{b}\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\;\;\;\;\text{and Berry curvature tensor}\;\;\;\;\Omega_{ab,v\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot(\partial_{a}\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\times\partial_{b}\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}),

(10)

of the valence band, with
\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\mathbf{k}}\equiv\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}/|\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}|
.
In particular, the optical affinity of the isotropic Weyl fermion [Eq. (
6
)] is obtained by plugging
\boldsymbol{d}=v\boldsymbol{k}
into Eq. (
7
), which we write for real-valued
\boldsymbol{e}
as

\displaystyle{\text{Optical affinity of Weyl fermion}:\;\;\;\;\text{Aff}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{e}\in\mathbb{R}}=\tfrac{1-(\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{e})^{2}}{4k^{2}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}=\tfrac{\boldsymbol{k}}{k};\;\;\;\;k=|\boldsymbol{k}|.}

(11)

This tells us the
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex lies on a
\boldsymbol{k}
-line that is parallel to
\boldsymbol{e}
and intersects the Weyl point.

This line intersects the
\boldsymbol{k}
-surface at two points, and each vortex point contributes
-1
to the net vorticity (
\text{Vor}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv}
) of the
\boldsymbol{k}
-surface, as is consistent with the Chern-vorticity theorem. When calculating the vorticity of a
\boldsymbol{k}
-surface, the orientation of the line integral in Eq. (
4
) is right-handed with the thumb pointing to the outward surface normal direction. However, if one orients the vorticity with a fixed thumb, say, pointing always in the positive z direction, then the vortex orientation flips discontinuously across the Weyl point as one moves along the
k_{z}
axis, as illustrated in Fig.
3
. We may say that the Weyl point is an optical singularity: a connection point between a vortex and antivortex line.
4
4

4

This is compatible with time-reversal symmetry, which constrains the shift vector field as [cf. Eq. (
15
)]

\displaystyle{\text{Time-reversal symmetry}:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}^{\boldsymbol{e}}=\boldsymbol{S}_{cv,-\boldsymbol{k}}^{\overline{\boldsymbol{e}}},}

(12)

implying that a vortex point at
\boldsymbol{k}
has a time-reversed anti-vortex point at
-\boldsymbol{k}
. This holds for Kramers-Weyl fermions in a nonmagnetic, noncentrosymmetric crystal with nontrivial spin-orbit coupling.
57

Figure 3:

(a) Vortex-antivortex line of an isotropic Weyl fermion for the light polarized along the
\mathbf{e}
direction.
The shift vector for the light polarized along the
\mathbf{e}
direction is
\boldsymbol{S}^{\mathbf{e}}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}=\frac{v}{k}\frac{(\mathbf{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{d}})\left(\mathbf{e}\times\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}\right)}{1-(\mathbf{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{d}})^{2}}
.
(b) and (c) Vortex-antivortex line of a three-fold Weyl fermion for the light polarized along the
\mathbf{z}
direction (b) and along a general direction
\mathbf{e}\neq\mathbf{z}
(c).
In all panels, the shift vector vanishes on the equatorial plane.
In both panels, red and blue arrows represent the circulating shift vectors.

Table 1:

The structure of vortex lines and the vorticity for isotropic Weyl,
n
-Weyl, pseudospin-1, and pseudospin-3/2 fermions. A number on the green-colored vortex half-line indicates its contribution to the interband vorticity (
\text{Vor}^{\mathbf{e}}_{bb^{\prime}}
) of a surface enclosing the topological band-touching point (orange colored).
Owing to both effective Hamiltonians in Eq. (
17
) having a symmetry that relates positive-energy eigenstates to negative-energy eigenstates, the interband Chern numbers
C_{b}-C_{b^{\prime}}
and interband vorticities are identical for
(b,b^{\prime})
and
(-b^{\prime},-b)
. To resolve some of the above vortex lines, point-group-symmetric perturbations have been added to both effective Hamiltonians in Eq. (
17
), as detailed in App.
B.4
.

II.2.2
n
-Weyl fermions and higher-order vortices

Optical vorticity is qualitatively different for the
(n{>}1)
-Weyl Hamiltonian:
31

\displaystyle{H^{n,w}=&\;\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{d}=(d_{1},d_{2},d_{3})=\big(f_{n}{\text{Re}\;}(k_{+})^{n},f_{n}{\text{Im}\;}(k_{+})^{n},vk_{z}\big);\;\;\;\;k_{\pm}=k_{x}\pm ik_{y}.}

(13)

(This Hamiltonian reduces to the isotropic Weyl Hamiltonian Eq. (
6
) for
n=1
and
f_{1}=v
, but in this section, we focus on
n>1
.) For an
enclosing
\boldsymbol{k}
-surface
surrounding an
n
-fold Weyl band touching point, the Chern numbers are
C_{c}=-C_{v}=-n
. Such a band touching is stable, meaning unsplittable into
n
1-fold Weyl points, assuming the crossing is between certain representations of an
M
-fold rotational symmetry, with
n
nontrivially dividing
M
.
31

(i) For
\boldsymbol{e}=\boldsymbol{z}
, Fig.
3
(b) illustrates a single vortex-antivortex line along the rotation-symmetric
k_{z}
axis. This line intersects the enclosing
\boldsymbol{k}
-surface at two vortex points, which each point contributing
n
to the vorticity of the surface, in accordance with the
Chern-vorticity theorem [Eq. (
5
)]. The vortex-antivortex line will be described as
higher-order
; more precisely, it is
(n{>}1)
-order, meaning that the shift vector winds by
\pm n
for an infinitesimal loop linking the line [Eq. (
5
)]. It is not possible to split the
n
-order vortex-antivortex line into
n
1-order vortex-antivortex line without violating the same
M
-fold symmetry that prevents the splitting of the
n
-order Weyl. This is because the photonic shift vector field is

\displaystyle{\text{$M$-fold rotation symmetric}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{z}}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}=&\;\{R_{z}^{\scriptscriptstyle{2\pi/M}}\}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{z}}_{cv,R_{z}^{\scriptscriptstyle{2\pi/M}}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;R_{z}^{\theta}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta&0\\
-\sin\theta&\cos\theta&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix},}

(14)

this being a particular case of a more general constraint [proven in App.
B.3
]

\displaystyle{\text{Point-group-symmetric}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}=&\;g^{s,{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{g^{s}\cdot\overline{\boldsymbol{e}}^{g}}_{cv,g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}=i_{g}g^{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{k},}

(15)

arising from the crystalline Hamiltonian having a point-group symmetry
g
that maps spacetime as

\displaystyle{\text{Point-group symmetry}\;\;\;\;g\circ(\boldsymbol{r},t)\rightarrow(g^{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{r},i_{g}t);\;\;\;\;g^{s}\in O(3)\;\text{matrix};\;\;\;\;i_{g}=\pm 1.}

(16)

In Eq. (
15
),
\overline{A}^{g}=\overline{A}
= (complex conjugate of
A
) if
g
inverts time, and otherwise
\overline{A}^{g}=A
.

(ii) For
\boldsymbol{e}\neq\boldsymbol{z}
, there is an
(n-1)
-order vortex-antivortex line (lying along the rotation-symmetric
k_{z}
axis and contributing
2n-2
to the vorticity of the enclosing surface), and an additional 1-order vortex-antivortex line (non-parallel to the
k_{z}
axis and contributing
2
to the vorticity), as illustrated in Fig.
3
(c).

II.2.3
Multifold fermions: Pseudospin-1 and -3/2

The Chern-vorticity theorem [Eq. (
5
)] guarantees optical vorticity in all 3D topological semimetals associated with nontrivial Chern numbers.
5
5

5

3D topological semimetals (with nontrivial Chern numbers) fall into two categories: (a) having nontrivial spin-orbit coupling with spin-split energy bands, and (b) having negligible spin-orbit coupling with spin-degenerate energy bands. In category (a), bands are energy-nondegenerate at generic
\boldsymbol{k}
; in category (b), bands (restricted to one spin sector) are energy-nondegenerate at generic
\boldsymbol{k}
.
These include also higher-angular-momentum generalizations of the 1-Weyl fermion [Eq. (
6
)]: while the 1-Weyl fermion has pseudospin-half, multifold fermions can have pseudospin-1 and pseudospin-3/2.

Pseudospin-1 fermions (sometimes called ‘spin-1 Weyl/chiral fermions’)
19
are materialized in the family RhSi, CoSi, and AlPt, if the spin-orbit coupling is neglected and in a magnetic phase of Mn
3
IrSi
21
; pseudospin-3/2 fermions (sometimes called ‘Rarita-Schwinger(-Weyl) fermions’) are materialized in RhSi, CoSi, and AlPt, in the presence of the spin-orbit coupling.
19
;
44
;
61
. Their respective effective Hamiltonians (at linear order in
\boldsymbol{k}
) are
19
;
18
;
45

\text{Pseudospin-1}\;\;\mathcal{H}^{s1}=v(\hat{\mathbf{L}}\cdot\mathbf{k})\;\;\;\;\text{and Pseudospin-3/2}\;\;\mathcal{H}^{s3/2}=v(\hat{\mathbf{J}}\cdot\mathbf{k}),

(17)

with
\hat{L}_{i=1,2,3}
(resp.
\hat{J}_{i=1,2,3}
) being spin-1 (resp. spin-3/2) matrices detailed in App.
B.4
.

\mathcal{H}^{s1}
describes a touching of three bands which we index by their angular momenta:
1,0
and
-1
. There are then three possible pairings of band indices:
(1,0),(1,-1),(0,-1)
, and the Chern-vorticity theorem [Eq. (
5
)] applies separately to each pairing. Likewise,
\mathcal{H}^{s3/2}
describes a touching of four bands (indexed by
3/2,1/2,-1/2,-3/2
) and the theorem applies separately to each of the six pairings. For each pairing, interband Chern numbers and optical vortex lines are depicted in Tab.
1
, demonstrating full consistency with the theorem.

II.3
Optical vorticity of 2D Dirac systems

A number of prominent 2D Dirac systems can be described by a

\displaystyle{\text{Mass-twisted Dirac Hamiltonian}\;\;\;\;H^{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}_{k_{x},k_{y}}=v(k_{x}\sigma_{1}+k_{y}\sigma_{2})+\big(m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v^{2}+\text{Twist}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\big)\sigma_{3},}

(18)

with
(k_{x},k_{y})
parametrizing the 2D Brillouin zone (of a 2D material) or a reduced Brillouin zone (associated with a surface facet of a 3D crystal). The name ‘mass-twisted’ emphasizes a
\boldsymbol{k}
-dependent Twist term which adds to the Dirac-massive
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v^{2}
; what Twist to add depends on the model/material application, as exemplified in Tab.
2
, which also lists their compatible rotational symmetry:
C_{2z},SO(2)
and
C_{3z}
.

Absent the Twist, the optical affinity vanishes only if
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}=0
, and this vanishing occurs along a straight
\boldsymbol{k}

\boldsymbol{e}
and intersecting
\boldsymbol{k}=\boldsymbol{0}
, as deducible from Eq. (

Suppose our choice is the
SO(2)
-symmetric Twist from Tab.
2
. By ‘
SO(2)
’, we mean a
U(1)
rotational symmetry about the z axis, with
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}
being the matrix-represented generator of rotation.
The resultant Hamiltonian is the small-momentum effective Hamiltonian of a representative Chern insulator,
72
such that
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}=0
marks a topological phase transition associated with a unit change of the Chern number.
6
6

6

In the untwisted case, the integrated Berry flux is
\int d^{2}k\,\Omega_{xy}^{v}/(2\pi)=m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}/4\int_{0}^{\infty}dx/\left(m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}^{2}+x\right)^{3/2}=\text{sgn}{\,m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}}/2

72
.

Fig.
4
(b) shows how the
SO(2)
-symmetric Twist curves the zero-
|A^{y}_{cv}|^{2}
line [in
(k_{x},k_{y},m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v)
-space] into a quadratic parabola, such that the parabola intersects a constant-
(m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v>0)
plane at two isolated
\boldsymbol{k}
-points, which are then the
\boldsymbol{y}
-vortex points for the Hamiltonian with that value of
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}
. This is consistent with a previous observation in Sec.
II.1
, namely that optical vortex centers are generically points in a 2D
\boldsymbol{k}
-space; after all, Twist terms of one form or another generically exist as higher-order-in-momentum, Taylor-series corrections to the massive Dirac Hamiltonian.

Figure 4:

(a)
\boldsymbol{y}
-vortex line (green) of a massive Dirac Hamiltonian [Eq. (

=0
. (b) The same
\boldsymbol{y}

(c) The
\boldsymbol{x}

To appreciate the qualitative features of Fig.
4
(b), we highlight the role of

magnetic reflection symmetry
(
TR_{x}=
composition of time-reversal and x-reflection) in the Hamiltonian and the shift vector field:

\displaystyle{\widehat{TR_{x}}H^{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}_{k_{x}k_{y}}\widehat{TR_{x}}^{{}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}=H^{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}_{k_{x},{-}k_{y}};\;\;\;\;\widehat{TR_{x}}=K=\,\text{complex conjugation};}

(19)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv,k_{x},k_{y}}=&\;\overleftrightarrow{R_{x}}\cdot\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv,k_{x},-k_{y}};\;\;\;\;\overleftrightarrow{R_{x}}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{e}=\vec{x}\;\text{or}\;\vec{y}.}

(20)

The last equation is a particular case of the general symmetry constraint [Eq. (
15
)].
7
7

7

Bear in mind the shift vector is invariant under scaling
\boldsymbol{e}
by a real multiplicative constant:
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{S}^{\text{constant}\cdot\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
. After all,
\boldsymbol{S}
depends on
\boldsymbol{e}
only through
\boldsymbol{\nabla_{k}}\arg\{\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}\}
, which is scale-invariant. Moreover, for the shift vector
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
of 2D electrons in the
xy
-plane, we may as well restrict
\boldsymbol{e}
to lie in the
xy
-plane, because
\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{S}^{\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
if
\boldsymbol{e}
and
\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}
differ only in their z components. This is because of the aforementioned scale invariance and the
k_{z}
-independence of energy eigenstates
|\,u_{b,k_{x},k_{y}}\,\rangle
in a 2D material.
Because a vortex is a circulation of the shift vector [Eq. (
4
)], Eq. (
20
) implies that
TR_{x}
maps a
\boldsymbol{y}
-vortex at generic
k_{y}
to a
\boldsymbol{y}
-vortex at
-k_{y}
, and not to an
\boldsymbol{y}
-anti-vortex at
-k_{y}
; in other words,
TR_{x}
-related
\boldsymbol{y}
-vortex pairs have the same circulation, as illustrated in Fig.
4
(b). Because the
\boldsymbol{y}
-vortex line bends upward (toward
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v=+\infty
), the interband vorticity changes by
\Delta\mathrm{Vor}^{\mathbf{y}}_{cv}=-2
as
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}
is tuned from negative to positive.
This is consistent with the Chern-vorticity theorem [Eq. (
5
)], since the interband Chern number,
C_{c}-C_{v}
, also changes by
-2
across the transition.
8
8

8

For the
k\cdot p
Dirac model, when examining the Chern-vorticity theorem, it is only meaningful to consider the changes in the vorticity and Chern number across the transition, rather than their absolute values, because the continuum description is valid only locally near the Dirac point and the absolute topological invariants generally depend on the ultraviolet regularization.

Due to the
SO(2)
rotational symmetry,
\Delta\text{Vor}^{\vec{y}}_{cv}=-2
across the transition leads us immediately to
\Delta\text{Vor}^{\boldsymbol{e}}_{cv}=-2
for any
\boldsymbol{e}
in the xy-plane; this means that if we embed the parabolic vortex line in a plane (which is the
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v-k_{y}
plane for
\boldsymbol{e}=\vec{y}
), then the plane simply rotates continuously as
\boldsymbol{e}
is rotated, this being a rotation-symmetric constraint on the shift vector field [Eq. (
14
) with
M
being arbitrarily large].

Qualitatively different vortex lines are possible. Fig.
4
(c) shows a cubic-parabolic
\boldsymbol{x}
-vortex line for the mass-twisted Dirac Hamiltonian [Eq. (
18
)] with a
C_{3z}
-symmetric Twist [Tab.
2
], which apply to the surface states of topological insulators.
33

II.4
Interlude: Optical vortices from the director field

II.4.1
Vortex-director relation for 3D
\boldsymbol{k}
-space

To represent where the vortex lines are in
\boldsymbol{k}
-space, we could pick some representative polarization vectors
\{\boldsymbol{e}_{1},\boldsymbol{e}_{2},\ldots,\boldsymbol{e}_{N}\}
and draw
N
corresponding vortex lines, labeling each line by the corresponding
\boldsymbol{e}_{j}
. Such a drawing quickly becomes convoluted as
N\rightarrow\infty
, i.e., as we attempt to capture the where-information for all polarizations.

The challenge is to compactly represent the where-information of all vortices. Our proposed solution is to represent this infinite family of vortex lines by a real vector field
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
, such that the condition
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{e}
is generically satisfied by lines in a 3D Brillouin zone, which hypothetically coincide with the
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex lines. In other words, for any fixed point
\boldsymbol{k}
,
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
is the direction of the real polarization vector
\boldsymbol{e}
for which an
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex hypothetically exists. For this reason, we call
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
the
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
.

For any pair of bands labeled
c
and
v
, we propose that the
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
is the normalized cross product of the real and imaginary components of the interband Berry connection vector field:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}\text{irector field}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=&\;\frac{\boldsymbol{D}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}}{|\boldsymbol{D}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|};\;\;\;\;\text{Director field}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{D}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{R}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\times\boldsymbol{I}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{R}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}={\text{Re}\;}\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{I}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}={\text{Im}\;}\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(21)

The above cross product is invariant
9
9

9

This transformation sends
\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\rightarrow\;\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}e^{-i\phi_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}}
with
\phi_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\phi_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-\phi_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
. Because
\boldsymbol{R}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{R}\cos\phi_{cv}+\boldsymbol{I}\sin\phi_{cv}
and
\boldsymbol{I}\rightarrow-\boldsymbol{R}\sin\phi_{cv}+\boldsymbol{I}\cos\phi_{cv}
, a gauge transformation may be viewed as a (generalized) rotation of (non)orthogonal vectors within the plane spanned by
\boldsymbol{R}
and
\boldsymbol{I}
; such a rotation does not affect the cross product which points out of plane.

under the gauge transformation
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\rightarrow e^{i\phi_{b\boldsymbol{k}}}|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
, with
b=v
or
c
. Crucially,
\boldsymbol{D}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=0
,

10
10

10

Because
\boldsymbol{D}_{cv}
is real while
\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}
is complex,

\displaystyle{|\boldsymbol{D}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}|^{2}=&\;|\boldsymbol{D}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{D}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}|^{2}=|\boldsymbol{R}\times\boldsymbol{I}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}|^{2}+|\boldsymbol{R}\times\boldsymbol{I}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}|^{2}=0.}

(22)

At a generic wavevector, we assume that both
\boldsymbol{D}_{cv}
and
\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}
are nonzero, finite vectors; then
\boldsymbol{D}_{cv}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}=0
if and only if
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv}=0
.

which implies that
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
is the direction along which
\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
vanishes:
11
11

11

Moreover, there can be no other direction along which
\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
vanishes:

\displaystyle{\text{No-misdirection lemma}:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=0\;\;\;\;\text{is incompatible with}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}\neq\pm\boldsymbol{e},}

(23)

Proof by contradiction: supposing the two statements are compatible, then
\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}=\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}=\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{R}=\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{I}=0
, implying that
\boldsymbol{R}//\boldsymbol{I}//\boldsymbol{e}\times\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}
, with ‘
//
’ meaning ‘collinear to’. This implies
\boldsymbol{D}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{0}
, contradicting
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{e}^{\prime}
.

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{D}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}\neq 0\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}=\boldsymbol{e}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}=0\;\;\;\;\text{(optical zero)}.}

(24)

The last equality defines an
optical zero
at
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
. An optical vortex is more than an optical zero: in addition to the defining condition for an optical zero, a vortex requires that the interband Berry phase (arg
\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{A}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
) winds as
\boldsymbol{k}
is varied in a small circle around
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
[Eq. (
4
)]. Optical zeros without interband Berry phase winding exist.
8
If
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}=\boldsymbol{e}
, can one be sure that there is an
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex at
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
and not a winding-less optical zero? Yes, if the
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
has a nonzero

\displaystyle{\text{Skyrmion density}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathscr{F}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=&\;\epsilon_{abc}{\mathscr{D}}^{a}_{cv}\boldsymbol{\nabla_{k}}{\mathscr{D}}^{b}_{cv}\times\boldsymbol{\nabla_{k}}{\mathscr{D}}^{c}_{cv}\bigg|_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}}

(25)

at
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
, as representatively illustrated in Fig.
6
.
For order-one vortices (defined to have interband Berry phase winding equaling
\pm 1
), we propose a duality [proven in App.
B.5.2
] between vortices of the interband Berry phase and skyrmions of the
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
:

\displaystyle{\text{Skyrmion-vortex duality:}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}=\boldsymbol{e}\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\boldsymbol{\mathscr{F}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}\neq&\;0\iff\;\boldsymbol{e}\text{-zero}\;=\;\text{order-1}\;\boldsymbol{e}\text{-vortex at }\boldsymbol{k}_{0}.}

(26)

For illustration, we present the

\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
and skyrmion density
of the isotropic Weyl Hamiltonian [Eq. (
6
)]:

\displaystyle{\text{Isotropic Weyl:}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}=-\boldsymbol{k}/k;\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\mathscr{F}}=-2\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}/k^{2}.}

(27)

Thus for
\boldsymbol{e}=-\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}
, both conditions on the left-hand side of the skyrmion-vortex duality [Eq. (
26
)] apply, implying that there is an
\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}
-vortex at
\boldsymbol{k}
.
As illustrated in Fig 4(a), the SO(3)-symmetric, hedgehog-like
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
implies that vortex lines are straight lines intersecting the Weyl point.

In contrast, the director field of the
(n{>}1)
-Weyl Hamiltonian [Eq. (
13
)] is anisotropic with curved vortex lines intersecting the
n
-Weyl point:

\displaystyle{\text{$n$-Weyl director field}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{D}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=-\frac{n}{4}\frac{{f_{n}^{2}v}\,(k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2})^{n-1}}{|\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}|^{3}}\boldsymbol{q};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{q}=(k_{x},k_{y},nk_{z}),}

(28)

with
|\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}|
determined from Eq. (
13
). Observe that the
n
-Weyl director field vanishes everywhere along the rotation-symmetric
k_{z}
axis, which is the same axis where an
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex line exists for any
\boldsymbol{e}
, as previously ascertained in Sec.
II.2.2
. This rotation-symmetric vortex line may be said to be
directionless
, in the sense that the director field just vanishes. To recapitulate, an
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex is either directed by the
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
(meaning
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}//\boldsymbol{e}
),
or directionless (if
\boldsymbol{D}=\boldsymbol{0}
), and admits no other possibility; in particular, a misdirected vortex does not exist [Eq. (
23
)].

Figure 5:

(a)
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
of the
3
-Weyl fermion, restricted to the
k_{x}-k_{z}
plane. The interpolation of all arrows collinear with a directional vector
\boldsymbol{e}
is
the
\bm{e}
-vortex line; representative examples are provided with
\bm{e}=(\boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{z})/\sqrt{2}
[green arrows] and
\boldsymbol{e}=\bm{z}
[magenta arrows].
For the massive Dirac Hamiltonian with an
SO(2)
-symmetric twist [panel (b)] and
C_{3z}
-symmetric twist [panel (c)], the
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
is indicated by blue values, and a black arrow (at
\boldsymbol{k}
on the red director-zero curve) indicates the direction
\boldsymbol{e}
of an
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex point at
\boldsymbol{k}
.

II.4.2
Vortex-director relation for 2D
\boldsymbol{k}
-space

For the
(k_{x},k_{y})
-space of a 2D material/surface, the director field [Eq. (
21
)] can only be collinear to
\vec{z}
:
\boldsymbol{D}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=D_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\vec{z}
, hence it is reducible to a real-valued

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{D}}\text{irector scalar field}\;\;\;\;{\mathscr{D}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\tfrac{D_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}}{|D_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}|};\;\;\;\;D_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=R^{x}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}I^{y}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-R^{y}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}I^{x}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}},}

(29)

with
R_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{x}
(resp.
I_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}^{y}
) meaning the x-component of the real part (resp. y-component of the imaginary part) of the interband Berry connection [Eq. (
21
)]. Because it takes one real parameter to tune a real scalar number to zero, the zeros of the director field are co-dimension one, meaning they form
\boldsymbol{k}
-lines (in our 2D
\boldsymbol{k}
-space) separating domains with opposite signs for
{\mathscr{D}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}=\pm 1
. We will refer to these lines as
director-zero lines
. Because of the no-misdirection lemma [Eq. (
23
)], an
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex point (with
\boldsymbol{e}
in the xy-plane) can only lie in a director-zero line. In other words, all
\boldsymbol{e}
-vortex points (in 2D materials/surfaces) are directionless, like the higher-order vortex of
n
-fold Weyl fermions in Sec.
II.2.1
.

An illustrative example is the massive Dirac Hamiltonian [Eq. (
18
)] with an
SO(2)
-symmetric Twist [Tab.
2
]. As observed in Sec.
II.3
, the set of vortex points (for all real
\boldsymbol{e}
) fills out a circle of radius
k_{r}=\sqrt{mm_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v}
, which is nothing more than a director-zero circle that separates domains with opposite signs for
{\mathscr{D}}_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}
, as illustrated in Fig.
5
(a). Defining
\boldsymbol{e}(\phi)
as the directional vector of a vortex point at the director-zero coordinate
(k_{x},k_{y})=(k_{r}\cos\phi,k_{r}\sin\phi)
,
\boldsymbol{e}(\phi)
rotates by
2\pi
as
\phi
is advanced by
2\pi
, as illustrated by the black arrows in Fig.
5
(a). If the
SO(2)
-symmetric mass-twisted Dirac Hamiltonian [Eq. (
18
)] is viewed as a quasi-2D Hamiltonian (over 3D
\boldsymbol{k}
-space) with no
k_{z}
dependence:
H_{\text{q2D}}(k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})=H^{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}_{k_{x},k_{y}}
, then a generic perturbation to
H_{\text{q2D}}
will transform the
\boldsymbol{{\mathscr{D}}}
irector field
from having two polarized domains to a skyrmionic vector field [Fig.
6
(a)], in accordance with the skyrmion-vortex duality [Eq. (
26
)].

For a final and brief illustration in Fig.
5
(c), the director-zero lines are three-fold symmetric and open for the massive Dirac Hamiltonian [Eq. (
18
)] with a
C_{3z}
-symmetric Twist [Tab.
2
].

Figure 6:

(a) Skyrmionic director field of quasi-2D Chern insulator obtained by adding a Hamiltonian perturbation, which nontrivially depends on
k_{z}
. Such a perturbation transforms the scalar field of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
domains to a skyrmionic director field, as is consistent with the skyrmion-vortex duality in Eq. (
26
).
(b) and (c) The director field in a neighborhood of
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
determines vorticity: as long as the director field is locally tilting into a hedgehog skyrmion (b) or a helical skyrmion (c) or some linear combination of the two, one can be sure an optical zero is an optical vortex.

III
Ballistic photovoltaic current in disordered Dirac-Weyl materials

III.1
Momentum-space vortices interplay with real-space crystalline disorder

1: Central theme

Momentum-space vortices interplay with real-space crystalline disorder to produce a ballistic photovoltaic current
that is sensitive to the type of Dirac-Weyl fermion, disordering impurity and light polarization.

Let us unpack this statement.

Ballistic photovoltaic current

In a nonmagnetic medium, the bulk photovoltaic current is a sum of two components: the shift current and the ‘ballistic photovoltaic current’ (in short,
ballistic photocurrent
).
66

While the shift current is an effect of the band-off-diagonal elements of the velocity matrix and can be nonzero even if the photoexcited electron distribution is symmetric (
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}=f_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
),
16
the
ballistic photocurrent relies on the distribution being asymmetric (
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\neq f_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
) and is proportional to the group velocity
v_{\boldsymbol{k}}
(the diagonal element of the velocity matrix):
13

\displaystyle{&\text{Ballistic photocurrent:}\;\;\;\;\mathbf{j}_{\text{ballistic}}=-\frac{|e|}{2\mathcal{V}}\sum_{B}\boldsymbol{v}_{B}(f_{B}-f_{-B}).}

(30)

Here
B=(b,\boldsymbol{k})
is the composite index including the band index and wavevector,
-B=(b,-\boldsymbol{k})
is its time-reversed partner, and
(f_{B}-f_{-B})
is proportional to the photo-excitation light intensity. We will largely focus on two-band models in which
b=c
(conduction) or
=v
(valence). A necessary condition for
f_{B}\neq f_{-B}
(in the steady state of a photoexcited medium) is that the
transition rate matrix elements

W_{B\leftarrow B^{\prime}}
[defined through

\displaystyle{\text{(Collisional integral)}_{B}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}\big(W_{B,B^{\prime}}f_{B^{\prime}}-W_{B^{\prime},B}f_{B}\big)=\;\text{Incoming transition rate - Outgoing transition rate}\bigg]}

(31)

are asymmetric:
W_{B,B^{\prime}}\neq W_{-B\leftarrow-B^{\prime}}
. As illustrated in Fig.
1
(a), collisional mechanisms that result in such asymmetry will be called
skew scattering
, and we will largely focus on skew scattering mediated by crystalline disorder/impurities.

Momentum-space vortices interplay with real-space crystalline disorder

2D
n
-Dirac and 3D
n
-Weyl fermions have topologically-enforced optical vortices [Sec.
II.1
].
When a 1-Weyl semimetal is radiated by monochromatic light with electric-field amplitude
|\boldsymbol{E}|
, complex polarization vector
\boldsymbol{e}
and frequency
\omega
, photo-excitation occurs at all
\boldsymbol{k}
on the

\displaystyle{\text{Excitation surface defined by}\;\;\;\;E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}=\hbar\omega.}

(32)

In this context, the excitation surface is spherical and encloses the Weyl point. Fig.
3
depicts how the
\vec{z}
-vortex line (for a 1-Weyl semimetal) emerges from the Weyl point and runs along the
k_{z}
axis, thus intersecting the spherical excitation surface at the north and south poles. Because the optical affinity (
|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}=
squared dipole matrix element ) vanishes along the vortex line,
the
\boldsymbol{k}
-dependent photo-excitation rate (which is shown in Eq. (
33
) to be proportional to the optical affinity)
12
12

12

In Eq. (
33
), we have assumed
f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\approx 1
and therefore the photo-excitation rate does not explicitly depend on the carrier distribution. This is justified if the thermal energy
k_{B}T
is small compared to one-particle energies (measured relative to the chemical potential) for
\boldsymbol{k}
on the excitation surface, and if the laser intensity is not too high. More general expressions can be found in App.
A.5
.

is anisotropic over the excitation surface: minimal at the poles and maximal at the equator, as illustrated in Fig.
1
(b).

\displaystyle{\big(\text{Photoexcitation rate}\big)_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}e^{2}|{\cal E}|^{2}|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega)=-\big(\text{Photoexcitation rate}\big)_{v\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(33)

(The above formula holds for a driving monochromatic light wave whose electric field has the Fourier transform
\boldsymbol{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}=|\boldsymbol{\cal E}|\boldsymbol{e},
with
\boldsymbol{e}
the polarization vector.) The vortex-induced anisotropy is quantified by the deviation of the photo-excitation rate from its iso-energy average:

\displaystyle{\big(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Deviating}\\
\text{photo-excitation rate}\end{subarray}\big)_{B}=\big(\text{Photoexcitation rate}\big)_{B}-\bigg[\big(\text{Photoexcitation rate}\big)_{B}\bigg];{\\
}\text{Iso-energy average}:\;\;\;\;[Fun_{B}]=\frac{\sum_{B^{\prime}}Fun_{B^{\prime}}\delta(E_{B^{\prime}B})}{\sum_{B^{\prime}}\delta(E_{B^{\prime}B})};\;\;\;\;E_{B^{\prime}B}=E_{B^{\prime}}-E_{B},}

(34)

because the excitation surface is also an iso-energy surface for
n
-Dirac/Weyl fermions with the electron-hole symmetry:
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
.
13
13

13

E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
holds for any effective
\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}
Hamiltonian of of the form:
H=\boldsymbol{d}(\boldsymbol{k})\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}
. It follows that the excitation surface is a surface of constant energy:
E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}=\hbar\omega/2=-E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}
.

Consider a hot electron that is photoexcited at a representative point (
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
) on the equator (of the excitation surface) and subsequently collides with an impurity and scatters elastically to
\boldsymbol{k}
at the north pole. The quantum amplitude for the direct transition
(\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
is first order in the impurity potential (
V^{\text{im}}
); in Feynman-addition, there is also an indirect, second-order transition via an intermediate one-electron state:
(\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
, as illustrated in Fig.
1
(c). Quantum interference between the direct and indirect transitions is encoded in the gauge-invariant amplitude for an electron to make a loop:

\displaystyle{V^{\text{im}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=(\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,V^{\text{im}}\,|\,\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}})(\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\,|\,V^{\text{im}}\,|\,\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})(\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,V^{\text{im}}\,|\,\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}})\;\;\;\;(\text{band index suppressed}).}

(35)

Such interference is generically not symmetric in
(\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\rightarrow(-\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
and results in third-order, elastic skew scattering which is proportional to the number (
N_{\text{imp}}
) of impurities:

\displaystyle{(\text{Asymmetric transition rate})_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\tfrac{W_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-W_{-\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{2}=\tfrac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\hbar}N_{\text{imp}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}){\text{Im}\;}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}).}

(36)

To recapitulate, the topologically-guaranteed momentum-space vortex induces a deviating photo-excitation rate [Eq. (
34
)] which activates an impurity-mediated skew scattering [Eq. (
36
)];
the ballistic photocurrent is then a convolution of the deviating photo-excitation rate with the asymmetric transition rate:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{j}_{\text{ballistic}}=-\frac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{B,B^{\prime}}\big(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Group}\\
\text{velocity}\end{subarray}\big)_{B}\big(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Momentum}\\
\text{relaxation time}\end{subarray}\big)^{2}_{B}\big(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Asymmetric}\\
\text{transition rate}\end{subarray}\big)_{B,B^{\prime}}\big(\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Deviating}\\
\text{photo-excitation rate}\end{subarray}\big)_{B^{\prime}},}

(37)

with the
(\text{momentum-relaxation time})^{2}
providing the saturation time for both rates.
14
14

14

The momentum-relaxation time is defined more precisely in Sec.
III.2.1
. Going beyond the momentum-relaxation-time approximation, a more general expression for
\mathbf{j}_{\text{ballistic}}
is available in App.
A.4.8
.

Assuming the two photoexcited bands are electron-hole symmetric [see previous footnote], skew scattering is purely intraband with an equal contribution from both conduction and valence bands; then we may as well drop the band index and multiply the current by a factor of two:

\displaystyle\mathbf{j}_{\text{ballistic}}

\displaystyle=-2_{\text{e-h}}\frac{4\pi^{3}|e|^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}(\tau^{mr}_{E_{ex}})^{2}n_{\text{imp}}|\mathbf{E}|^{2}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{k}}\;\operatorname{Im}(V^{\text{im}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\mathbf{k}}V^{\text{im}}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})\delta(E_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}})\,\delta(E_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime}})\;\delta|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv,\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}|^{2}\,\delta(E_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-E_{ex}).

(38)

Here,
2_{\text{e-h}}=2
, with the subscript reminding us that the factor originates from electron-hole symmetry;
15
15

15

A factor of half emerges from
\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega)=\delta(2E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega)=\delta(E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega)/2
.

\tau^{mr}_{E_{ex}}
is the energy-dependent momentum relaxation time evaluated at the excitation energy
E_{ex}=\hbar\omega/2
;
n_{\text{imp}}\equiv N_{\text{imp}}/\mathcal{V}
is the impurity density; and

\displaystyle{\text{Affinity deviant}\;\;\;\;\delta|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}=&\;|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}-\big[\;|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\;\big]}

(39)

is the deviation of the optical affinity from its iso-energy average.

In subsequent model calculations, it is convenient to present not
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
but the response tensor
\boldsymbol{B}^{\text{de}}
defined by

\displaystyle{&j_{\text{ballistic},a}=\sum_{b,c}\big(\,B^{\text{de},l}_{abc}(\omega)+B^{\text{de},c}_{abc}(\omega)\,\big)e_{b}\overline{e_{c}}|{\cal E}|^{2};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}=|{\cal E}|\boldsymbol{e};{\\
}\text{Linear defect-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor}\;\;\;\;B^{\text{de},l}_{abc}=B^{\text{de},l}_{acb}\in\mathbb{R};{\\
}\text{Circular defect-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor}\;\;\;\;B^{\text{de},c}_{abc}=-B^{\text{de},c}_{acb}\in i\mathbb{R},}

(40)

The linear component is the
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
response to linearly-polarized light; the circular component is the differential
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
response to right-handed vs left-handed circularly-polarized light, and does not contribute in the response to unpolarized light.
66

Sensitivity to the type of Dirac-Weyl fermion, the type of disordering impurity and the light polarization

In conventional noncentric semiconductors, the band gap sets the frequency threshold for photo-excitation, i.e., a bulk photovoltaic current exists only for
\hbar\omega>
band gap. In contrast, for topological semimetals, the vanishing of the band gap is enforced by a combination of symmetry and topology, and the frequency threshold for photo-excitation is set not by the band gap but by the chemical potential (which is measured relative to the Dirac-Weyl point, and is much smaller than semiconducting band gaps). Therefore, it is the low-frequency bulk photovoltaic response that distinguishes topological semimetals from conventional semiconductors. In this work, we focus on how the low-frequency ballistic photocurrent scales with frequency:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}\propto\omega^{\text{exponent}}}

(41)

for impurity-mediated, intra-pocket/valley scattering,
and elucidate how this
frequency exponent
depends on the type of Dirac-Weyl fermion, the type of disordering impurity and the light polarization. A representative result for
n
-Weyl fermions was given in Fig.
2
and will be greatly generalized in Sec.
III.2
.

Let us make several remarks to generalize the above story:

(i) The dictum of

\displaystyle{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Optical vortex in}\\
\text{Dirac-Weyl semimetal}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\rightarrow\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Anisotropic photo-excitation \&}\\
\text{Asymmetric transition rate}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\rightarrow\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Ballistic}\\
\text{photocurrent}\end{subarray}}

(42)

generalizes to 3D
n
-Weyl semimetals [Sec.
III.2.1
] and 2D
n
-Dirac [Sec.
III.2.2
], with the caveat that for massive
n
-Dirac semimetals, the vortex line is proximate to (but does not generically intersect) the excitation surface.

(ii) The self-interference amplitude in Eq. (
35
) is one member of a broader class of

\displaystyle{\text{N-point self-interference amplitudes:}\;\;\;\;V_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}V_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}\boldsymbol{k}_{3}}\ldots V_{\boldsymbol{k}_{N-1}\boldsymbol{k}_{N}}V_{\boldsymbol{k}_{N}\boldsymbol{k}_{1}};\;\;\;\;V_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=(\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,{V}\,|\,\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}),}

(43)

which are invariant under redefining
|\,\psi_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,)
by a
\boldsymbol{k}
-dependent phase. Our expressions for skew scattering [Eq. (
36
)] and the ballistic photocurrent [Eq. (
38
)] apply in the lowest order of perturbation theory, which is third order in the impurity potential; in principle,
(N\geq 4)
-point self-interference amplitudes make higher-order contributions to ballistic photocurrent,
40
which we neglect on the basis of weak impurity potentials and dilute impurity concentrations.
For small-momentum scattering induced by a disorder potential that is smooth on the scale of the crystalline lattice period, the
N
-point amplitude simplifies to a product of the Fourier transform (
\widetilde{V}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
) of the impurity potential
V_{\boldsymbol{r}}
and the N-point Bargmann
16
16

16

The impurity matrix element for small-momentum scattering is elaborated in App.
A.3
. The Bargmann invariant was previously studied by Bargmann in the context of Wigner’s theorem as a way to distinguish unitary transformations from antiunitary ones.
12
invariant

\displaystyle{&\text{N-point self-interference amplitude}\approx\widetilde{V}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}-\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}\ldots\widetilde{V}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{N}-\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\;\times\;{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}\ldots\boldsymbol{k}_{N}};{\\
}\text{N-point Bargmann invariant}:\;\;\;\;{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}\ldots\boldsymbol{k}_{N}}=\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}\rangle\ldots\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{N}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\rangle.}

(44)

The Bargmann invariant (i) is generally non-reducible

17

17

If the quantum distance between every pair of

and intraband Berry curvature
\boldsymbol{\Omega}

\displaystyle\big|\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{3}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{3}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\rangle\big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\frac{1}{2}g_{12}^{ij}\delta k_{12}^{i}\delta k_{12}^{j}-\frac{1}{2}g_{23}^{ij}\delta k_{23}^{i}\delta k_{23}^{j}-\frac{1}{2}g_{31}^{ij}\delta k_{31}^{i}\delta k_{31}^{j}+O(\delta k^{3}),

(45)

\displaystyle\arg\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{3}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{3}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{123}\cdot d\boldsymbol{S}_{123}/(2\pi)+O(\delta k^{3}),

(46)

with repeated indices
i,j
being summed over.
g_{12}^{ij}
is the quantum metric evaluated at the midpoint of
\boldsymbol{k}_{1}
and
\boldsymbol{k}_{2}
;
\boldsymbol{\Omega}_{123}
is the Berry curvature vector evaluated at the midpoint of the triangle with vertices
\boldsymbol{k}_{1,2,3}
;
dS_{123}=\delta\boldsymbol{k}_{12}\times(\delta\boldsymbol{k}_{13}+\delta\boldsymbol{k}_{12})/4
is the oriented triangular area element.
The above approximation of the Bargmann invariant is practically useless for skew scattering within an iso-energy surface enclosing a Dirac-Weyl point, which unavoidably involves large-angle scattering between one-electron states separated by a large quantum distance.

III.2
Impurity-dependent power laws of the ballistic photocurrent

To recapitulate a few salient points in Sec.
III.1
, the low-frequency ballistic photocurrent response offers a unique experimental diagnostic of topological semimetals, and we are interested in how the
frequency exponent
in Eq. (
41
) depends on the type of Dirac-Weyl fermion, the type of disordering impurity and the light polarization. Let us refer to this dependence as an
impurity-dependent power law
for the ballistic photocurrent. A representative result for the

\displaystyle{\text{$n$-Weyl Hamiltonian}\;\;\;\;H^{w,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=f_{n}\big[k_{+}^{n}\sigma_{-}+\;h.c.\;\big]+vk_{z}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}};\;\;\;\;k_{\pm}=k_{x}\pm ik_{y};\;\;\;\;\sigma_{\pm}=\sigma_{x}\pm i\sigma_{y},}

(47)

coupled to dipolar impurities was given in Fig.
2
. This result will be generalized [in Tab.
3
below] to

\displaystyle{\text{$n$-Dirac Hamiltonian}\;\;\;\;H^{d,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=f_{n}\big[k_{+}^{n}\sigma_{-}+\;h.c.\big],}

(48)

and further generalized to wider classes of impurities:

\displaystyle{\text{Monopolar}\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{m}}_{\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\text{Dipolar}\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\text{Hybrid = monopolar + dipolar:}\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{h}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\widetilde{V}^{\text{m}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\widetilde{V}^{\text{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(49)

For simplicity, we assume that all our impurity potentials are
overscreened
by Dirac-Weyl fermions with a nonzero chemical potential, which means that the Thomas-Fermi screening wave vector greatly exceeds the
\boldsymbol{k}
-radius of the excitation surface defined by Eq. (
32
) [see App.
A.3.2
for details]. This implies that
\widetilde{V}^{\text{m}}
is independent of
\boldsymbol{k}
while
\widetilde{V}^{\text{d}}
is proportional to
\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}
, with
\boldsymbol{d}
the oriented dipole moment of the impurity.

In the case of the monopolar impurity,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
vanishes due to the high symmetry of both the Dirac-Weyl Hamiltonians [Eqs. (
47
)-(
48
)] and the impurity potential; in this case, it is necessary to add symmetry-reducing corrections (e.g., warping, tilting) to the effective Hamiltonian so as to activate
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
, as elaborated in Sec.
III.3
. To ascertain which response tensor elements of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
are symmetry-allowed given a certain Dirac-Weyl Hamiltonian and a certain impurity, we offer three symmetry theorems in Sec.
IV
.

Why should the ballistic photocurrent scale with the frequency (
\omega
) of the light source? Impurity-mediated skew scattering [Eq. (
36
)] is elastic and occurs on the excitation surface of constant energy
E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}=\hbar\omega/2
(measured from the Dirac-Weyl node). This implies that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
depends on the dispersion and wave function of carriers (electrons and holes) with energy
E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}
, and only with energy
E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}
, as is evident from the energy-conserving delta functions in Eq. (
38
). It follows that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
has a scaling relation with
E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}
and therefore with
\omega
.

To determine this scaling relation, we begin with the energy-momentum dispersion of the
n
-Dirac/Weyl fermions [Eqs. (
47
)-(
48
)]:

\displaystyle E=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\left(f_{n}\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}}\right)^{2n}+(vk_{z})^{2}},&\text{for }n\text{-Weyl},\\
\sqrt{\left(f_{n}\sqrt{k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}}\right)^{2n}},&\text{for }n\text{-Dirac},\end{cases}

(50)

With respect to energy scaling,
k_{z}
(parallel to rotation axis) has dimensions of
E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}
(hence
\omega
) while
k_{x,y}
(orthogonal to rotation axis) has dimensions of
E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}^{1/n}
(hence
\omega^{1/n}
). Both
k_{z}
and
k_{x,y}
enter the formula in Eq. (
38
) in a number of ways, including: (i) the monopole potential is
\boldsymbol{k}
-independent while the dipolar potential scales as
k_{z}
or
k_{x}
, depending on whether the dipole moment
\boldsymbol{d}
is aligned parallel or orthogonal to the rotation axis, (ii) the optical affinity scales as
\partial_{k_{j}}^{2}\sim(k_{j})^{-2}
for linear light polarized in the
j
direction, and (iii) more ways besides, as detailed in App.
C.1
. Already with (i-ii), we appreciate that this scaling relation depends on the impurity type, the light polarization, and the order
n
of the Dirac-Weyl fermion, which substantiates the main finding of Sec.
III.1
. Reserving the full scaling analysis to App.
C.1
, we merely summarize our findings in Tab.
3
.
