The frequency exponent (fourth column) of the impurity-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor element [third column; cf. Eq. (
40
)] depends on the the type of Dirac-Weyl fermion (first column) and disordering impurity (second column).

For some cases in Tab.
3
, it may be seen that the frequency exponent is negative. This does not imply a divergence of
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
,
18
18

18

Similarly, there is no divergence of the shift current in Ref.
2
, despite their title.

but it suggests
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
can be large as
\omega
approaches the photo-excitation threshold, this threshold being twice the absolute value of the chemical potential measured from the energy of the Weyl point.
19
19

19

For resonant photo-excitation,
\hbar\omega>2|\mu|
by the Pauli exclusion principle. Sending
\mu\rightarrow 0
at fixed
\omega
invalidates our assumption that the impurities are overscreened [App.
A.3.2
],
thus also invalidating the impurity-dependent power laws in Tab.
3
. However, sending
\mu\rightarrow 0
with fixed
\omega/|\mu|
does not necessarily imply that the impurity loses its screening, because screening becomes more effective if the excitation surface is small [App.
A.3.2
].

In comparison, previous works have calculated how the shift current (in Dirac-Weyl systems) scales with
\omega
.
38
;
2
;
59
We may say that
|\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}|\gg|\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{shift}}}|
at low frequency if the frequency exponent (
\text{exponent}_{\text{ballistic}}
) for
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}\propto\omega^{\text{exponent}_{\text{ballistic}}}
is smaller than that for
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{shift}}}\propto\omega^{\text{exponent}_{\text{shift}}}
. Whether
|\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}|\gg|\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{shift}}}|
or
|\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}|\ll|\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{shift}}}|
at low frequency depends on the order
n
of the Dirac-Weyl fermion, the type of impurity,
and the material-dependent corrections to the effective Hamiltonian.
To clarify the last point, some of the negative
\text{exponent}_{\text{shift}}
reported in the literature
2
relies essentially on tilting corrections to the Weyl effective Hamiltonian; such tilting is symmetry-allowed for Weyl semimetals in which the Weyl points lie at generic wavevectors, but symmetry-forbidden if the Weyl points lie at time-reversal-invariant wavevectors (as in Kramers-Weyl semimetals). In contrast, the
\text{exponent}_{\text{ballistic}}
’s in Tab.
3
apply generally to all materials in a given topological class (
n
-Dirac or
n
-Weyl), independent of material-dependent corrections to the effective Hamiltonian.

III.2.1
Vortex-intersection effect in
n
-Weyl semimetals

The central theme of Sec.
III.1
[summarized in Box
III.1
and Eq. (
42
)] is now expounded for
n
-Weyl fermions, generalizing the story in Sec.
III.1
to
n>1
, to more impurity types and to arbitrary light polarizations.

According to the Chern-vorticity theorem, each
n
-Weyl fermion has topologically-enforced optical vortex lines which radiate from the
n
-Weyl node [Sec.
II.1
]. Such lines necessarily intersect the excitation surface, which we assume has the topology of a sphere and encloses the
n
-Weyl node. These intersections are illustrated in Fig.
7
for
n=1,2,3
and for light polarization
\boldsymbol{e}=\boldsymbol{z}
(parallel to the rotation symmetry axis of the
n
-Weyl Hamiltonian [Eq. (
47
)]) and
\boldsymbol{x}
(orthogonal to the rotation axis).

Because the optical affinity (
|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}=
squared dipole matrix element) vanishes along each vortex line, the
\boldsymbol{k}
-dependent photo-excitation rate [Eq. (
33
)] is anisotropic and vanishes at the aforementioned intersection points.
This anisotropy is quantified by the affinity deviant [Eq. (
39
)], which we plot over the excitation surfaces in Fig.
7
.

Figure 7:

Excitation surface in an
n
-Weyl semimetal for three values of
n
, i.e.,
n=1
(left panel),
n=2
(middle panel), and
n=3
(right panel). The normalized by its maximal magnitude affinity deviant is shown in colors; see Eq. (
303
) for its definition. The light polarization vector is directed along the
z
-axis (top row) and the
x
-axis (bottom row).
In all panels, we use
v=2~\mbox{eV}\cdot\mbox{\AA}
,
f_{n}=2~\mbox{eV}\cdot\mbox{\AA}^{n}
, and
E_{\rm ex}=1~\mbox{eV}
.

Consider a hot electron that is photo-excited at a representative point (
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
on the excitation surface) of higher optical affinity, within the conduction band. The electron subsequently collides with an impurity and scatters elastically to
\boldsymbol{k}
of lower affinity, i.e., closer to a vortex intersection point. Quantum interference between the direct (
\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
) and indirect transitions
(\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
is encoded in the three-point Bargmann invariant [defined in Eq. (
44
) and illustrated as green triangles in Fig.
7
]. Such interference is not symmetric in
(\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})\rightarrow(-\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
and results in skew scattering [Eq. (
36
)], which is the origin of the ballistic photocurrent in Eq. (
37
). To recapitulate,

\displaystyle{\text{Vortex-intersection effect:}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Vortex intersects excitation surface}\\
\text{in $n$-Weyl semimetal}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\rightarrow\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Anisotropic photo-excitation \&}\\
\text{Asymmetric transition rate}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\rightarrow\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Ballistic}\\
\text{photocurrent}\end{subarray},}

(51)

which is one realization of the dictum in Eq. (
42
).

To calculate
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
, we combine Eq. (
9
), Eq. (
38
) and Eqs. (
43
)-(
44
) with the explicit forms of the Bargmann invariant

\displaystyle{\mbox{Re}{{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}=&\;\frac{1}{8}\bigg(\;(\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}+\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})^{2}-1\;\bigg);\;\;\;\;\mbox{Im}{{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}=-\frac{1}{4}\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\times\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},}

(52)

[with
\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}/|\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}|
determined (in our context) by the
n
-Weyl Hamiltonian in Eq. (
47
):
H^{w,n}=\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}
and
\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}},\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}})
], and the matrix elements of the overscreened impurity potentials:

\displaystyle{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Overscreened monopolar}\\
\text{impurity matrix element}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;&V^{m,3D}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{V}^{\text{m,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}|u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{cell}};\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{m,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon k_{\text{scr}}^{2}};{\\
}\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Overscreened dipolar}\\
\text{impurity matrix element}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;V^{\text{d,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{V}^{\text{d,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}|u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{cell}};\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{d,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-i\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon k_{\text{scr}}^{2}}\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}.}

(53)

As a result, the linear and circular components of the impurity-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor [Eq. (
40
)] are

\displaystyle B^{\text{de},l}_{abc}+B^{\text{de},c}_{abc}=

\displaystyle-2_{\text{e-h}}\frac{4\pi^{3}|e|^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}(\tau^{mr}_{E_{ex}})^{2}n_{\text{imp}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{\mathbf{k}}\;\left\{\mbox{Re}{{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}\mbox{Im}{\widetilde{V}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\mathbf{k}}\widetilde{V}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}}\widetilde{V}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}+\mbox{Im}{{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}\mbox{Re}{\widetilde{V}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}\mathbf{k}}\widetilde{V}_{\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}}\widetilde{V}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\right\}

(54)

\displaystyle\times\left\{\left(g_{bc,\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\left[g_{bc,\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\right]\right)+i\left(\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{bc,\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\left[\frac{1}{2}\Omega_{bc,\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\right]\right)\right\}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}})\,\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\delta(E_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}-E_{ex}).

Some remarks:

(i) Bearing in mind that the quantum metric (
g_{bc,\boldsymbol{k}}
) and curvature (
\Omega_{bc,\boldsymbol{k}}
) are also expressible in terms of the Hamiltonian
\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
-vector [Eq. (
10
)], Eq. (
54
) explicates how
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
depends on the Hamiltonian eigenstates
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
with manifest gauge invariance.

(ii) Eq. (
54
) also elegantly synthesizes three fundamental geometric characteristics: the
\boldsymbol{k}
-local two-point distance and
\boldsymbol{k}
-local curvature (associated to the
\boldsymbol{k}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}
photo-excitation) and
\boldsymbol{k}
-nonlocal three-point distance (associated to the impurity-meditated large-momentum scattering).

Directly calculating Eq. (
54
), we find that
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
mediated by monopolar impurities (
\widetilde{V}_{im}=\widetilde{V}^{m,3D}
) vanishes. This is ultimately because the electron wave-function geometry of
n
-Weyl fermions lacks a polarity required for skew scattering by maximally-symmetric impurities, as elaborated in Sec.
III.3
.

For both dipolar and hybrid [Eq. (
49
)] impurities (whose dipole moments are uniformly oriented), the reduced symmetry of these impurity allows for a nontrivial current:

\displaystyle B^{\text{de}}_{abc}=

\displaystyle\;-{2_{\text{p-h}}}|e|^{3}\frac{n_{\rm imp}}{\hbar^{2}(2\pi)^{6}}\left(\frac{e\mathscr{d}}{\varepsilon k_{\rm scr}^{2}}\right)^{2}\frac{E_{\rm ex}^{6/n}}{n^{2}v^{2}f_{n}^{6/n}}\times\begin{cases}\big(\tau^{mr,d}_{E_{\rm ex}}\big)^{2}\;\widetilde{B}^{d}_{abc}(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}),\;\;\;\;\text{dipolar};\\
\big(\tau^{mr,h}_{E_{\rm ex}}\big)^{2}\;\widetilde{B}^{h}_{abc}(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}),\;\;\;\;\text{hybrid}.\end{cases}

(55)

We list nontrivial components of the tensor
\widetilde{B}_{abc}(\bm{\mathscr{d}})
in Tabs.

and
5
.

The explicit dependence of the nontrivial coefficients
\widetilde{B}_{abc}
in the ballistic current given in Eq. (
55
) for the hybrid impurity potential
V^{\text{im}}=V^{m,3D}+V^{d,3D}
. We list the dominant terms that can originate from the different combinations of the dipolar and monopolar potentials.

The momentum-relaxation time
\tau^{mr}_{E}
[in Eq. (
55
)]
deserves further clarification. In thermal equilibrium, the electron distribution
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}
(band index suppressed) is the Fermi-Dirac function and is
iso-energy-symmetric
, meaning that the

\displaystyle{\text{Distributional deviant}\;\;\;\;\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}}=f_{\boldsymbol{k}}-[f_{\boldsymbol{k}}]}

(56)

vanishes; cf. Eq. (
34
). In the absence of a drive (e.g., a photoexciting light source), any non-equilibrium, non-iso-energy-symmetric distribution will relax to being iso-energy-symmetric on a time scale that we call the momentum-relaxation time:

\displaystyle{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg(W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\bigg)\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\approx-\tfrac{\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\tau^{mr}_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}};\;\;\;\;W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\tfrac{2\pi}{\hbar}N_{\text{imp}}|V^{\text{im}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|^{2}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}),}

(57)

owing to collisions with impurities which act as a momentum bath.
20
20

20

Phonons can also relax the electron’s momentum, but we assume that impurities are the dominant momentum-relaxers. Eq. (
57
) is an approximation; more precisely, the
distributional deviant

\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{h}\delta f^{h}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is decomposable into generalized harmonics (indexed by
h
) which are eigenfunctions of the impurity-mediated collisional integral, and each harmonic relaxes at a different rate
=-1/\tau^{mr}_{h,E}
:

\displaystyle{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg(W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\bigg)\delta f^{h}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=-\tfrac{\delta f^{h}_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\tau^{mr}_{h,E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}},}

(58)

as detailed in App.
A.4.4
and App.
A.4.5
. In Hamiltonians with spherical symmetry, these harmonics are simply the real spherical harmonics [App.
A.4.7
]. The non-equilibrium distributional deviant is often dominated by one or a few harmonics, in which case
\tau^{mr}_{E}
in Eq. (
57
) is the relaxation time for that one harmonic, or an average over those few harmonics. As a case in point, z-polarized photo-excitation of the
n
-Weyl semimetal predominantly excites the
Y_{l=2,m=0}
spherical harmonic, as illustrated in Fig.
7
. A more general expression for
\mathbf{j}_{\text{ballistic}}
that encodes multiple harmonic-dependent relaxation times [Eq. (
58
)] is available in App.
A.4.8
.

For
n
-Weyl fermions coupled to dipolar impurities,
V^{\text{im}}
in Eq. (
57
) is the dipolar impurity potential [Eq. (
53
)]; for
n
-Weyl fermions coupled to hybrid impurities,
V^{\text{im}}
in Eq. (
57
) is approximated by the monopolar impurity potential [Eq. (
53
)].
21
21

21

For small momentum scattering within a single valley of a Dirac-Weyl fermion, we assume that matrix elements (
V^{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
) of the dipole potential are suppressed by a multiplicative factor
|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}|/
(Brillouin-zone period) compared to matrix elements of the monopolar potential. We therefore approximate
|V^{m}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+V^{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|^{2}\approx|V^{m}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|^{2}
within Eq. (
57
).

III.2.2
Vortex-proximity effect in
n
-Dirac semimetals

The central theme of Sec.
III.1
[summarized in Box
III.1
and Eq. (
42
)] is now expounded for 2D
n
-Dirac fermions with the generalized Hamiltonian

\displaystyle{\text{massive $n$-Dirac Hamiltonian}\;\;\;\;H^{d,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=f_{n}\big[k_{+}^{n}\sigma_{-}+k_{-}^{n}\sigma_{+}\big]+m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v^{2}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{k}=(k_{x},k_{y}),}

(59)

which includes Eq. (
48
) as a special case with Dirac mass
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}=0
. This mass is tunable by different means: (a) magnetic field, strain, and dopants for the surface states of 3D topological insulators
36
;
11
, and (b) dual gating in multilayer graphene.
51
.

Observe that replacing
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v\rightarrow k_{z}
in Eq. (
59
) gives us the
n
-Weyl Hamiltonian in Eq. (
47
). This structural similarity may be exploited: the
n
-Weyl vortex lines in
(k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})
-space [illustrated in Eq. (
7
)] are exactly the
n
-Dirac vortex lines in
(k_{x},k_{y},m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v)
-space.

There is one crucial difference between
n
-Weyl and
n
-Dirac fermions: the excitation surface is two-dimensional in the former case and one-dimensional in the latter case. A 2D excitation surface that encloses the
n
-Weyl point is necessarily intersected by the vortex lines that radiate from the
n
-Weyl point; in contrast, the intersection of a 1D excitation surface (henceforth called an
excitation curve
) with a vortex line is non-generic, i.e., requires finetuning of Hamiltonian parameters. In particular, for light polarization
\boldsymbol{e}=\boldsymbol{x}
, the excitation curve intersects the vortex line (parallel to
k_{x}
) if and only if
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}=0
, as illustrated in Fig.
8
. If
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}\neq 0
, the optical affinity is anisotropic over the excitation curve merely because the excitation curve is
proximate
to the vortex line, as illustrated by the color plot of the affinity deviant in Fig.
8
.

Figure 8:
Illustration of the vortex proximity effect for
1
-Dirac materials with the gap
\Delta=m_{D}v^{2}
. We fixed
m_{D}v=0
,
m_{D}v=0.5\,E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}/v
, and
m_{D}v\approx E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}/v
for the three excitation surfaces. For
(n{>}1)
-Dirac semimetals, there is an additional vortex line parallel to the
m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v
-axis, which does not qualitatively change any of our conclusions.

The same argument for skew scattering by quantum interference (for the electron loop
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
) applies as in Sec.
III.2.1
, with a new constraint that
\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
and
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}
all lie on an iso-energy excitation curve, as illustrated in Fig.
8
. Altogether,

\displaystyle{\text{Vortex-proximity effect:}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Vortex proximate to excitation curve}\\
\text{in $n$-Dirac semimetal}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\rightarrow\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Anisotropic photo-excitation \&}\\
\text{Asymmetric transition rate}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\rightarrow\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Ballistic}\\
\text{photocurrent}\end{subarray},}

(60)

which is a second realization of the dictum in Eq. (
42
).

To calculate
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
, we plug into Eq. (
38
) the explicit forms of the Bargmann invariant

\displaystyle{{\text{Re}\;}{\mathscr{B}}_{\bm{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=&\;\frac{f_{n}^{2}k^{2n}}{4E_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}\left\{1+\frac{4\Delta^{2}}{f_{n}^{2}k^{2n}}+\cos{[n(\phi-\phi^{\prime\prime})]}+\cos{[n(\phi^{\prime\prime}-\phi^{\prime})]}+\cos{[n(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)]}\right\}{\\
}{\text{Im}\;}{\mathscr{B}}_{\bm{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\;\frac{f_{n}^{2}k^{2n}\Delta}{4E_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{3}}\left\{\sin{[n(\phi-\phi^{\prime\prime})]}+\sin{[n(\phi^{\prime\prime}-\phi^{\prime})]}+\sin{[n(\phi^{\prime}-\phi)]}\right\},}

(61)

[obtained from evaluating Eq. (
52
) with the Hamiltonian
\boldsymbol{d}
-vector from Eq. (
59
), in the polar angle representation:
(k_{x},k_{y})={(k\cos\phi,k\sin\phi)}
] and
the matrix elements of the overscreened
impurity potentials:

\displaystyle{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Overscreened monopole}\\
\text{impurity matrix element}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;&V^{m,2D}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{V}^{\text{m,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}|u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{cell}};\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{m,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{2\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon k_{\text{scr}}};{\\
}\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Overscreened dipolar}\\
\text{impurity matrix element}\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;V^{\text{d,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{V}^{\text{d,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}|u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{cell}};\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{d,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-i\frac{1}{2\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon k_{\text{scr}}}\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{k},}

(62)

[obtained from screening of a 3D potential by 2D crystalline electrons; cf. App.
A.3.3
].
The final result for monopolar impurities is:

\displaystyle{V^{\text{im}}=V^{m,2D}:\;\;\;\;\mathbf{j}_{\rm ballistic}=&\;0,}

(63)

because the electron wave function geometry (of
n
-Dirac fermions) lacks a certain complexity that is needed for skew scattering mediated by maximally-symmetric monopole impurities, as elaborated in Sec.
III.3
.

For dipolar and hybrid impurities (both with uniformly-oriented dipole moments),

\displaystyle{B^{\text{de}}_{abc}=&\;-{2_{\text{p-h}}}\frac{e^{6}\mathscr{d}_{\perp}^{3}(\tau^{mr}_{E_{\rm ex}})^{2}}{2^{12}\hbar^{2}\varepsilon^{3}k_{\rm scr}^{3}E_{\rm ex}^{4}}\frac{\left(E_{\rm ex}^{2}-\Delta^{2}\right)^{1+3/n}}{f_{n}^{6/n}}\widetilde{B}_{abc}.}

(64)

We show the nonzero coefficients
\widetilde{B}_{abc}
in Tab.
6
.

For dipolar impurities, the momentum-relaxation time [
\tau^{mr}_{E}
in Eq. (

)] is determined by Eq. (

) with

equal to the dipolar impurity potential [Eq. (

)]; for

in Eq. (

) is determined by Eq. (

) with

approximated by the monopolar impurity potential [Eq. (

)].

.

III.3
Symmetry-reduced Dirac-Weyl fermions and maximally-symmetric impurities

The bulk photovoltaic current mediated by monopole impurities (with the maximal point-group symmetry
O(3)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
) is a case study that asks: if the symmetry-breaking (required for bulk photovoltaics) does not originate from the impurity, whence does it come? In part, it comes from the electronic wave function:

For nontrivial skew scattering mediated by nonpolar, isotropic monopole impurities, there must exist a preferred direction ( i.e.,
polarity) for the Hamiltonian-vector
of the
complex-valued
electronic wave function.

Therein we identify two geometric characteristics (of wave functions over
\boldsymbol{k}
-space) are measures of the required symmetry-breaking for the ballistic photocurrent, i.e.,
23
23

23

It was pointed out in [Fu,Sangyu,japanese] that nontrivial wave-function geometry can activate the low-frequency ballistic photocurrent via perturbing the Fermi surface in metals; here, we identify the precise geometric characteristics, and extend the theory to higher-frequency resonant photo-excitations.
these characteristics are necessary to activate monopole-impurity-mediated skew scattering [Eq. (
36
)]. Namely, we identify:

(i) Complex (as opposed to real) values of the electronic energy eigenstates
\{|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\}
. For Schrödinger-type Hamiltonians,
\{|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\}
are eigenstates of
e^{-i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}H^{0}e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}
; for an
m
-band effective Hamiltonian
H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, each
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
is a vector in
\mathbb{C}^{m}
vs
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, given an
m
-dimensional Bloch-wave basis of Löwdin-orthogonalized atomic orbitals.
3
For any two-band effective Hamiltonian [Eq. (
8
)],

|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
if
H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
involves all three Pauli matrices, and otherwise
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. In bundle-theoretic language,
5
;
28

skew scattering requires the vector bundle to be complex rather than real.

(ii) The second required wave-function geometric characteristic is a nontrivial

\displaystyle{\text{$\vec{H}$-vector polarization}\;\;\;\;[\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}]_{E}=\tfrac{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E)}{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E)};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\tfrac{\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{|\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}|}.}

(65)

This is essentially the iso-energy average of the normalized Hamiltonian vector (in short,
\vec{H}
-vector) in Eq. (
8
), which provides a notion of polarization in the Hilbert space spanned by the two Löwdin-orthogonalized atomic orbitals.

The necessity of both geometric characteristics (i-ii), as summarized colloquially in the above text box, is formally expressed by two theorems in Sec.
IV.2
. One implication of these theorems is that the monopole-impurity-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
vanishes for both
n
-Weyl Hamiltonians [
H^{w,n}
in Eq. (
47
)] and
n
-Dirac Hamiltonians [
H^{d,n}
in Eq. (
48
)]. For both classes of Hamiltonians, the required
\vec{H}
-vector polarization is symmetry-forbidden; for
n
-Dirac Hamiltonians, the required complexity (for
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
) is also symmetry-forbidden – a double blow. These conclusions are summarized in the second and eighth rows of Tab.
7
.

In the remainder of this section, we discuss various ways to activate the monopole-impurity-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
by symmetry reduction of the
D_{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
-symmetric
n
-Weyl Hamiltonian and the
O(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
-symmetric
n
-Dirac Hamiltonian [cf. App.
A.2.2
]. Let us add to
H^{w,n}
(and
H^{d,n}
) various symmetry-reducing corrections:

Dirac mass

\displaystyle H^{dm}=m_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{D}}}v^{2}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}};

(66)

x-tilt

\displaystyle H^{xt}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=v_{xt}k_{x}\mathbb{1};

(67)

z-tilt

\displaystyle H^{zt}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=v_{zt}k_{z}\mathbb{1};

(68)

3-warp

\displaystyle H^{3}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\lambda(k_{+}^{3}+k_{-}^{3})\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}};

(69)

6’-warp

\displaystyle H^{6^{\prime},n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{a,b,c}^{\mathbb{N}}h_{abc}k_{+}^{a}k_{-}^{b}\sigma_{+}+h.c.-H^{d,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;b+2a+3c=n,

(70)

with
h_{abc}\in\mathbb{R}
being real Hamiltonian parameters indexed by natural numbers
a,b,c\in\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,3,\ldots\}
;
in particular,
h_{0n0}=f_{n}
, which is the same ‘

III.3.1
Tilt-activated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
for symmetry-unpinned Weyl fermions

For Kramers-Weyl semimetals (e.g.,
\beta
-Ag
2
Se
80
and (TaSe
4
)
2
I
39
, Ag
3
BO
3
and AgBi(Cr
2
O
7
)
2

22
, and TaGe
2

43
), the Weyl band-touching point is symmetry-pinned to a time-reversal-invariant
\boldsymbol{k}
-point, which forbids tilting corrections like those in Eqs. (
67
)-(
68
). Such symmetry-pinned Weyl fermions can sustain a
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
mediated by dipole/hybrid impurities [Eq. (
55
)] but not by overscreened monopole impurities [Tab.
7
].

In other Weyl semimetals like TaAs
77
;
76
;
49
;
50
and WTe
2

71
;
73
, the Weyl band-touching points are not symmetry-pinned and lie at generic
\boldsymbol{k}
-points. The lower symmetry (of the group of a generic
\boldsymbol{k}
-point) allows for tilting corrections, and can sustain a
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
mediated by overscreened monopole impurities [Eq. (
53
)], as we now demonstrate for a model of z-tilted Weyl fermions:

H^{1,w}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\big|_{f_{1}=v}+H^{zt}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=v\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+v_{zt}k_{z}\sigma_{0}.

(71)

The only nonzero elements of the
\text{photovoltaic}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{im}}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
tensor are derived in Appendix
C.5
to be

\displaystyle B_{xyz}^{\text{de},l}=-B_{yzx}^{\text{de},l}=\frac{1}{2}f_{0}(\omega,v)\,f^{l}\big(\tfrac{v_{zt}}{v}\big);\;\;\;\;B_{xzx}^{\text{ed},c}=B_{yzy}^{\text{de},c}=\frac{i}{2}f_{0}(\omega,v)\,f^{c}\big(\tfrac{v_{zt}}{v}\big);

(72)

\displaystyle f_{0}(\omega,v)=\frac{|e|^{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\tau_{\text{eff}}^{2}\left(\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)^{3}\frac{n_{\text{imp}}}{k_{scr}^{6}}\left(\frac{\omega}{2v}\right)^{2}\tfrac{1}{v^{2}};

\displaystyle f^{l}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{105}\frac{(\text{arctanh}(\alpha)-\alpha/(1-\alpha^{2}))(7+3\alpha^{2})}{\alpha(1-\alpha^{2})^{2}}=-\frac{2}{45}\alpha^{2}+O(\alpha^{4});

\displaystyle f^{c}(\alpha)=\frac{35+3\alpha^{2}(14+\alpha^{2})}{105}\frac{(\alpha/(1-\alpha^{2})-\text{arctanh}(\alpha))}{4\alpha^{2}(1-\alpha^{2})^{2}}=\frac{1}{18}\alpha+O(\alpha^{3}),

as is consistent with the symmetry analysis in Table
7
. The dimensionless tilt-dependent functions vanish as the tilt velocity
v_{zt}
goes to zero, with
f^{l}\propto v_{zt}^{2}
and
f^{c}\propto v_{zt}
in the lowest-order Taylor truncation; apparently, the circular
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
is more sensitive to tilting than the linear component.

III.3.2
Warp-activated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
in
n
-graphene

6’-warped
n
-Dirac fermions [Eq. (
48
), Eq. (
70
)] are materialized by
n
-graphene,
52
;
41
;
82
;
46
;
35
as is evident from two observations:

(i)
n
-graphene is described by four flavors of
n
-Dirac fermions (distinguished by a valley index
\xi=\pm
and a spin index
s=\pm
) subject to the (valley+spin)-dependent Hamiltonian:

\displaystyle{H^{\xi,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{a,b,c}^{\mathbb{N}}h_{abc}(k^{\xi}_{+})^{a}(k^{\xi}_{-})^{b}\sigma_{+}+h.c.+\Delta_{\xi,s}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}};\;\;\;\;k^{\xi}_{\pm}=\xi k_{x}\pm ik_{y};\;\;\;\;b+2a+3c=n.}

(73)

This is essentially
24
24

24

We emphasize ‘essentially’, because Eq. (
73
) corrects an error in Eq. (14) of Ref.
41
. Our Eq. (
73
) is consistent with spatial inversion being represented as
\widehat{P}=\tau_{1}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}s_{0}
, with
s_{0}
being the identity matrix in the spin subspace,
\tau_{j}
being a Pauli matrix in the valley subspace; the eigenvalues of
\tau_{3}
correspond to the valley index
\xi
. The proportionality of
\widehat{P}
to
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}
is because spatial inversion interchanges the orbitals
\psi_{A,1}
and
\psi_{B,n}
with a trivial phase factor.
41
Each orbital here is labelled by a sublattice index (to the left) and a layer index (to the right); the sublattice index
A,B
corresponds to the
\pm 1
eigenvalues of
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}
.

the Koshino-McCann effective Hamiltonian for a single valley of
n
-graphene,
41
with the inter/intralayer coupling parameters (
\gamma_{j}
) absorbed into the real coefficients
h_{abc}
. It should be understood that
H^{+,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is the small-
|\boldsymbol{k}|
effective Hamiltonian centered at
C_{3z}
-invariant wavevector
K
, while
H^{-,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
is the small-
|\boldsymbol{k}|
effective Hamiltonian centered at the other inequivalent
C_{3z}
-invariant wavevector
K^{\prime}
. The term
\Delta_{\xi,s}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}
, if nonzero, opens a band gap; in the absence of this [potentially (valley+spin)-dependent] gapping term,
H^{+,s}
reduces to
H^{d,n}+H^{6^{\prime},n}
[Eq. (
48
) and Eq. (
70
)];

(ii) The layer group of rhombohedral
n
-graphene is
p\bar{3}m1\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
,
54
with point group
D_{3d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
. This point group contains elements:
C_{2}^{\prime}
(two-fold rotation with an in-plane axis),
P
(spatial inversion),
T
and
S_{6}=M_{x}C_{6z}
(six-fold roto-rotation); the aforementioned four elements map
K
to
K^{\prime}
, i.e., they exchange valleys. Therefore, only pairwise compositions (like
TP
) belong to the

\displaystyle{\text{Little group of K}\;={\bar{3}^{\prime}m}=C_{3v}\cup\{TP,3TC_{2}^{\prime},2TS_{6}\},}

(74)

which are the set of point group symmetries that map
K
to
K
.
If we ignore the
z
coordinate in these three-dimensional spatial transformations, the little group of K projects to

\displaystyle{\bar{3}^{\prime}m\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{projects}\\
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;6^{\prime}mm^{\prime}=C_{3v}\cup\{TC_{2z},3TM_{x},2TC_{6z}\}.}

(75)

Thus the two groups are isomorphic, with
TP
corresponding to
TC_{2z}
and
TC_{2}^{\prime}
corresponding to
TM_{x}
, etc. The distinction between the two groups is irrelevant to symmetry constraints of photovoltaic tensors for in-plane currents and electric fields. This is why
6^{\prime}mm^{\prime}
-symmetric
H^{d,n}+H^{6^{\prime},n}
[cf. Tab.
7
] can also be viewed as being
{\bar{3}^{\prime}m}
-symmetric, which is the little-group symmetry of
n
-graphene.

In estimating
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
for
n
-graphene, we assume for longer-wavelength disorder that intra-valley electron-defect scattering dominates over inter-valley scattering, and for non-magnetic defects that electron-defect scattering is spin-preserving, such that the ballistic photovoltaic response tensor decomposes into a sum of intra-valley and intra-spin contributions:
B^{\text{de}}\approx\sum_{\xi,s}B^{\text{de}}_{\xi,s}
. Each contribution [derived in Appendix
C.6
] reads as

\displaystyle B^{\text{de}}_{abc,\xi,s}=2_{\rm p-h}|e|^{3}\frac{(2\pi)^{3}}{\hbar^{2}}n_{\rm imp}(\widetilde{V}^{m}_{\mathbf{0}})^{3}\frac{(\tau^{mr}_{E_{\rm ex}})^{2}}{2}\widetilde{B}_{abc,\xi,s}^{(n)}.

(76)

\widetilde{B}_{abc,\xi}^{(n)}
for a few representative values of

(number of graphene layers) and

in Tab.

.

By inspection,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}

is proportional to
(h_{abc})^{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{positive integer}}}\Delta_{\xi,s}
, which reflects that combining both the gap
\Delta_{\xi,s}
and the 6’-warp is crucial to activate the intravalley
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}^{\xi}
, and that neither gap nor warp alone is sufficiently activating, in accordance with the symmetry analysis in Tab.
7
. In the presence of an out-of-plane displacement field, the gap
\Delta_{\xi,s}
is valley- and spin-independent, in which case
\sum_{\xi,s}\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}^{\xi,s}
adds up rather than canceling out, in accordance with the displacement field breaking centrosymmetry. More interesting possibilities for the gap
\Delta_{\xi,s}
are discussed in Sec.

.

IV
Symmetry theorems for bulk photovoltaic response tensors

This is a self-contained, read-alone or read-without interlude on the symmetry constraints of the photovoltaic response tensor.

Suppose a homogeneous medium is subject to a dynamic electric field [
{\cal E}(\boldsymbol{r}t)={\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}e^{i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-i\omega t}+c.c.
] associated to a light wave.
The bulk photovoltaic response tensor
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is defined by

\displaystyle{j_{a}=\sum_{b,c}\big(\Sigma^{l}_{abc}+\Sigma^{c}_{abc}\big){\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,b}\overline{{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,c}};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{abc}=\Sigma^{l}_{acb};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{c}_{abc}=-\Sigma^{c}_{acb},}

(77)

with
\boldsymbol{j}
being the static homogeneous current (‘direct current’) density. The linear and circular components of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
are respectively defined to be symmetric and antisymmetric under interchange of the last two spatial indices.
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
implicitly depends on
\omega
but does not depend at all on
\boldsymbol{q}
, hence ruling out photon drag effects
26
.

We are interested in how
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is constrained by the Hamiltonian being symmetric with respect to a point-group transformation
g
. We distinguish between spatial point-group transformations (
g=g^{s}
) and magnetic point-group transformations (
g=Tg^{s}=g^{s}T
, meaning the composition of time-reversal with a spatial point-group transformation, which could be the trivial spatial transformation). Many of the point-group transformations we will encounter are
order-two
, meaning that
g^{2}
has a trivial action on spacetime; we list them in Tab.
9
. For any
g
which acts on spacetime, order-two or not, we define
g^{s}_{ab}
as an
O(3)
matrix for the reduced action of
g
on 3D space alone, as exemplified by the last column in Tab.
9
.

Table 9:
A dictionary of commonly-used, order-two point-group transformations. In the last column, ‘diag
(i,j,k)
’ means a diagonal
O(3)
matrix with diagonal elements equal to
(i,j,k)
.

If a Hamiltonian
H
acts in the Hilbert space of a certain set of wave functions, we say the Hamiltonian is
g
-symmetric if
\hat{g}
(the representation of
g
in said Hilbert space) commutes with the Hamiltonian, or equivalently

\displaystyle{\text{$g$-symmetric Hamiltonian:}\;\;\;\;H^{g}=H;\;\;\;\;H^{g}\equiv\hat{g}H\hat{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}.}

(78)

\hat{g}
is a unitary operator if
g=g_{s}
, and an antiunitary operator if
g=Tg_{s}
. In the photovoltaic context,
H=H^{0}+V
is the sum of a zeroth-order Hamiltonian
H^{0}
(typically a mean-field Hamiltonian of electron quasiparticles in a perfect crystalline medium) and a perturbation
V
that encodes the coupling to a photo-exciting light wave (in the dipole approximation) and the coupling to an environment (e.g., impurities and a thermal background of photons and phonons, which play the role of a source/sink of energy or momentum or both).

In the specific application to the impurity-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
,
H^{0}
reduces (in
\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}
theory) to the effective Hamiltonians for
n
-Weyl fermions [
H^{w,n}
in Eq. (
47
)] and
n
-Dirac fermions [
H^{d,n}
in Eq. (
48
)], which respectively have the point groups
D_{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
and

O(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
respectively, with the specific symmetry representations detailed in App.
A.2.2
.
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
group generated by time reversal,
O(2)
is the orthogonal group in two spatial dimensions, and
D_{\infty}
is the dihedral group
D_{2}
(generated by two-fold rotations
C_{2x}
and
C_{2y}
about the x- and y-axes respectively) with an additional continuous rotation symmetry about the z axis.

IV.1
General symmetry constraints

As derived in App.
D
, a symmetric Hamiltonian has the following implications for the photovoltaic tensor:

\displaystyle{\text{Spatial-symmetric photovoltaic theorem}:\;\;\;\;H^{g^{s}}=&\;H\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{abc}=g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{def};{\\
}\text{Magnet-symmetric photovoltaic theorem}:\;\;\;\;H^{Tg^{s}}=\;H\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{abc}=\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{def}.}

(79)

The second theorem
25
25

25

A precursor of this theorem can be found in Ref.
2
, which is the source of our inspiration. Their formulation lacked a clarity and generality that our theorem and derivation aim to provide.

applies: (i) whether or not
H^{T}=H
, (ii) whether or not
H^{g^{s}}=H
, and (iii) to components of the bulk photovoltaic tensor with a well-defined
26
26

26

Any response tensor
\Sigma
can separated into components
\Sigma_{\pm}
with compatibility index
\pm 1
:

\displaystyle{\Sigma(H)=\Sigma_{+}+\Sigma_{-};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{\pm}=\tfrac{\Sigma(H)\pm\Sigma(H^{T})}{2}.}

(80)

For most of the manuscript, we do not explicitly manifest the subscript
\pm
.

compatibility index under time reversal
:

\displaystyle{\Sigma\big(H^{T}\big)=\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma\big(H\big);\;\;\;\;\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}=\pm 1.}

(81)

The meaning of
\Sigma(H)
is that we view
\Sigma
(the response tensor) as the output of a calculational algorithm (also denoted
\Sigma
) which inputs the Hamiltonian. For instance, the calculational algorithm for Eq. (
38
) manifestly inputs the eigen-energies and eigenstates of
H^{0}
, as well as inputs the impurity potential and light polarization vector of
V
.
\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}
, the compatibility index of
\Sigma
under time reversal, is defined in Eq. (
81
) as the sign factor difference between
\Sigma(H)
and
\Sigma(H^{T})
. The advantage of knowing this index is that if
\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}=+1
,
\Sigma
can be nonzero if
H^{T}=H
, while if
\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}=-1
,
\Sigma
must be zero if
H^{T}=H
; we say that
\Sigma
is compatible with a time-reversal-symmetric Hamiltonian in the former case, and incompatible in the latter case. Postponing an explicit calculation of the compatibility indices to App.
D
, we merely list the compatibility indices for various components of the bulk photovoltaic tensor in Tab.
10
.

As our first application of the theorems in Eqs. (

)-(

), the symmetry constraints arising from the order-two symmetries in Tab.

are listed in the bottom sub-table in Tab.

. These constraints are of three kinds: (a) trivial, (b) complete, meaning all tensor elements (in a specific class of tensors) vanish, as indicated by ‘0’ in Tab.
9
, or (c) a parity constraint on
N_{j}
, which is defined as the number of ‘
abc

\Sigma_{abc}
) that equals
j
. For illustration, let us consider how the magnetic reflection
TM_{x}
constrains the circular component of the shift current; we look up the
T
-compatibility index from the top sub-table of Tab.
10
, namely
-1
, and plug this index into our second theorem in Eq. (
79
):

\displaystyle{H^{TM_{x}}=&\;H\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\text{shift},c}_{abc}=-(M_{x})_{ad}(M_{x})_{be}(M_{x})_{cf}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\text{shift},c}_{def};\;\;\;\;(M_{x})=\text{diag}(-1,1,1).}

(82)

It follows that any nonzero
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\text{shift},c}_{abc}
must have
an even number of
x
indices, e.g.,
(abc)=(xxy)
and
(xyx)
and
(yzz)
are allowed but
(xxx)
is not.

In the specific application of Dirac-Weyl fermions coupled to impurities, the symmetry of
H
is the intersection of the symmetry of
H^{0}
and the symmetry of the impurity potential
V^{\text{im}}
. This means that we only apply the theorems in Eqs. (
79
)-(
79
) if
g
is simultaneously a symmetry of
H^{0}=H^{0,g}
and
V^{\text{im}}=V^{\text{im,g}}
. The set of all
g
forms a group specified in the third column of Tab.
11
. For instance, the monopolar impurity potential
V^{m}
has the highest symmetry:
O(d)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
in
d
spatial dimensions, so the symmetry of
H
is simply the symmetry of
H^{0}
, which is sufficiently large to extinguish all ballistic photocurrents for
H^{0}=H^{d,n}
, and sufficiently large to leave only one independent tensor element (
\Sigma^{l}_{xyz}
) for
H^{0}=H^{w,n}
; this is the statement of the second and fifth rows in Tab.
11
. For completeness, we also provide [in Tab.
12
] the point groups and symmetry-allowed tensor elements for symmetry-reduced
n
-Dirac/Weyl fermions [Eqs. (
66
)-(
70
)]; the majority of these point groups are magnetic point groups which do not contain time reversal
T
as an element, but contain various magnetic point-group elements (
Tg^{s}
), thereby necessitating the use of the second theorem in Eq. (
79
). Both tables [Tab.
11
and Tab.
12
] are more broadly applicable outside the context of Dirac-Weyl fermions; that is to say, one can erase the leftmost columns and view what remains as
general symmetry constraints
on the photovoltaic tensor given a symmetry group of
H
.

IV.2
Hamiltonian-specific symmetry constraints

In contrast with
general symmetry constraints
that apply to all photovoltaic tensors independent of specific details of the Hamiltonian (beyond the assumption that the Hamiltonian is symmetric:
H^{g}=H^{e}
), we introduce here the notion of
H
-specific symmetry constraints
(short for Hamiltonian-specific), which apply to specific components of the photovoltaic tensor and depend on specific structural details of the Hamiltonian. Here are two examples:

TC_{2z}
theorem:
For 2D systems with
TC_{2z}
-symmetric Hamiltonians, the ballistic photocurrent (mediated by centrosymmetric impurities) vanishes.

\vec{H}
-polarity theorem:
For any two-band
H^{0}
with a point group
G
, if there exists no invariant vector in the Hamiltonian-vector representation of
G
, then the ballistic photocurrent (mediated by overscreened monopole impurities) vanishes.

What’s ‘specific’ here is that (i) both theorems apply to the impurity-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
, which is only one component of the photovoltaic response; (ii) both theorems assume a coupling to a specific class of impurities, namely centrosymmetric impurities (
V^{\text{im}}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=V^{\text{im}}_{-\boldsymbol{r}}
) in the first theorem, and overscreened monopole impurities [Eq. (
53
) and Eq. (
62
)] in the second; (iii) the second theorem applies to two-band Hamiltonians [Eq. (
8
)] which are essentially dot products of a Hamiltonian vector
\boldsymbol{d}
with the vector of Pauli matrices.

The formal notion of an ‘invariant vector in the Hamiltonian-vector representation’ is described in
App.
A.2.3
; the detailed derivation of the theorems are relegated to App.
D.7.1
. Here, we will informally convey the essence of the derivation: for long-wavelength skew scattering mediated by either class of impurities, the
(\text{Asymmetric transition rate})_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
[Eq. (
36
)] is proportional to

\displaystyle{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}{\text{Im}\;}\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\rangle\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}-E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}})\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{Bargmannrealimag}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\frac{1}{4}\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\times\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\cdot[{\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}}]_{E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}},}

(83)

which is proportional to the
\vec{H}
-vector polarization [Eq. (
65
)] at the excitation energy
E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}
(the energy of the excitation surface).
Being proportional to the asymmetric transition rate [Eq. (
38
)], the ballistic photocurrent evidently vanishes if either the wave function is real (
|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
) or if the
\vec{H}
-vector polarization is trivial (
[{\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}}]_{E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}}=0
).

Certain symmetries render the wave function to be real for all
\boldsymbol{k}
in the Brillouin zone. For illustration, the massless
n
-Dirac Hamiltonian in Eq. (
48
) is symmetric under
C_{2z}T
(the composition of two-fold rotation and time-reversal):

\displaystyle{[H^{d,n}_{k_{x},k_{y}},\widehat{C_{2z}T}]=0;\;\;\;\;\widehat{C_{2z}T}=(\text{unimportant phase factor})\,\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}K;\;\;\;\;K\;\text{implements complex conjugation},}

(84)

which ensures that
H^{0}
involves only two of three Pauli matrices; a unitary transformation can always be chosen to make these two Pauli matrices real. In contrast, the
n
-Weyl Hamiltonian involves all three Pauli matrices, which reflects the absence of

PT
symmetry (composition of parity and time-reversal).
27
27

27

In 3D systems with negligible spin-orbit coupling,
\widehat{PT}
squares to the identity operator in its action on
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
, which ensures
|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
can be made real.
These observations underlie the above
TC_{2z}
theorem.

Any symmetry
g
of
H^{0}
[Eq. (
8
)] has three representations encapsulated in:

\displaystyle{H^{0}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}}=\hat{g}H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}=(\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\delta E_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathbb{1}_{2},}

(85)

namely (i)
g\circ
is an orthogonal matrix acting in
\boldsymbol{k}
-space, (ii)
\hat{g}
is a (possibly antiunitary) matrix acting on complex wave functions
\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, and (iii)
\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}
is an orthogonal, three-by-three matrix acting on the Hamiltonian
\boldsymbol{d}
-vector. As a case in point, for
g=T
(time-reversal),
g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}=-\boldsymbol{k}
,
\hat{T}=i\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}K
acts on (pseudo)spin-half wave functions, and
\overleftrightarrow{T}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
because time-reversal flips all (pseudo)spin-half components. More generally, for any symmetry
g
of
H^{0}
(time-reversing or not), Eq. (
85
) implies that the
\vec{H}
-vector polarization [Eq. (
65
)] transforms as a scalar under
g
:
[\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}]_{E}=\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}\cdot[\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}]_{E}
, meaning it is invariant under the action of
g
in the Hamiltonian-vector representation. In the case of
\hat{g}=\hat{T}=i\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}K
, this evidently implies there can be no polarization:
[\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}]_{E}=-[\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}]_{E}=0
, which extinguishes the ballistic photocurrent. This observation, when generalized to all magnetic point groups and taken to its logical conclusion, leads to the above
\vec{H}
-polarity theorem.

As applications of the two aforementioned theorems, Tab.
7
shows which of our case studies have
TC_{2z}
symmetry and which have an invariant vector in the Hamiltonian-vector representation; a ‘0’ in the column headed by
[\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}]
means that no invariant vector exists; ‘
\shortparallel 3
’ means that it exists and lies parallel to the 3 direction. Accordingly,
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}=0
in Tab.
7
wherever the assumptions of the two theorems hold true.

Our final example of a
\vec{H}
-specific symmetry constraint, which we will call the ‘impurity-parity theorem’, again applies to the impurity-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor:
B^{\text{de}}
. Unlike previous examples wherein
H^{g}=H
, the impurity-parity theorem relies on
(H=H^{0}+V)
being
g
-
a
symmetric in a particular way, namely, with
H^{0}
being
g
-symmetric but the impurity potential
V^{\text{im}}
being
g
-
anti
symmetric:
(V^{\text{im}})^{g}=\hat{g}V^{\text{im}}\hat{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}=-V^{\text{im}}
. (We may say the impurity has odd
g
-parity.) Under these conditions, a third theorem states, for
g=g^{s}
being a spatial symmetry,

\displaystyle{\text{Impurity-parity theorem}:\;\;\;\;H^{0,g^{s}}=H^{0},\;\;\;\;V^{\text{im},g^{s}}=-V^{\text{im}}:\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{\text{im}}_{\text{ballistic},abc}=-g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\Sigma^{\text{im}}_{\text{ballistic},def}.}

(86)

As detailed in App.
D.7.2
,
Eq. (
86
) derives from the impurity-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
[Eq. (
38
)] being third order in
V^{\text{im}}
(at lowest-order perturbation theory), and hence is odd under
V^{\text{im}}\rightarrow-V^{\text{im}}
. To describe one application, the impurity potential (of a dipole moment oriented in the
x
direction) is antisymmetric under a two-fold rotation (
C_{2z}
) about the
z
-axis;
g^{s}=C_{2z}
is a symmetry of both
n
-Weyl and
n
-Dirac Hamiltonians, either of which can be
H^{0}
in Eq. (
86
); the impurity-parity theorem then imposes
H
-specific constraints (in addition to the general constraints in Sec.
IV.1
) which are summarized in Tab.
11
; cf. App.
D.8
-
D.9
. This concludes the interlude on symmetry.

-Dirac (
H^{d,n}
) fermions, coupled to monopolar (
V^{m}
) or j-oriented dipolar (
V^{d\shortparallel j}
) impurities or their combination.
B^{\text{de}}_{\{abc\}}
means all permutations of
(abc)
are symmetry-allowed; ‘0’ means all tensor elements vanish.

V
Discussion, generalization, outlook

Herein we present a partial summary of our results from a broader perspective, discuss their generalizations, and present an outlook for future theoretical and experimental progress.

V.1
Optical vortices in materials both topological and non-topological

Topological band touchings are known to be sources/sinks of the intraband Berry curvature;
68
;
34
;
70
here we uncover their dual property as sources/sinks of vorticity in the interband Berry phase, meaning that the topological band touching
\boldsymbol{k}
-point is a wave-function-singular point from which optical vortex lines radiate, see Fig.
1
(a). This follows from a generalized Chern-vorticity theorem [Eq. (
5
)] which guarantees optical vorticity in every topological material (
\equiv
insulator + metal) associated with nontrivial Chern numbers, including
n
-Weyl semimetals, higher-angular-momentum multifold fermions, and Chern insulators.

This is not to say that optical vortices are absent in non-topological materials; on the contrary, vortices likely exist
generically
in materials – topological or not. What the theorem [Eq. (
5
)] implies (for materials with trivial Chern numbers) is that the net vorticity of generically-occurring vortex points vanish. Moreover, it is not possible to infer the
\boldsymbol{k}
-locations of generically-occurring vortex points by topological arguments.

It is also worth emphasizing having a nontrivial Chern number is not the defining characteristic of all topological materials. After all, the Chern number is a quantized Abelian intraband Berry phase associated to energy-nondegenerate bands, and some topological materials are characterized by a quantized non-Abelian intraband Berry phase associated to bands with symmetry-enforced energy degeneracy. Examples of the latter category include 3D Dirac semimetals
78
and 2D and 3D
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
topological insulators.
20
It may be interesting to explore the implications of a postulated, non-Abelian generalization of the Chern-vortex theorem to the bulk photovoltaic effect in broader classes of topological materials.

V.2
Vortex-enhanced photovoltaic current in both bulk and surface

Optical vorticity makes the photoexcitation rate anisotropic over the excitation surface, see Fig.
1
(c). For
n
-Weyl semimetals, such anisotropy follows from a topologically-guaranteed intersection of the excitation surface by vortex lines [Eq. (
51
)]; for
n
-Dirac materials, such anisotropy follows from a topologically-guaranteed proximity of the excitation curve to vortex points [Eq. (
60
)]. An anisotropic photoexcitation rate can also result from an accidental intersection/proximity to generically-occurring vortex lines/points in non-topological materials; whether such accidents happen or not depends on material details, and can only be determined on a case-by-case basis, in contrast with the general certitude
gained with topological materials.

The aforementioned
vortex-induced photoexcitation anisotrop
y activates several photovoltaic effects:

(i) The first effect is that when electrons collide with phonons/impurities and scatter from
\boldsymbol{k}
-regions of higher photoexcitation rate to
\boldsymbol{k}
-regions of lower photoexcitation rate, there is an accompanying shift associated to the electron scattering transitions,
16
resulting in a shift photovoltaic current.

(ii) Secondly, the same scattering (from high- to low-photoexcitation regions) has a skew component that makes the electron distribution asymmetric:
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}\neq f_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
. Thus vortices enhance the ballistic photovoltaic current. We have focused on skew scattering mediated by crystalline defects, which results in the following:

\text{Defect-mediated}\;\boldsymbol{j}_{\text{ballistic}}=-\frac{|e|}{\mathcal{V}}\sum_{BB^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime\prime\prime}}\boldsymbol{v}_{B}(W^{mr})^{-1}_{BB^{\prime}}W^{de,a}_{B^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}}(W^{mr})^{-1}_{B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime\prime\prime}}\,\delta I^{ex,s}_{B^{\prime\prime\prime}}.

(87)

Here,
B=(b\boldsymbol{k})
and
B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})
are labels for electronic Bloch states,
\boldsymbol{v}_{B}
is the group velocity,
W^{x}_{BB^{\prime}}
is the transition rate matrix elements associated to momentum relaxation [for
x=mr
] and defect-mediated skew scattering [for
x=(de,a)
], and
\delta I^{ex,s}_{B}
is the anisotropic component of the photoexcitation rate, with explicit expressions in App.
A.4.8
. Eq. (
87
) is a more general expression for
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
which generalizes our previous expressions [Eqs. (
37
)-(
38
)] beyond the assumption of electron-hole symmetry and the momentum-relaxation-time approximation; in the last regard, Eq. (
87
) accounts for the distinct relaxation times of the different harmonics of a momentum-excited electron distribution, as explained in
A.4.5
. In addition to defect-mediated processes, other mechanisms of skew scattering (e.g., phonon-mediated
28
28

28

See Eq. (20) in
15
.
) can also be activated by the vortex-induced photoexcitation anisotropy.

(iii) Beyond bulk photovoltaic effects, the vortex-induced photoexcitation anisotropy should combine with diffuse surface scattering to produce a surface photovoltaic effect.
9
In 2D systems, the above sentence should be modified by replacing ‘bulk’ with ‘2D-bulk’ and ‘surface’ with ‘edge’.

It would be interesting to investigate the above vortex-enhanced photovoltaic effects more generally in topological materials, beyond the present study of the defect-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
.

V.3
Bulk-photovoltaic probe of topological semimetallic and correlated insulating phases

As a representative application of our theory of the defect-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
, we
consider
n
-graphene (rhombohedral
n
-layer graphene), which hosts both topological semimetallic and correlated insulating phases depending on the value of
n
, the choice of substrate, and the externally applied displacement field.
46
;
35
.

We begin with the uncorrelated semimetallic phases in free-standing
n
-graphene, which is centrosymmetric (
P
-symmetric) and does not admit a bulk photovoltaic current in its pristine form. If impurities are implanted (by defect engineering) with a uniformly-oriented electric dipole moment, then these impurities can mediate
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}\propto\omega^{\text{exponent}}
in the low-frequency response. This frequency exponent is
-2/n
if the impurities are pure dipoles with negligible net charge, and equals
+2/n
if the impurities are charged dipoles [Tab.
3
], according to the impurity-dependent power laws more generally described in Sec.
III.2
.

If we restrict ourselves to plain-vanilla, monopolar-charged impurities with no dipolarity, then free-standing, uncorrelated
n
-graphene will not admit a defect-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
, according to the symmetry theorems in Sec.
IV.2
. However, an out-of-plane displacement field will generate a
P
-breaking Dirac mass and induce a band gap, and concomitantly activate
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
. Theorists be forewarned! A naive modeling based on lowest-order effective Hamiltonians can give the illusion of a null
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
; higher-order-in-
\boldsymbol{k}
warping terms are key to dispelling this illusion, as explicated in Sec.
III.3.2
.

Let us move on to the correlated insulating phases of
n
-graphene, some of which have manifested experimentally for
n\geq 4
,
35
according to Tab.
14
. To the extent that intra-valley and intra-spin scattering dominate for longer-wavelength, non-magnetic disorder, the total
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
is a sum of contributions from each valley and spin flavor [Eq. (
76
)], and each of these contributions depends on the flavor-dependent Dirac mass listed in Tab.
14
. Thus do we determine which phases admit
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
(mediated by monopolar impurities), and which phases admit a spin-differentiated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}^{s}
(defined by spin-up
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
minus spin-down
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
). This illustrates the utility of bulk photovoltaics in diagnosing correlated phases, as will be elaborated in the following experimental program.

Each correlated insulating phase (first column) is described by valley- and spin-dependent Dirac mass (third column). ‘
\Delta\,\sigma_{3}s_{3}
’ means that the gapping parameter
\Delta_{\xi,+}=-\Delta_{\xi,-}=\Delta
[Eq. (

)] is valley-independent but spin-dependent; ‘
\Delta\,\sigma_{3}\tau_{3}

\Delta_{+,s}=-\Delta_{-,s}=\Delta
is spin-independent but valley-dependent. The second column indicates the number of layers
(n)

\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
(mediated by monopole impurities, summing over all valleys and spin) is symmetry-allowed; the last column specifies if
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}^{s}
(defined by spin-up
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
minus spin-down
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
) is symmetry-allowed.

V.4
Tri-pronged experimental program

Consider a tri-pronged experimental program that combines:

(i) Photoconductivity measurements: polarization-variability of the optical setup is necessary to test the symmetry constraints of the photovoltaic tensor [Sec.
IV
] so as to experimentally confirm that the photocurrent is a bulk photovoltaic current; probing the low-energy properties of topological semimetals requires a mid- to far-infrared laser; to test the impurity-dependent power laws [Sec.
III.2
], frequency tunability of the laser is desirable;

(ii) Crystalline defect characterization, by transmission electron and/or scanning tunneling microscopy;

(iii) Defect engineering by variation of material synthesis parameters, e.g., dopant type and concentration, stochiometric ratios in flux growths.

Such a tri-pronged program serves three purposes:

(a) A phenomenological framework to rigorously diagnose dirty topological materials. The sharpest experimental signatures of topological materials rely on the materials being clean.
4
In contrast, the defect-mediated bulk photovoltaic effect dominates when materials are dirty.

(b) Pin down the dominant mechanism of the bulk photovoltaic current. A bulk photovoltaic current that grows in proportionality to the defect concentration is certainly defect-mediated. It is desirable to distinguish between the two components of the defect-mediated bulk photovoltaic current: shift vs ballistic.
16
;
13
The impurity-dependent power laws [Sec.
III.2
] and the Hamiltonian-specific symmetry constraints of the photovoltaic tensor [Sec.
IV.2
] are unique to the defect-mediated ballistic photovoltaic effect.

(c) Defect engineering allows to move in the material phase space; such motion can be theory-guided toward optimizing optoelectronic functionalities (e.g., polarization-sensitive photodetection) based on the bulk photovoltaic effect.

: Preliminaries

•

: A review of the basic vocabulary underlying Hamiltonians with crystallographic symmetry.

•

: We transition from exact Hamiltonians to effective few-band Hamiltonians, explaining their strengths, limitations and point-group symmetries. Emphasis is placed on the effective Hamiltonians of
n
-Dirac/Weyl fermions.

•

A.3
: A review of basic properties of matrix elements of screened impurity potentials. Extra care is taken for 3D impurity potentials screened by 2D electrons.

•

A.4
: This appendix elucidates and solves the kinetic model which applies to disordered bulk-photovoltaic materials.

•

A.5
: Expressions for the impurity-mediated ballistic photocurrent are derived, with and without the momentum relaxation time approximation.

•

: The Chern-vorticity theorem is proven for a spherical, enclosing surface.

•

B.2
: The optical affinity of two-band effective Hamiltonians is expressed in terms of the quantum metric and Berry curvature, with an application to the isotropic Weyl fermion.

•

B.3
: The point-group-symmetric constraint on the photonic shift vector field is derived.

•

B.4
: Based on the effective Hamiltonians of multifold fermions, their Chern numbers and optical vorticity are calculated and demonstrated to satisfy the Chern-vorticity theorem.

•

B.5
: Equivalent expressions of the director field are discussed. A duality is proven between optical vortices and skyrmions of the director field, with an application to the isotropic Weyl fermion.

Appendix A
Preliminaries

A.1
Crystalline Hamiltonians and their point-group symmetries

Let
\check{H}^{0}
be the mean-field Hamiltonian of electronic quasiparticles in a 3D crystalline medium of volume
{\cal V}=L_{x}L_{y}L_{z}
(which will be taken to infinity). Because this medium has discrete translational symmetry in three independent directions, the eigenstates of
\check{H}^{0}
can be chosen to have a Bloch-wave form
10
in the Schrödinger representation,

\displaystyle{(\check{H}^{0}-E_{B})|\,\psi_{B}\,)=0;\;\;\;\;\psi_{B}(\boldsymbol{r})=(\boldsymbol{r}\,|\,\psi_{B})=\tfrac{e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\sqrt{{\cal V}}}}u_{B}(\boldsymbol{r});\;\;\;\;u_{B}(\boldsymbol{r})=u_{B}(\boldsymbol{r}+\boldsymbol{R}).}

(88)

meaning that each wave function is product of a plane-wave phase factor and a
cell-periodic function
that is periodic under translation by any Bravais-lattice vector (
\boldsymbol{R}
). Here and throughout the text, we have employed the composite index

\displaystyle{B=(b,\boldsymbol{k});\;\;\;\;B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime});\;\;\;\;B^{\prime\prime}=(b^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime})}

(89)

to combine the band index and wavevector.

In addition to having discrete translational symmetry, a crystalline medium may also have point-group symmetries. A general point-group symmetry (
g
) acts on spacetime

\displaystyle{g\circ(\boldsymbol{r},t)=(g^{s,{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{r},i_{g}t);\;\;\;\;g^{s,{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}=g^{s,{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{ab}r_{b};\;\;\;\;i_{g}\in\{-1,1\};}

(90)

with
g^{s}_{ab}
being an
O(3)
matrix.
i_{g}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
index that distinguishes between a

\displaystyle{\text{Spatial point-group transformation:}\;\;\;\;g=g^{s},\;\;\;\;i_{g^{s}}=1;{\\
}\text{Magnetic point-group transformation:}\;\;\;\;g=Tg^{s}=g^{s}T,\;\;\;\;i_{Tg^{s}}=-1,}

(91)

with
Tg_{s}
meaning the composition of time-reversal with a spatial point-group transformation.

When the same symmetry
g
acts on Schrödinger-type wave functions [
\psi(\boldsymbol{r})=(\boldsymbol{r}|\psi)
] and the first-quantized position operator
\check{r}
,
g
is represented by an operator
\check{g}
:

\displaystyle{\check{g}\check{r}_{a}\check{g}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}=g^{s,{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{ab}\check{r}_{b};\;\;\;\;(\boldsymbol{r}|\check{g}=K^{g}(g^{s,{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}|\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;(\boldsymbol{r}|\check{g}|\psi)=\overline{(g^{s,{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}|\psi)}^{g}.}

(92)

\check{g}
is unitary if
i_{g}=1
and otherwise is antiunitary; this manifests in Eq. (
92
) with

K^{g}=K
(operator of complex conjugation) if
g
inverts time, and otherwise
K^{g}=1
. In particular, for the exponentiated position operator,

\displaystyle{\check{g}e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\check{\boldsymbol{r}}}\check{g}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}=e^{i(g\cdot\boldsymbol{k})\cdot\check{\boldsymbol{r}}};\;\;\;\;g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}=i_{g}g^{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}.}

(93)

Our crystalline Hamiltonian has a point-group symmetry
g
if

\displaystyle{[\check{g},\check{H}^{0}]=0\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\check{g}\check{H}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\check{g}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{expposition}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\check{H}^{0}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\check{H}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv e^{-i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\check{\boldsymbol{r}}}\check{H}^{0}e^{i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\check{\boldsymbol{r}}}.}

(94)

It is convenient to separate the positional coordinate into an intracellular coordinate (parametrizing a primitive unit cell) and a Bravais-lattice vector:

\displaystyle{\text{Position}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{r}=\;(\text{Intracellular}\;\boldsymbol{\tau})+\;(\text{Bravais-lattice vector}\;\boldsymbol{R})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.}

(95)

Bloch’s theorem allows to view
\check{H}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
as a Hamiltonian acting on wave functions of the intracellular coordinate defined within a primitive unit cell.
10
In the transition from the Hilbert space of wave functions over
\mathbb{R}^{3}
to intracellular wave functions, we concomitantly modify our notations for operators and bra-kets:

\displaystyle{(\hat{H}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{b\boldsymbol{k}})|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle=0;\;\;\;\;\hat{g}\hat{H}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}=\hat{H}^{0}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(96)

Our normalization is

\displaystyle{(\psi_{B}\,|\,\psi_{B^{\prime}})=\int_{\boldsymbol{R}^{d}}|\psi_{B}(\boldsymbol{r})|^{2}d\boldsymbol{r}=\delta_{BB^{\prime}}\iff\langle u_{B}\,|\,u_{B}^{\prime}\rangle=\int_{{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}}\overline{u_{B}(\boldsymbol{\tau})}u_{B^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\tau})\tfrac{d\boldsymbol{\tau}}{{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}}=\delta_{BB^{\prime}},}

(97)

with
\delta_{BB^{\prime}}=\delta_{bb^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
being a product of dimensionless Kronecker delta functions. If spin is an important degree of freedom, then both inner products in Eq. (
97
) should include a summation over spin indices.

A.2
Effective Hamiltonians and their point-group symmetries

A.2.1
The topological value of effective Hamiltonians

A minimal model for a geometrically nontrivial wave function involves a two-band, Luttinger-Kohn-type, effective Hamiltonian:
48

\displaystyle{H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\delta E_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathbb{1}_{2};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{3};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\sigma}=(\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}},\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}},\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}});\;\;\;\;\mathbb{1}_{2}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}.}

(98)

Such a Hamiltonian acts in a two-dimensional Hilbert space with basis vectors expressible as
|\,u_{1,\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}\,\rangle
and
|\,u_{2,\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}\,\rangle
, with
|\,u_{b,\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}\,\rangle
being an energy eigenstate of the exact Hamiltonian
\hat{H}^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
[Eq. (
96
)] at a (typically high-symmetry) wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
, which in our context is the wavevector of the Dirac-Weyl node.

By diagonalizing the effective Hamiltonian as

\displaystyle{U_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{{}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}H_{\boldsymbol{k}}U_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\delta E_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathbb{1}_{2}+|\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}|\sigma_{3},}

(99)

the columns of the unitary matrix
U_{\boldsymbol{k}}
can be used to construct:

\displaystyle{\text{Approximate energy eigenstates}\;\;\;\;|\,u_{\pm,\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{2}|\,u_{\alpha\boldsymbol{k}_{0}}\,\rangle(U_{\boldsymbol{k}})_{\alpha\pm}.}

(100)

In contrast, the exact energy eigenstates (at
\boldsymbol{k}\neq\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
) are not generally expressible as linear combinations of the exact energy eigenstates (at
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
). The error in the Luttinger-Kohn approximation [Eq. (
100
)] becomes more severe as
\boldsymbol{k}
deviates further from
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
. It follows that eigenstate-dependent quantities (like the Berry connection) calculated in the Luttinger-Kohn approximation will deviate quantitatively from their exact values.
3

Nevertheless, not all is doom and gloom. The value of effective Hamiltonians like Eq. (
98
) is that they accurately encode topological quantities like the Chern number (for a
\boldsymbol{k}
-sphere enclosing
\boldsymbol{k}_{0}
), which implies [by the Chern-vorticity theorem in Eq. (
5
)] that effective Hamiltonians also accurately encode the optical vorticity (of said
\boldsymbol{k}
-sphere). Because of quantitative errors in the interband Berry connection,
3
the
\boldsymbol{k}
-position of a vortex line (approximated by an effective Hamiltonian) may deviate from the exact
\boldsymbol{k}
-position. But this will not change the topological fact that vortex lines must intersect the
\boldsymbol{k}
-sphere, with a net vorticity determined by the Chern numbers of conduction and valence bands [Eq. (
5
)]. To further reduce the gloom, certain point-group-symmetric effective Hamiltonians will exactly encode the
\boldsymbol{k}
-position of the vortex line, e.g., in cases when the vortex line is symmetry-pinned to a rotation-invariant
\boldsymbol{k}
-line [cf. Eq. (
238
)]. This motivates identifying the point groups of the effective Hamiltonians we use to model
n
-Dirac/Weyl fermions.

A.2.2
Point groups of
n
-order Dirac-Weyl Hamiltonians

The
n
-Weyl Hamiltonian
31
is

\displaystyle{H^{w,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=f_{n}\big[k_{+}^{n}\sigma_{-}+\;h.c.\;\big]+vk_{z}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}};\;\;\;\;k_{\pm}=k_{x}\pm ik_{y};\;\;\;\;\sigma_{\pm}=\tfrac{\sigma_{x}\pm i\sigma_{y}}{2}.}

(101)

The point group is
D_{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
, with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
being the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
group generated by time reversal, and
D_{\infty}
being the dihedral group
D_{2}
(generated by two-fold rotations
C_{2x}
and
C_{2y}
about the x- and y-axes respectively) with an additional continuous rotation symmetry about the z axis denoted
R_{z}^{\theta}
:

\displaystyle{\widehat{R_{z}^{\theta}}H^{w,n}_{k_{\pm},k_{z}}\widehat{R_{z}(-\theta)}=H^{w,n}_{e^{\pm i\theta}k_{\pm},k_{z}};\;\;\;\;e^{-i\tfrac{\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}}{2}\phi}\sigma_{\pm}e^{i\tfrac{\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}}{2}\phi}=e^{\mp i\phi}\sigma_{\pm};\;\;\;\;R_{z}\big(\tfrac{2\pi}{m}\big)=C_{mz};\;\;\;\;m=2,3,4,6.}

(102)

The direct product structure in
D_{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
encodes the fact that time-reversal commutes with all spatial transformations. If
v=f_{1}
, then the point group of
H^{w,1}
is upgraded to
SO(3)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
. Spatial inversion
P
is not a symmetry; if it were,
PT
would be, ensuring either a Kramers degeneracy or zero Abelian Berry curvature at each
\boldsymbol{k}
, depending on whether the representation of
PT
is projective or linear; either case is incompatible with n-Weyl fermions. Mirror reflection is not a symmetry; if it were, mirror composed with two-fold rotation gives
P
symmetry, which has been demonstrated to be absent.

The specific Hamiltonian and symmetry representations for each
n
-Weyl fermions are given in Tab.
15
. For odd
n
, the symmetry representations of
D_{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
are realized projectively (e.g.,
T^{2}=
identity is projectively represented as
T^{2}=-\mathbb{1}
), meaning that the Weyl fermion is naturally interpreted as having half-odd-integer (pseudo)spin.

n

\widehat{C_{2x}}

\widehat{C_{2y}}

\widehat{C_{2z}}

Tab.
7
shows the resultant point groups when we add to
H^{w,n}
(and
H^{d,n}
) various symmetry-reducing corrections [Eqs. (
66
)-(
70
)].
In verifying if a Hamiltonian is
C_{mz}
-symmetric with the specific representation (
R_{z}^{n}
) of
C_{mz}
of
n
-Dirac/Weyl fermions:

\displaystyle{R^{n}_{z}\big(\tfrac{2\pi}{m}\big)H_{k_{\pm},k_{z}}R^{n}_{z}\big(-\tfrac{2\pi}{m}\big)=H_{e^{\pm i{2\pi}/{m}}k_{\pm},k_{z}}\;\;\;\;\text{with}\;\;\;\;R^{n}_{z}(\theta)=\exp\big({-i\tfrac{n\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}}{2}\theta}\big),}

(104)

it is useful to recognize that the general Hamiltonian term proportional to

\displaystyle{&(k_{+}^{a}k_{-}^{b}\sigma_{+}+h.c.)\;\text{is $C_{mz}$-symmetric}\;\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;a-b+n\in m\mathbb{Z};{\\
}k_{+}^{c}k_{-}^{d}\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}\;\text{is $C_{mz}$-symmetric}\;\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;c-d\in m\mathbb{Z},}

which follows from the rotation-induced transformation of
\sigma_{\pm}
in Eq. (
102
). A useful corollary is that

\displaystyle{a-b+n\;\text{odd,}\;\;\;\;\text{or}\;\;\;\;c-d\;\text{odd}\;\;\;\;\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;C_{2z}\text{-symmetry broken,}}

(105)

which is a necessary condition for any bulk photovoltaic current in a 2D system, according to the symmetry theorem in Eq. (
79
).

For pedagogy and demonstrated rigor, we carry out the symmetry-group identification for our most complex effective Hamiltonian, namely, the
n
-Dirac Hamiltonian plus a
6^{\prime}
-warp correction, which sums to the Koshino-McCann effective Hamiltonian for a single valley of
n
-graphene:
41

\displaystyle{H^{d,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+H^{6^{\prime},n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv H^{KM,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{a,b,c}^{\mathbb{N}}h_{abc}k_{+}^{a}k_{-}^{b}\sigma_{+}+h.c.;\;\;\;\;b+2a+3c=n;\;\;\;\;h_{abc}\in\mathbb{R};\;\;\;\;a,b,c\in\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,3,\ldots\}.}

(106)

•

The condition on the natural numbers in Eq. (
106
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle{a-b+n=3(a+c)\in 3\mathbb{Z},}

(107)

which certainly satisfies the condition for
C_{3z}
-symmetry [Eq. (
A.2.2
) with
m=3
].

•

Given that the summation in Eq. (
106
) contains terms with
(a+c)
being odd, Eq. (
107
) implies that
C_{2z}
-symmetry is broken, according to Eq. (
A.2.2
) with
m=2
.

•

Let us verify that the Koshino-McCann Hamiltonian has
M_{x}T
symmetry:

\displaystyle{\widehat{TM_{x}}H^{KM,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\widehat{TM_{x}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}=H^{KM,n}_{TM_{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}};\;\;\;\;\widehat{M_{x}T}=K.}

(108)

This holds because conjugation by
K
maps
k_{\pm}\rightarrow k_{\mp}
, and so does
TM_{x}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}=(k_{x},-k_{y})
map
k_{\pm}\rightarrow k_{\mp}
.

•

The Koshino-McCann Hamiltonian also has
M_{y}
symmetry represented by
\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}
(modulo multiplicative phase factor):

\displaystyle{\widehat{M_{y}}H^{KM,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\widehat{M_{y}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}=\sum_{a,b,c}^{\mathbb{N}}h_{abc}k_{+}^{a}k_{-}^{b}\sigma_{-}+h.c.=H^{KM,n}_{k_{x},-k_{y}}.}

(109)

•

T
-transforming the Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle{\text{Even}\;n:\;\;\;\;\widehat{T}=\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}K;\;\;\;\;\widehat{T}H^{KM,n}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\widehat{T}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}=\sum_{a,b,c}^{\mathbb{N}}h_{abc}k_{-}^{a}k_{+}^{b}\sigma_{-}+h.c.}

(110)

which only equals
H^{KM,n}_{-\boldsymbol{k}}
is
(a+b)\in 2\mathbb{Z}
. Given that the summation in Eq. (
106
) contains terms with
(a+b)
being odd,
T
symmetry is broken. One arrives at the same condition for
n
odd, albeit with a different representation for time reversal:
\widehat{T}=-i\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}K
.

•

Since
TM_{x}
is preserved but
T
broken,
M_{x}
must also be broken.

Altogether, the preserved symmetries are
C_{3z},M_{y}
and
M_{x}T
, which generate the magnetic point group
6^{\prime}mm^{\prime}
.

Let us check that this result is consistent with the layer group of
n
-graphene being
p\bar{3}m1\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
,
54
with point group
D_{3d}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
. This point group contains elements:
C_{2}^{\prime}
(two-fold rotation with an in-plane axis),
P
(spatial inversion),
T
and
S_{6}=M_{x}C_{6z}
(six-fold roto-rotation); the aforementioned four elements map
K
to
K^{\prime}
, i.e., they exchange valleys. Therefore, only pairwise compositions (like
TP
) belong to the

\displaystyle{\text{Little group of K}\;={\bar{3}^{\prime}m}=C_{3v}\cup\{TP,3TC_{2}^{\prime},2TS_{6}\}}

(111)

If we ignore the
z
coordinate in these three-dimensional spatial transformations, the little group of K projects to

\displaystyle{\bar{3}^{\prime}m=C_{3v}\cup\{TP,3TC_{2}^{\prime},2TS_{6}\}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\text{projects}\\
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;6^{\prime}mm^{\prime}=C_{3v}\cup\{TC_{2z},3TM_{x},2TC_{6z}\}.}

(112)

Thus the two group are isomorphic, with
TP
corresponding to
TC_{2z}
and
TC_{2}^{\prime}
corresponding to
TM_{x}
, etc. The distinction between the two groups is irrelevant to symmetry constraints of photovoltaic tensors; this crucially justifies our application of
6^{\prime}mm^{\prime}
to
n
-graphene.

A.2.3
The Hamiltonian-vector representation

For any two-band Hamiltonian
H^{0}
[Eq. (
98
)] with the symmetry of the point group
G
, we will introduce a
Hamiltonian-vector representation
of
G
, which represents each element of
G
as
an
O(3)
-matrix, which should not be confused with the defining
O(3+1)
-matrix representation of
G
