acting on spacetime.

What it means for
g\in G
to be a symmetry of
H^{0}
is

\displaystyle{\text{Two-band Hamiltonian symmetry}:\;\;\;\;H^{0}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}}=\hat{g}H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\hat{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}=\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\hat{g}\boldsymbol{\sigma}\hat{g}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}+\delta E_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathbb{1}_{2}=(\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}})\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\delta E_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathbb{1}_{2},}

(113)

with
\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}
an
O(3)
matrix acting on vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. In fact, the set of all
\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}
forms an
O(3)
-matrix representation of
G
with the property that
\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}
is improper if and only if
g
is time-inverting (
i_{g}=-1)
:

\displaystyle{\det\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}=i_{g}=-1\;\;\;\;\text{if $g$ inverts time, and otherwise equals}\;\;\;\;+1.}

(114)

(Even the parity transformation which is an improper rotation in the defining representation [
P\circ(x,y,z)=(-x,-y,-z)
] is a proper rotation in the Hamiltonian-vector representation.)
The reason for Eq. (
114
) is that if
g
inverts time, the representation
\hat{g}
acting on complex wave functions (in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
) is antiunitary, meaning
\hat{g}=U_{g}K
, with
U_{g}
a unitary two-by-two matrix, and
K
implementing complex conjugation. On one hand, because of the group homomorphism between
U(2)
and
SO(3)
,
69

\displaystyle{U_{g}\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}U_{g}^{-1}=[(\text{Proper rotation matrix})\cdot\boldsymbol{d}]\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}.}

(115)

On the other hand, because
K\sigma_{2}K=-\sigma_{2}
,

\displaystyle{K\boldsymbol{d}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}K=(d_{1},-d_{2},d_{3})\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}=[(\text{Improper rotation matrix})\cdot\boldsymbol{d}]\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}.}

(116)

Bringing both hands together by multiplying the proper rotation matrix [Eq. (
115
)] with the improper rotation matrix [Eq. (
116
)], we deduce that
\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}
must be improper. We present the mapping between
\hat{g}
and
\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}
in Tab.
17
.

\hat{g}

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{1}}K

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}K

\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}K

e^{-i\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{3}}\phi/2}

\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}

\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}

\begin{pmatrix}\cos(\phi)&-\sin(\phi)&0\\
\sin(\phi)&\cos(\phi)&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}

Table 17:
Homomorphism between two-by-two (anti)unitary matrices and three-by-three orthogonal matrices. This mapping is preserved even if we multiply
\hat{g}
by an arbitrary complex phase factor.

For illustration, for the 1-Weyl Hamiltonian in Eq. (
47
) with
\hat{g}
tabulated in Tab.
15
,

\displaystyle{\overleftrightarrow{{C_{2x}}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix};\;\;\;\;\overleftrightarrow{{C_{2y}}}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix};\;\;\;\;\overleftrightarrow{{C_{2z}}}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix};\;\;\;\;\overleftrightarrow{{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0\\
0&-1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}.}

(117)

This Hamiltonian-vector representation
\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}
is linear (e.g.
\overleftrightarrow{T}
squares to the identity matrix), even though the representation
\hat{g}
is projective.

In some cases, the Hamiltonian-vector representation contains the trivial representation as a subrepresentation. This means that there exists an
invariant vector

\boldsymbol{v}
which is invariant under all
O(3)
transformations:

\displaystyle{\text{Invariant vector:}\;\;\;\;\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\;\;\;\;\text{for all}\;\;\;\;g\in G,}

(118)

and is a basis vector in the trivial representation.

For all
n
-order Dirac-Weyl Hamiltonians, there are no trivial
\boldsymbol{d}
-vector subrepresentations, i.e., no invariant vectors. To prove this for odd
n
, we observe from Tab.
15
-
16
that time reversal is represented as
\hat{T}\propto\sigma_{\scriptscriptstyle{2}}K
, which implies
\overleftrightarrow{{T}}
has the form given by the right-most equality in Eq. (
117
), and that
\overleftrightarrow{{T}}\circ\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{v}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{0}.
For even
n
,

\displaystyle{\overleftrightarrow{{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&-1\end{pmatrix}\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;\overleftrightarrow{{R_{z}^{\theta}}}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos(n\theta)&-\sin(n\theta)&0\\
\sin(n\theta)&\cos(n\theta)&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix};}

(119)

the invariance of
\boldsymbol{v}=(v_{1},v_{2},v_{3})
under
\overleftrightarrow{{T}}
implies
v_{3}=0
; the invariance under
\overleftrightarrow{{R_{z}^{\theta}}}
implies
v_{1}=v_{2}=0
; altogether,
\boldsymbol{v}=\boldsymbol{0}
for all
n
.

As a final application, the two- and three-point Bargmann invariants [defined in Eq. (
44
), with
|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle
being the higher-energy eigenstate]
are expressible in terms of the Hamiltonian vector as in Eq. (
52
) and

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=&\;\tfrac{1}{2}\bigg(1+\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\bigg);\;\;\;\;\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}/|\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}|}

(120)

If
g
is a Hamiltonian symmetry, then
\boldsymbol{d}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}}=\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
[from Eq. (
113
)] implies the following constraints on the Bargmann invariants:

\displaystyle{{\mathscr{B}}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k},g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=&\;{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;\mbox{Im}{{\mathscr{B}}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k},g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime},g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}=i_{g}\mbox{Im}{{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}};\;\;\;\;\mbox{Re}{{\mathscr{B}}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k},g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime},g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}=\mbox{Re}{{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}},}

(121)

bearing in mind that time-inverting symmetries (
i_{g}=-1
) are represented as an improper rotation on the
\boldsymbol{d}
vector [Eq. (
114
)], which leaves dot products invariant, but inverts the orientation of the triple product.

A.3
Impurity potentials

A.3.1
Matrix elements of general scalar potentials

In general, matrix elements of a scalar potential
V
(in the Bloch basis) are written as

\displaystyle{\text{Scalar potential matrix element}\;\;\;\;V_{B,B^{\prime}}=(\,\psi_{B}\,|V|\,\psi_{B^{\prime}}\,).}

(122)

For small-momentum scattering induced by a scalar potential that is smooth on the scale of the crystalline lattice period,

\displaystyle{\text{Small-momentum scattering:}\;\;\;\;V_{BB^{\prime}}\approx\widetilde{V}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\langle u_{B}\,|\,u_{B^{\prime}}\rangle;\;\;\;\;\text{Fourier transform}\;\;\;\;\tilde{V}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv\frac{1}{{\cal V}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}d\boldsymbol{r}e^{-i\boldsymbol{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}V_{\boldsymbol{r}}.}

(123)

How come? Decomposing the total crystalline volume
{\cal V}={\cal N}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}
as the product of the number of primitive unit cells times the volume of each cell, and separating the spatial coordinate
\boldsymbol{r}=\boldsymbol{\tau}+\boldsymbol{R}
into an intracellular coordinate and a Bravais-lattice vector,

V_{BB^{\prime}}=\int_{\text{cell}}\frac{d\bm{\tau}}{{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}}e^{i(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k})\bm{\tau}}\overline{u_{B}(\bm{\tau})}u_{B^{\prime}}(\bm{\tau})\frac{1}{{\cal N}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}e^{i(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k})\cdot\mathbf{R}}V_{\bm{\tau}+\mathbf{R}},

(124)

We approximate
V_{\bm{\tau}+\mathbf{R}}\approx V_{\mathbf{R}}
because it is smooth on the scale of the crystalline lattice period.

V_{BB^{\prime}}\approx\int\frac{d\bm{\tau}}{{\cal V}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}}e^{i(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k})\bm{\tau}}\overline{u_{B}(\bm{\tau})}u_{B^{\prime}}(\bm{\tau})\times\frac{1}{{\cal N}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{R}}e^{i(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k})\cdot\mathbf{R}}V_{\mathbf{R}}.

(125)

Within the left integral,
e^{i(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k})\bm{\tau}}\approx 1
with a correction that is bounded by
|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}|/
(Reciprocal period); by ‘small-momentum’, we mean precisely that
|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}|/
(Reciprocal period) is small. The discrete Fourier sum in Eq. (
125
) can be approximated by a Fourier integral for ‘small-momentum’:

\displaystyle{\frac{1}{{\cal N}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{cell}}}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{R}}e^{i(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k})\cdot\mathbf{R}}V_{\mathbf{R}}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{small momentum}}\\
\approx\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\frac{1}{{\cal V}}\int_{{\cal V}}d\boldsymbol{r}e^{i(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k})\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}V_{\boldsymbol{r}}\equiv\tilde{V}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},}

(126)

bearing in mind
{\cal V}
is eventually taken to infinity. Altogether, these imply that Eq. (
125
) reduces to Eq. (
123
).

A.3.2
Symmetry classes and overscreening of impurity potentials

Let us distinguish between three symmetry classes of impurity potentials:

\displaystyle{&\text{Centrosymmetric}\;\;\;\;V^{\text{im}}=V^{s}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=V^{s}_{-\boldsymbol{r}};{\\
}\text{Noncentric}\;\;\;\;V^{\text{im}}=V^{a}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=-V^{a}_{-\boldsymbol{r}};{\\
}\text{Hybrid}\;\;\;\;V^{\text{im}}=V^{s}_{\boldsymbol{r}}+V_{\boldsymbol{r}}^{a}=V^{s}_{-\boldsymbol{r}}-V^{a}_{-\boldsymbol{r}},}

(127)

whose respective Fourier transforms (
\widetilde{V}^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}},\widetilde{V}^{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
\widetilde{V}^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}+\widetilde{V}^{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
) are real, imaginary and complex.

In this work, we focus on the two leading contributions in the multipole expansion of a three-dimensional (3D) impurity potential. Depending on whether the monopole/dipole impurity is screened by 3D
n
-Weyl fermions or by 2D
n
-Dirac fermions, its matrix elements and Fourier transforms are given as:

\displaystyle{\text{3D screening:}\;\;\;\;&V^{\text{m,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{V}^{\text{m,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\langle u_{B}|u_{B^{\prime}}\rangle;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{m,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon}\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}+k_{\text{scr}}^{2}};{\\
}V^{\text{d,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{V}^{\text{d,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\langle u_{B}|u_{B^{\prime}}\rangle;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{d,3D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv-i\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}}{|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}+k_{\text{scr}}^{2}};{\\
}\text{2D screening:}\;\;\;\;V^{\text{m,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{V}^{\text{m,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\langle u_{B}|u_{B^{\prime}}\rangle;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{m,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{2\epsilon}\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{k}|+k_{\text{scr}}};{\\
}V^{\text{d,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{V}^{\text{d,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\langle u_{B}|u_{B^{\prime}}\rangle;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{V}^{\text{d,2D}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv-i\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{2\epsilon}\frac{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{k}}{|\boldsymbol{k}|+k_{\text{scr}}},}

(128)

where
|e|\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}
is the dipole moment of the dipole impurity,
k_{\text{scr}}
is the generalized Thomas-Fermi wavevector due to screening by fermions [explicated for 2D screening in Eq. (
134
)], and
\epsilon
is a dielectric constant that heuristically accounts for screening by lattice deformations.

For intra-pocket/valley impurity-mediated scattering in a photoexcited Dirac-Weyl material,
|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}|
has an upper bound comparable to the
\boldsymbol{k}
-radius of the excitation surface [Eq. (
32
)], which is much smaller than the Brillouin zone period. For a sufficiently low frequency of the driving light source, the
\boldsymbol{k}
-radius of the excitation surface will be much smaller than generalized Thomas-Fermi wavevector, which implies that we can replace
|\boldsymbol{k}|^{2}+k_{\text{scr}}^{2}\rightarrow k_{\text{scr}}^{2}
in Eqs. (
128
)-(
128
) and
|\boldsymbol{k}|+k_{\text{scr}}\rightarrow k_{\text{scr}}
in Eqs. (
128
)-(
128
). This is what we mean for an impurity to be
overscreened
.

A.3.3
Screening of 3D potential by 2D crystalline electrons

While Eqs. (
128
)-(
128
) are attainable from standard texts,
10
Eqs. (
128
)-(
128
) deserve a careful derivation which we provide here.

We begin by setting up the basic problem of electronic screening in an arbitrary medium. Let
H^{0}
be the mean-field Hamiltonian of electron quasiparticles, and let
V
be a perturbing scalar potential:

\displaystyle{H=H^{0}+V;\;\;\;\;V_{\boldsymbol{r}}=-|e|\phi_{\boldsymbol{r}}.}

(129)

\phi_{\boldsymbol{r}}
here is the screened electric potential; its Fourier transform
\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}

relates to the externally-applied potential
\widetilde{\phi}_{ext\boldsymbol{p}}
by the

\displaystyle{\text{Inverse dielectric matrix:}\;\;\;\;\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}\epsilon^{{}^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\phi}_{ext\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}.}

(130)

This is a matrix whose elements are indexed by the canonical momentum
\boldsymbol{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, so
\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}={\cal V}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}d\boldsymbol{p}/(2\pi)^{3}
. The dielectric matrix relates
62
to the

\displaystyle{\text{Susceptibility matrix}\;\;\;\;\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}=&\;\tfrac{|e|^{2}}{{\cal V}}\sum_{BB^{\prime}}\;\langle\psi_{B^{\prime}}\,|\,e^{-i\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\psi_{B}\rangle\langle\psi_{B}\,|\,e^{i\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\psi_{B^{\prime}}\rangle\frac{f^{\text{FD}}_{BB^{\prime}}}{E_{BB^{\prime}}};\;\;\;\;f^{\text{FD}}_{BB^{\prime}}=f^{\text{FD}}_{B}-f^{\text{FD}}_{B^{\prime}},{\\
}\text{by}\;\;\;\;\epsilon_{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{p},\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}-\tfrac{1}{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}.}

(131)

(
\epsilon_{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}
is unrelated to the
\epsilon
in Eqs. (
128
)-(
128
).)
Here,
|\,\psi_{B}\,\rangle
is an eigenstate of
H^{0}
,
\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}
is the first-quantized position operator and
f^{\text{FD}}
is the Fermi-Dirac function [Eq. (
145
)]. The susceptibility matrix relates the induced charge density (screened charge density minus externally-applied charge density) to the screened potential:

\displaystyle{\text{Induced charge density}\;\;\;\;\widetilde{\delta\rho}_{\boldsymbol{p}}=&\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}\chi_{\boldsymbol{p}\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}}.}

(132)

To the extent that
B
is not necessarily a label for a Bloch wave in Eq. (
131
), the above formulation generally applies no matter the form or symmetry of
H^{0}
. In the specific context of ‘Screening of 3D potential by 2D crystalline electrons’,
we assume that:

(i) Within an energy window
[\mu-k_{B}T,\mu+k_{B}T]
, the eigenstates of
H^{0}
(labelled by
|\,\psi_{B}\,\rangle
) are two-dimensional (2D), meaning that
B=(b,\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}})
with
\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}=(k_{x},k_{y})\in rBZ
in a reduced Brillouin zone, and the wave function
\psi_{B}(x,y,z)
has negligible width in the
z
direction. This implies that screening is dominated by virtual excitations of 2D
H^{0}
-eigenstates, because the susceptibility matrix [Eq. (
131
)] is proportional to
f^{\text{FD}}_{BB^{\prime}}/E_{BB^{\prime}}
.

(ii) Since the perturbing scalar potential
V_{\boldsymbol{r}}=-|e|\phi_{\boldsymbol{r}}
is slowly varying with respect to
z
(relative to the localization length of the 2D
H^{0}
-eigenstates), in evaluating the matrix element

\displaystyle{(\psi_{B}\,|\,V_{\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\psi_{B^{\prime}})\approx-|e|(\psi_{B}\,|\,{\phi}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{x}},\hat{\boldsymbol{y}},\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}}\,|\,\psi_{B^{\prime}})\big|_{\hat{\boldsymbol{z}}\rightarrow 0}}

(133)

between two 2D eigenstates, we may as well set the scalar potential to its value at
z=0
:

\displaystyle{\phi_{\boldsymbol{r}_{\perp},z=0}=&\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{p}}e^{i\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}_{\perp}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}}e^{i\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}_{\perp}}\widetilde{\phi}^{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}};\;\;\;\;\widetilde{\phi}^{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}}=\sum_{p_{z}}\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{p}};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}=(p_{x},p_{y});\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{r}_{\perp}=(x,y);{\\
}\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}}=\;{\cal V}\int_{\boldsymbol{R}^{3}}\tfrac{d\boldsymbol{p}}{(2\pi)^{3}};\;\;\;\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}}=L_{x}L_{y}\int_{\boldsymbol{R}^{2}}\tfrac{d\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}}{(2\pi)^{2}};\;\;\;\;\sum_{p_{z}}=L_{z}\int_{\boldsymbol{R}}\tfrac{dp_{z}}{2\pi};\;\;\;\;{\cal V}=L_{x}L_{y}L_{z}.}

Under these assumptions, long-wavelength, intraband screening of the potential leads to an effective dielectric function

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\phi}^{\perp}_{ext\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}\equiv\sum_{p_{z}}\widetilde{\phi}_{ext\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}p_{z}}=\epsilon^{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}\widetilde{\phi}^{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}};\;\;\;\;\epsilon^{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}=1+\tfrac{k_{scr}}{|\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}|};\;\;\;\;k_{scr}=\tfrac{e^{2}}{2}\partial_{\mu}n^{\perp};\;\;\;\;n^{\perp}=\int_{rBZ}\tfrac{d\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\sum_{b}f^{\text{FD}}_{bk_{\perp}},}

(134)

with
n^{\perp}
being the number density (per unit area) of 2D eigenstates, and
\partial_{\mu}n^{\perp}
being the derivative with respect to the chemical potential (or ‘quasi-Fermi level’ in the non-equilibrium steady state).

To prove Eq. (
134
), we begin from the general susceptibility matrix in Eq. (
131
) and split the canonical momentum as

\displaystyle{\text{canonical momentum}\;\boldsymbol{p}=(\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp},p_{z});\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}\in rBZ;\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}\in rRL,}

(135)

with
\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}
(or sometimes
\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}
) a 2D crystal momentum in the reduced BZ, and
\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}
a 2D reciprocal lattice vector. Letting
B=(b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}})
and
B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}^{\prime})
label our 2D eigenstates,

\displaystyle{&(\psi_{B^{\prime}}\,|\,e^{-i\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\psi_{B})(\psi_{B}\,|\,e^{i\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\psi_{B^{\prime}})=(\psi_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}^{\prime}}\,|\,e^{-i(\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\perp}}e^{-ip_{z}\hat{z}}\,|\,\psi_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}})(\psi_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\,|\,e^{i(\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\perp}}e^{ip_{z}^{\prime}\hat{z}}\,|\,\psi_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}^{\prime}}).}

(136)

Because of assumption (ii), we may set
e^{-ip_{z}\hat{z}}\rightarrow 1
within a matrix element. By 2D crystalline momentum conservation,

\displaystyle{(\psi_{B^{\prime}}\,|\,e^{-i\boldsymbol{p}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\psi_{B})(\psi_{B}\,|\,e^{i\boldsymbol{p}^{\prime}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\psi_{B^{\prime}})=(\psi_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}^{\prime}}\,|\,e^{-i(\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\perp}}\,|\,\psi_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}})(\psi_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}\,|\,e^{i(\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime})\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\perp}}\,|\,\psi_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}^{\prime}})\delta_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp},\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}},\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}.}

(137)

Substituting this into Eq. (
131
), we find that the susceptibility matrix

\displaystyle{\chi_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp},p_{z};\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime},p_{z}^{\prime}}=&\;\chi^{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}_{\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp},\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp},\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}}\;\text{is independent of}\;p_{z}\;\text{and}\;p_{z}^{\prime};{\\
}\chi^{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}_{\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp},\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}}\equiv\;\tfrac{|e|^{2}}{{\cal V}}\sum_{bb^{\prime}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}^{rBZ}\langle u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\,|\,e^{-i\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{\tau}}_{\perp}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}\rangle\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}\,|\,e^{i\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{\tau}}_{\perp}}\,|\,u_{b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\rangle\frac{f^{\text{FD}}_{(b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp})(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}})}}{E_{(b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp})(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}})}},}

(138)

with
\hat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}_{\perp}
the intracell component of
\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\perp}
, and
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}^{rBZ}=L_{x}L_{y}\int_{rBZ}d\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}/(2\pi)^{2}
. Because the susceptibility matrix is diagonal in
\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}
and independent of
p_{z}
and
p_{z}^{\prime}
, the induced charge density [Eq. (
132
)]

\displaystyle{\widetilde{\delta\rho}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp},p_{z}}=&\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}\chi_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}p_{z},\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}}\chi^{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}_{\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp},\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}}\sum_{p_{z}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}\;\text{is independent of}\;p_{z},}

(139)

which implies
\delta\rho_{\boldsymbol{r}}\propto\delta(z),
in accordance with the screening electrons being in the
z=0
layer.

If we focus on the intraband contribution (
b=b^{\prime}
) and ignore Umklapp scattering, the susceptibility matrix reduces to a scalar function:

\displaystyle{\text{Intraband, long-wavelength susceptibility}\;\;\;\;\chi^{\text{intra}\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}_{\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{0}}=&\;-\tfrac{e^{2}}{L_{z}}\partial_{\mu}n^{\perp}+\tfrac{|e|^{2}}{{\cal V}}\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}\cdot\sum_{b}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}^{rBZ}f_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}f^{\text{FD}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\;\boldsymbol{g}_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+O(\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{3}).}

(140)

The first term is analogous to the Thomas-Fermi susceptibility; the
O(\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{2})
correction involves the quantum metric
\boldsymbol{g}_{B}
defined by

\displaystyle{\big|\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}\rangle\big|^{2}=1-\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{g}_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+O(\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{3}).}

(141)

To derive Eq. (
140
), we begin from Eq. (
138
) and approximate the right-most fraction by the first-order Taylor approximation of both numerator and denominator:

\displaystyle{\frac{f^{\text{FD}}_{(b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp})(b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}})}}{E_{(b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp})(b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}})}}\approx\frac{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}f^{\text{FD}}_{b}}{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}E_{b}}=-\partial_{\mu}f^{\text{FD}}_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}},}

(142)

which is valid for
b=b^{\prime}
and not otherwise. Then

\displaystyle{\chi^{\text{intra}\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}_{\boldsymbol{0}\boldsymbol{0}}=&\;-\tfrac{|e|^{2}}{{\cal V}}\sum_{b}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}^{rBZ}(\psi_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\,|\,e^{-i\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\psi_{b\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}})(\psi_{b\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}\,|\,e^{i\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{r}}}\,|\,\psi_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}})\partial_{\mu}f_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}=-\tfrac{|e|^{2}}{{\cal V}}\partial_{\mu}\sum_{b}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}^{rBZ}f_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\big|\langle u_{b\boldsymbol{k}_{{\perp}}}\,|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}+\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}\rangle\big|^{2}.}

By expanding the last inner product in powers of
\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}
[Eq. (
141
)] and applying
{\cal V}=L_{x}L_{y}L_{z}
, we arrive at Eq. (
140
).

The general dielectric matrix [Eq. (
130
)] reduces in our context to

\displaystyle{\epsilon_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}p_{z},\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}p_{z},\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}-\tfrac{1}{(\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}+\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp})^{2}+p_{z}^{2}}\chi^{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}_{\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp},\boldsymbol{G}_{\perp}^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp},\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}}.}

(143)

Keeping only the leading, Thomas-Fermi-like term in Eq. (
140
),

\displaystyle{\epsilon_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}p_{z},\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}\approx&\;\delta_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp},\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}}\bigg(\delta_{p_{z},p_{z}^{\prime}}+\tfrac{A}{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{2}+p_{z}^{2}}\bigg);\;\;\;\;A=\tfrac{e^{2}\partial_{\mu}n^{\perp}}{L_{z}}{\\
}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\widetilde{\phi}_{ext\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}p_{z}}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{dielectricinhomogeneous3}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}\epsilon_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}p_{z},\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{\prime}p_{z}^{\prime}}=\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}p_{z}}+\tfrac{A}{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{2}+p_{z}^{2}}\sum_{p_{z}^{\prime}}\widetilde{\phi}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}p_{z}^{\prime}}.}

Integrating the above equation over
p_{z}
,

\displaystyle{\int\tfrac{dp_{z}}{2\pi}\tfrac{1}{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}^{2}+p_{z}^{2}}=\tfrac{1}{2|\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}|}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\widetilde{\phi}^{\perp}_{ext\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}=\sum_{p_{z}}\widetilde{\phi}_{ext\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}p_{z}}=\widetilde{\phi}^{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}\bigg(1+\tfrac{e^{2}\partial_{\mu}n^{\perp}}{2|\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}|}\bigg),}

(144)

which completes the proof of Eq. (
134
).

As an application, we consider the 3D potential of an electric dipole with an in-plane dipole moment (
|e|\mathscr{d}
):

\displaystyle{\phi_{ext\boldsymbol{r}}=&\;\tfrac{|e|^{2}\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}}{r^{3}}=-\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\nabla_{r}}\tfrac{|e|^{2}}{r}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\widetilde{\phi}_{ext\boldsymbol{p}}=-i\tfrac{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}}{{\cal V}}\tfrac{|e|^{2}}{\boldsymbol{p}^{2}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\widetilde{\phi}^{\perp}_{ext\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}}=\sum_{p_{z}}\widetilde{\phi}_{ext\boldsymbol{p}}=-i\tfrac{|e|^{2}}{2L_{x}L_{y}}\tfrac{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}}{|\boldsymbol{p}_{\perp}|};{\\
}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\widetilde{\phi}^{\perp}_{\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}=\tfrac{\widetilde{\phi}^{\perp}_{ext\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}}{1+k_{scr}/|\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}|}=-i\tfrac{|e|^{2}}{2{\cal V}}\tfrac{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}}{|\boldsymbol{q}_{\perp}|+k_{scr}},}

which forms the basis for Eqs. (
128
)-(
128
).

A.4
Kinetic model for the impurity-mediated ballistic photocurrent

This appendix elucidates and solves the kinetic model which underlies the impurity-mediated ballistic photocurrent. After introducing the electron distribution in App.
A.4.1
, the basic kinetic equation is set up in App.
A.4.2
. The ingredients of the kinetic equation are the collisional integrals due to photo-excitation [App.
A.4.3
], electron-impurity scattering [App.
A.4.4
], and electron-phonon scattering. The latter two scatterings relax the energy and momentum of the hot photo-excited electrons, as elaborated in App.
A.4.5
; therein, we also introduce a generalized harmonic approach to solving the eigenmodes of the impurity-mediated collisional integral. The generalized harmonics reduce to spherical harmonics in isotropic cases [App.
A.4.7
]. A linear algebra of generalized harmonics is formalized in App.
A.4.6
. Finally, the solution of the kinetic equation in App.
A.4.8
gives us the electron distribution on the excitation surface, which is an essential ingredient to calculate the ballistic photocurrent. We present two solutions: an exact one which accounts for the harmonic-dependent momentum relaxation of the generalized harmonics, as well as a simpler solution which employs the momentum-relaxation time approximation. The meaning and limitations of this approximation will be discussed.

A.4.1
Electron distributions

A key ingredient in the kinetic model, the electron distribution
f_{B}
can be viewed as a diagonal element of the reduced one-electron density matrix, in the band basis. It will prove useful to decompose the distribution into
B
-symmetric and
B
-asymmetric components:

\displaystyle{&\text{Symmetry-decomposed electron distribution:}\;\;\;\;f_{B}=f^{s}_{B}+\delta f^{a}_{B};{\\
}\text{$B$-symmetric}\;\;\;\;f^{s}_{B}=f^{s}_{-B};{\\
}\text{$B$-asymmetric}\;\;\;\;\delta f^{a}_{B}=-\delta f^{a}_{-B}.}

The meaning of
-B
is
(b,-\boldsymbol{k})
, which
can be thought of as the time-reversed partner of
B=(b,\boldsymbol{k})
; we are interested in bulk photovoltaic materials with broken centrosymmetry, so energy-eigenstates are energy-nondegenerate at generic
\boldsymbol{k}
, allowing us to choose our band indices such that time reversal just inverts
\boldsymbol{k}
while preserving
b
.

An important
B
-symmetric distribution is the

\displaystyle{\text{Fermi-Dirac distribution}\;\;\;\;f^{\text{FD}}_{B}=\frac{1}{e^{\beta(E_{B}-\mu)}+1}=f^{\text{FD}}_{-B},}

(145)

with
E_{B}=E_{-B}
guaranteed by time-reversal symmetry. The Fermi-Dirac distribution applies not just to materials in thermodynamic equilibrium, but also (in an approximate sense) to photo-excited materials in quasi-equilibrium.
74
In the latter case, the temperature
T
and ‘quasi-Fermi level’
\mu
depends on the source-driven light intensity.

The Fermi-Dirac distribution exemplifies an
iso-energy-symmetric
distribution, meaning it depends on
B
only through its dependence on
E_{B}
. Equivalently, an iso-energy-symmetric distribution equals its iso-energy average; more generally, for any function
Fun_{B}
of
B

\displaystyle{\text{Iso-energy-symmetric}\;\;\;\;Fun_{B}=[Fun_{B}]\;\;\;\;\text{iso-energy average}.}

(146)

The iso-energy average at
B
is defined as the average of
Fun_{B^{\prime}=(b^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})}
over the hypersurface (in
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
-space) defined by
E_{B^{\prime}}=E_{B}
:

\text{Iso-energy average}:\;\;\;\;[Fun_{B}]=[Fun]_{E_{B}}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}Fun_{B^{\prime}}\frac{\delta(E_{B^{\prime}}-E_{B})}{D_{E_{B}}},

(147)

with
D_{E}=\sum_{B}\delta(E_{B}-E)
the volume-extensive density of states per unit energy. For any function
Fun_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, we will refer to

\displaystyle{\delta Fun_{\boldsymbol{k}}=Fun_{\boldsymbol{k}}-[Fun_{\boldsymbol{k}}]\;\;\;\;\text{as an iso-energy deviant (noun)},}

(148)

or as being iso-energy-deviating (adjective).

A.4.2
Symmetric decomposition of the collisional integral

In the kinetic theory approach to bulk photovoltaics,
16
;
66
all electron quasiparticle transitions are modeled by the collision integral
I_{B}(f)
which is a functional of the non-equilibrium steady-state electron distribution
f_{B}
. Because
f_{B}
is time-independent, we set the collisional integral to zero:

\displaystyle{\text{Kinetic equation}:\;\;\;\;I_{B}(f)=I_{B}^{ex}(f)+I_{B}^{pn}(f)+I_{B}^{im}(f)=0.}

(149)

This integral is decomposed into three terms with superscripts
ex,pn,im
which respectively account for photo-
ex
citation (due to the electron’s interaction with a monochromatic light wave), electron-
p
hono
n
scattering, and electron-
im
purity scattering.
29
29

29

In the general case, we may consider a fourth term (
I_{B}^{re}
) that accounts for
radiative
electron-hole recombination.
66

I^{re}
is ignorable for topological semimetals, because electron-hole recombination is dominated by much-faster electron-phonon scattering and accounted for by
I^{pn}
. For (massive-Dirac) semiconductors,
|I_{B}^{re}|
is typically significant for
B
near a semiconducting band edge, where photo-excitation is absent and intraband collisions are substantially reduced owing to the formation of a quasi-thermal Boltzmann/Fermi-Dirac gas;
83
conversely,
|I_{B}^{re}|
is typically insignificant for
B
on the excitation surface, in comparison with the much faster electron-phonon/impurity scatterings. The application to the impurity-mediated ballistic photocurrent only requires that we solve the kinetic equation for
B
on the excitation surface.

The collisional integral and its components can be further split into
B
-symmetric and
B
-asymmetric
components
13

\displaystyle{&I^{x}_{B}=I_{B}^{x,a}+I_{B}^{x,s};\;\;\;\;I^{x,a}_{B}(f^{s})=-I^{x,a}_{-B}(f^{s});\;\;\;\;I^{x,s}_{B}(f^{s})=I^{x,s}_{-B}(f^{s});\;\;\;\;x=ex,pn,im,{\\
}I_{B}=I_{B}^{a}+I_{B}^{s};\;\;\;\;I_{B}^{a}=\sum_{x}I_{B}^{x,a};\;\;\;\;I_{B}^{s}=\sum_{x}I_{B}^{x,s},}

(150)

I^{s}_{B}(f)
exemplifies a
B
-symmetric functional of
f_{B}
, a notion that is distinct from (but overlaps with) a
B
-symmetric function of
B
[Eq. (
A.4.1
)]:
I^{s}_{B}(f)
can be viewed as a
B
-symmetric function only if the input
f
is
B
-symmetric [Eq. (
150
)], and otherwise,

\displaystyle{I^{a}_{B}(\delta f^{a})=I^{a}_{-B}(\delta f^{a});\;\;\;\;I^{s}_{B}(\delta f^{a})=-I^{s}_{-B}(\delta f^{a}).}

(151)

The significance of symmetrically decomposing the collisional integral is that without a
B
-asymmetric component
I^{a}_{B}
, a
B
-symmetric distribution
f^{s}_{B}
will remain
B
-symmetric for all times. Conversely, a
B
-asymmetric
\delta f^{a}_{B}
[Eq. (
A.4.1
)] can only be generated from an initially
B
-symmetric distribution if
I^{a}_{B}
is nontrivial. For instance, assuming that a non-magnetic material is in thermal equilibrium before photo-excitation, it is guaranteed that the pre-excitation distribution, being the Fermi-Dirac distribution
f^{\text{FD}}_{B}
[Eq. (
145
)], is
B
-symmetric and iso-energy-symmetric [Eq. (
146
)]. We will speak synonymously of the
B
-symmetric (resp.
B
-asymmetric) collisional integral as resulting in
symmetric (resp. skew) scattering
.

The precise forms of
I^{ex}
and
I^{im}
are respectively elaborated in App.
A.4.3
and App.
A.4.4
; the effect of the
B
-symmetric component of
I^{pn}+I^{im}
is to relax energy and momentum [App.
A.4.5
].

A.4.3
The photo-excitation collisional integral

\displaystyle{\text{The dipole coupling to a classical light wave:}\;\;\;\;V^{\text{ex}}=|e|\boldsymbol{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}\cdot\check{\boldsymbol{r}}}

(152)

is responsible for photo-excitations. Ignoring spontaneous emission, the
\boldsymbol{B}
-symmetric component of the photo-excitation collisional integral is, at lowest nontrivial order in
V^{\text{ex}}
,

\displaystyle{I^{ex,s}_{B}=&\;\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{ex}_{BB^{\prime}}f_{B^{\prime}B};\;\;\;\;W^{ex}_{BB^{\prime}}=W^{ex}_{B^{\prime}B}=\tfrac{2\pi}{\hbar}\sum_{B^{\prime}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|V^{\text{ex}}_{BB^{\prime}}|^{2}\bigg[\delta(E_{BB^{\prime}}-\hbar\omega)+\delta(E_{B^{\prime}B}-\hbar\omega)\bigg];\;\;\;\;f_{BB^{\prime}}=f_{B}-f_{B^{\prime}}.}

(153)

In a two-band model with band labels
c
(for conduction) and
v
(for valence), Eq. (
153
) simplifies to

I^{\text{ex},s}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}(f)=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}e^{2}(f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}})|\mathbf{E}|^{2}|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega)=-I^{\text{ex},s}_{v\boldsymbol{k}}(f).

(154)

The electric field (
\boldsymbol{E}
) induced by the light source is assumed to be sufficiently strong that spontaneous emission is negligible compared to induced emission.
83
The energy-conserving delta function in Eq. (
154
) confines collisions (with source-produced photons) to the excitation surface [Eq. (
32
)]. With the weaker laser intensities typical of continuous wave experiments, the non-equilibrium steady
f_{B}
is typically nondegenerate (meaning
f_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-f_{c\boldsymbol{k}}\approx 1
) on the excitation surface,

30
30

30

For semiconductors, this is justified experimentally
79
and theoretically.
83
For semimetals, we assume that
f_{B}
quasi-equilibrates with
k_{B}T
that is small compared to the one-particle excitation energies measured relative to the chemical potential, i.e.,
k_{B}T\ll|E_{c\boldsymbol{k}}-\mu|
and
\ll|E_{v\boldsymbol{k}}-\mu|
, with
E_{c/v,\boldsymbol{k}}
satisfying Eq. (
32
).

in which case Eq. (
154
) simplifies to:

\displaystyle{\text{Nondegenerate distribution}:\;\;\;\;I^{\text{ex},s}_{c\boldsymbol{k}}(f)\approx\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}e^{2}|\mathbf{E}|^{2}|\boldsymbol{e}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{cv,\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}\delta(E_{cv\boldsymbol{k}}-\hbar\omega).}

(155)

We will ignore the
B
-asymmetric
I^{ex,a}
which makes a different contribution to the ballistic photocurrent than the one we focus on.
13
;
15
;
25
;
24

A.4.4
The impurity-mediated collisional integral

Elastic collisions with impurities are generally described by a collisional integral which is linear in
f
, no matter the order of the Born approximation:
65

\displaystyle{\text{Impurity-mediated, elastic:}\;\;\;\;I^{im}_{B}(f)=\;\text{Incoming rate - Outgoing rate}\;=\sum_{B^{\prime}}\bigg(W^{im}_{BB^{\prime}}f_{B^{\prime}}-W^{im}_{B^{\prime}B}f_{B}\bigg).}

(156)

We refer to
W^{x}_{B,B^{\prime}}
as the
transition rate matrix element
for the transition
B\leftarrow B^{\prime}
mediated by
x
.
W^{im}
is derivable from Lippmann-Schwinger scattering theory; assuming incoherent scattering with
N_{\rm imp}
number of impurities, the transition rates add up as

W^{im}_{B,B^{\prime}}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}N_{\rm imp}|T_{B,B^{\prime}}|^{2}\delta{\left(E_{BB^{\prime}}\right)};\;\;\;\;E_{BB^{\prime}}=E_{B}-E_{B^{\prime}},

(157)

with
T_{B,B^{\prime}}
being the
T
matrix element. Assuming that the impurity is non-magnetic, time-reversal symmetry gives the

\displaystyle{\text{Reciprocity theorem:}\;\;\;\;|T_{B,B^{\prime}}|=|T_{-B^{\prime},-B}|\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;W^{im}_{B,B^{\prime}}=W^{im}_{-B^{\prime},-B}.}

(158)

Let us split the transition rate matrix element into components which are even and odd under the simultaneous inversion of both
B
and
B^{\prime}
:

\displaystyle{&W^{\text{im}}_{B,B^{\prime}}=W^{\text{im},s}_{B,B^{\prime}}+W^{\text{im},a}_{B,B^{\prime}};{\\
}(BB^{\prime})\text{-inversion-symmetric}\;\;\;\;W^{\text{im},s}_{B,B^{\prime}}=W^{\text{im},s}_{-B\leftarrow-B^{\prime}};{\\
}(BB^{\prime})\text{-inversion-asymmetric}\;\;\;\;W^{\text{im},a}_{B,B^{\prime}}=-W^{\text{im},a}_{-B\leftarrow-B^{\prime}}.}

(159)

Because
I^{\text{im}}
is linear in
W^{\text{im}}
[Eq. (
156
)], it splits into
B
-symmetric and
B
-asymmetric components [Eq. (
150
)] according to the decomposition of
W^{\text{im}}
[Eq. (
159
)]. An explicit expression for
W^{\text{im},s}
is attained expanding the T matrix in powers of the impurity potential energy
V
and retaining the lowest nontrivial order, this being second order:

\displaystyle W_{B,B^{\prime}}^{im,s}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}N_{\rm imp}\delta{\left(E_{BB^{\prime}}\right)}\left|V^{\text{im}}_{B,B^{\prime}}\right|^{2},

(160)

with
V^{\text{im}}_{B,B^{\prime}}
being the the impurity potential matrix element [Eq. (
122
)].
The lowest-order approximation of
W^{im,a}
is third order:
64

\displaystyle W_{B,B^{\prime}}^{im,a}=\frac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\hbar}N_{\rm imp}\delta{\left(E_{BB^{\prime}}\right)}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}{\text{Im}\;}{V^{\text{im}}_{B,B^{\prime\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{B^{\prime\prime},B^{\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{B^{\prime},B}}\delta{\left(E_{BB^{\prime\prime}}\right)}.

(161)

To motivate the form of Eq. (
161
),
the net amplitude (
T_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
) for a transition from
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}
(band index suppressed) is a Feynman sum of individual amplitudes
a_{j}
for the possible histories of the transition, including
a^{1}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}}
for the direct one-step scattering from
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{k}
,
a^{2}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}}
for a two-step scattering from
\mathbf{k}^{\prime}
to
\mathbf{k}^{\prime\prime}
and then to
\mathbf{k}
[illustrated in Fig.
10
(a)], and
a^{j\geq 3}
for higher-step scattering processes. The total transition rate
W^{im}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\propto|T_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}|^{2}
(via the standard T matrix theory) is proportional to
|a^{1}|^{2}
at lowest perturbative order, and
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}(a^{2}\overline{a^{1}}+\overline{a^{2}}a^{1})
to the next leading order. The third-order
a^{2}\overline{a^{1}}
can be interpreted as the quantum-interfered amplitude for two Feynman paths connecting
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
to
\boldsymbol{k}
, or it can be interpreted as self-interference of an electron making a loop
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
if we view
\overline{a^{1}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}}=a^{1}_{\boldsymbol{k}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
. Interchanging
\boldsymbol{k}\leftrightarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
in
\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}a^{1}_{\boldsymbol{k}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}a^{2}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{k}}
is equivalent to taking the complex conjugate, hence only the imaginary component of this sum contributes to skew scattering, which is the essence of Eq. (
161
). This is the lowest-order contribution to skew scattering because
|a^{1}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}}|^{2}
is manifestly symmetric under interchanging
\boldsymbol{k}\leftrightarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
. The truncation of Eq. (
161
) to third order (in the impurity potential term) applies to dilute/sparse concentrations of defects with weak impurity potentials.
31
31

31

With denser concentrations of strong potentials, so-called ‘diffractive’ and ‘Gaussian’ skew scatterings become relevant at fourth order in the potential.
40

Combining the reciprocity theorem [Eq. (
158
)] with the (
BB^{\prime}
)-inversion (a)symmetry [Eqs. (
159
)-(
159
)], one deduces that
W^{im,s}
(resp.
W^{im,a}
) is also symmetric (resp. antisymmetric) under interchange of
B
and
B^{\prime}

\displaystyle{&(BB^{\prime})\text{-interchange symmetry:}\;\;\;\;W^{\text{im},s}_{B,B^{\prime}}=W^{\text{im},s}_{B^{\prime},B}=\frac{1}{2}\big(W^{\text{im}}_{B,B^{\prime}}+W^{\text{im}}_{B^{\prime},B}\big);{\\
}(BB^{\prime})\text{-interchange asymmetry:}\;\;\;\;W^{\text{im},a}_{B,B^{\prime}}=-W^{\text{im},a}_{B^{\prime},B}=\frac{1}{2}\big({W^{\text{im}}_{B,B^{\prime}}-W^{\text{im}}_{B^{\prime},B}}\big).}

(162)

The interchange (a)symmetry allows to simplify our expressions for the impurity collisional integral:

\displaystyle I_{B}^{im,s}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{im,s}_{BB^{\prime}}(f_{B^{\prime}}-f_{B}),

(163)

\displaystyle I_{B}^{im,a}(f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{im,a}_{BB^{\prime}}(f_{B^{\prime}}+f_{B})=\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{im,a}_{BB^{\prime}}f_{B^{\prime}}.

(164)

The last step of Eq. (
164
) follows from a relation arising from unitarity of the scattering matrix:
32
32

32

This closely relates to the optical theorem of elastic scattering
27

.

\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{im}_{B^{\prime}B}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{im}_{BB^{\prime}},

(165)

and the trivial identity
\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{im,s}_{B^{\prime}B}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{im,s}_{BB^{\prime}}
,
which together imply

\displaystyle{\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{im,a}_{B^{\prime}B}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{im,a}_{BB^{\prime}}=0.}

(166)

The impurity collisional integral has a trivial action on iso-energy-symmetric distributions:
33
33

33

The first vanishing may be seen from
f_{B^{\prime}}-f_{B}=[f_{B^{\prime}}]-[f_{B}]=0
in Eq. (
163
) for elastic scattering; the second vanishing follows from Eq. (
164
) being proportional to
2[f_{B}]\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{a}_{BB^{\prime}}=0
, as per Eq. (
166
).

\displaystyle{I_{B}^{im,s}([f])=I_{B}^{im,a}([f])=0.}

(167)

A.4.5
Energy and momentum relaxation

Momentum relaxation (or ‘isotropization’
14
) is the smoothening of any iso-energy-asymmetries in the distribution; energy relaxation is the relaxation of one-electron energies toward a quasi-thermal distribution, or more precisely, it relaxes a generic, non-equilibrium, iso-energy-symmetric distribution to a specific, quasi-equilbrium iso-energy-symmetric distribution (
f^{\text{FD}}
), with a temperature and chemical potential (or ‘quasi-Fermi level’) that depends on the light wave intensity. Static impurities scatter electrons elastically and contribute to momentum relaxation but not energy relaxation; electron-phonon scattering contributes to both momentum and energy relaxation. As will be explained, only the
B
-symmetric component of the collisional integral leads to relaxation of energy/momentum. Altogether,

\displaystyle{&I^{pn,s}_{B}(f)+I^{im,s}_{B}(f)\approx I^{er}_{B}(f)+I^{mr}_{B}(f);{\\
}\text{{E}nergy {r}elaxation}:\;\;\;\;I^{er}_{B}(f)=\sum_{E^{\prime}}dN_{E^{\prime}}W_{EE^{\prime}}^{er}[f]_{E^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;W_{EE^{\prime}}^{er}=W_{E\leftarrow E^{\prime}}-\delta_{E,E^{\prime}}\sum_{E^{\prime\prime}}W_{E^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow E};{\\
}\text{{M}omentum {r}elaxation}:\;\;\;\;I^{mr}(f)=\sum_{B^{\prime}}W^{mr}_{BB^{\prime}}\delta f_{B^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;\delta f_{B^{\prime}}=f_{B^{\prime}}-[f_{B^{\prime}}]}

(168)

In Eq. (
168
), we have represented an energy integral as a discrete sum, with a newly introduced factor
dN_{E}
that encodes the density of states per unit energy, as elaborated in Eq. (
176
) below. Thus
\sum_{E^{\prime}}dN_{E^{\prime}}W_{E\leftarrow E^{\prime}}[f]_{E^{\prime}}
can be understood as the net transition rate of one-electron states from the energy interval
E^{\prime}+dE
to the energy interval
E+dE
. If the phonon energy is small compared to
k_{B}T
and any other energy scale for the variation of
[f]_{E}
, then the action of
W_{EE^{\prime}}^{er}
can be expressed as a differential operator on
[f]_{E}
in the Fokker-Planck approximation.
30
If the phonon energy is not small, as may be for optical phonons, then other models for
W_{EE^{\prime}}^{er}
are appropriate.
30
If we are interested only in the long-term distribution, at times
t\gg\tau^{E_{exc}}_{er}
(the
energy relaxation time
for electrons at the excitation energy), we may sacrifice accuracy in the transient dynamics by crudely adopting the

\displaystyle{\text{Energy relaxation time approximation (RTA):}\;\;\;\;I^{er}_{\boldsymbol{k}}([f])=-\frac{[f_{\boldsymbol{k}}]-f^{\text{FD}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\tau^{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}_{er}},}

(169)

with
f^{\text{FD}}
the quasi-equilibrium Fermi-Dirac function [Eq. (
145
)].

For disordered materials at low temperature, we make the simplifying assumption that momentum relaxation is dominated by impurities alone:

\displaystyle{I^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=I^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;{W}^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{Iims}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}.}

(170)

Because
I^{im,s}
has a trivial action on iso-energy-symmetric distributions [Eq. (
167
)],
I^{im,s}(f)=I^{im,s}(\delta f)
, in accordance with Eq. (
168
).

Now
W^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
is symmetric under interchanging
\boldsymbol{k}
with
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
, because both
W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
and
\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
are interchange symmetric [Eq. (
162
)]. If we view
\boldsymbol{k}
as a discrete wavevector, then
W^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
is a real, symmetric, negative-trace matrix. Being ‘real, symmetric’ allows to express
W^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
as a sum over real eigenmodes with real eigenvalues. These eigenvalues are guaranteed to be non-positive by the Gershgorin circle theorem.
34
34

34

By the Gershgorin theorem, every eigenvalue of
W^{mr}
lies within at least one Gershgorin disc associated with a certain
\boldsymbol{k}
, which is centered at
W^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}}
and has radius
R_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\neq\boldsymbol{k}}W^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
in the complex plane. Since
W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\geqslant 0
and
W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}=W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
, we have

W^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}}=-\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\neq\boldsymbol{k}}W^{im,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv-R_{\boldsymbol{k}},

Therefore, for any eigenvalue
\lambda
of
W^{mr}
, there exists a momentum
\boldsymbol{k}
such that

\left|\lambda+R_{\boldsymbol{k}}\right|\leqslant R_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Rightarrow-2R_{\boldsymbol{k}}\leqslant\lambda\leqslant 0,

where
\Rightarrow
used the fact that
\lambda
is a real number because
W^{mr}
is a normal matrix.

To reformulate a previous observation, any iso-energy-symmetric distribution is an eigenmode in the kernel of
W^{mr}
; it follows that any eigenmode with nonzero (and necessarily negative) eigenvalue must be an iso-energy-deviant [Eq. (
148
)], leading to

\displaystyle{\text{Multi-harmonic momentum relaxation:}\;\;\;\;W^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=-\sum_{hE}\frac{h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{\tau_{hE}^{mr}}.}

(171)

As indexed by
(hE)
, each eigenmode is a real function of
\boldsymbol{k}
(denoted
h_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{E}
) which (i) is nonzero only if
E=E_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, and (ii) has zero iso-energy average:
[h_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{E}]=0
, just as
[\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=0
.
We further presume that the set of all
(hE)
-eigenmodes form a

\displaystyle{\text{Complete basis for iso-energy deviant functions:}\;\;\;\;\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{hE}{h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}.}

(172)

We will refer to
\{h_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{E}\}
as
generalized harmonics
, because they reduce to real spherical harmonics in isotropic cases, as elaborated in App.
A.4.7
. The meaning of Eq. (
171
) is that impurity-mediated collisions diminish each harmonic by a factor
1/e
in time
\tau^{mr}_{hE}
, which is harmonic-dependent. Because
\tau^{mr}_{hE}\geq 0
, the harmonics do not grow in time, i.e., there is no momentum excitation, in accordance with the H theorem (of non-decreasing entropy) proven for elastic scattering in Ref.
65
.

In a given application, it may be that only a few harmonics are generated by a driving force (e.g., light source), and for these few harmonics, their relaxation times may not be very different, in which case
Eq. (
171
) reduces to

\displaystyle{\text{Momentum relaxation time approximation (RTA):}\;\;\;\;I^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(f)\approx-\frac{\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\tau_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}^{mr}},}

(173)

with the
momentum relaxation time
(
\tau_{E}^{mr}
) being some weighted average of
\tau_{hE}^{mr}
over the relevant harmonics at fixed energy
E
. Warning: ‘momentum relaxation time’ may be defined differently in other literature, e.g., for transport calculations in Ref.
47
.

A.4.6
Interlude: Braket formulation of generalized harmonics

To clarify the linear algebraic structure of the space of generalized harmonics, we introduce a braket notation with a symmetric inner product:
\langle a\,|\,b\rangle=\langle b\,|\,a\rangle
, and the resolution of identity for real, smooth functions of
\boldsymbol{k}
:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{1}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}|\,\boldsymbol{k}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{k}\,|=\sum_{E}\bigg\{|\,oE\rangle\langle oE\,|+\sum_{h}|\,hE\rangle\langle hE\,|\bigg\},}

(174)

with each ket an orthonormal basis vector:

\displaystyle{\langle\boldsymbol{k}\,|\,\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;\langle oE\,|\,oE^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{EE^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;\langle hE\,|\,h^{\prime}E^{\prime}\rangle=\delta_{h,h^{\prime}}\delta_{E,E^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;\langle hE\,|\,oE\rangle=0.}

(175)

\sum_{E}
can be viewed as a discrete sum if we split
\boldsymbol{k}
-space into infinitesimally-separated iso-energy contours:
\ldots,E-dE/2,E+dE/2,E+3dE/2,\ldots
; our ‘smooth’ functions are assumed to vary negligibly over
dE/|\boldsymbol{\nabla_{k}}E|
. The number of
\boldsymbol{k}
-points within one
iso-energy band

[E-dE/2,E+dE/2)
sandwiched by two neighboring contours is

\displaystyle{dN_{E}=D_{E}dE=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}},E},\;\;\;\;D_{E}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E-E_{\boldsymbol{k}}),}

(176)

with
D_{E}
the volume-extensive density of states per unit energy.
The
energy Kronecker delta function
in Eq. (
175
) should be understood as a projector to
\boldsymbol{k}
-points within an iso-energy band:

\displaystyle{\delta_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}},E}=1\;\;\;\;\text{if and only if}\;\;\;\;E_{\boldsymbol{k}}\in[E-dE/2,E+dE/2).}

(177)

This allows us to view discrete sums and continuous integrals interchangeably:

\displaystyle{\frac{\delta_{EE^{\prime}}}{dN_{E}}=\frac{\delta(E-E^{\prime})}{D_{E}};\;\;\;\;\sum_{E}dN_{E}=\int dE\,D_{E}.}

(178)

The quantity in the curly brackets of Eq. (
174
) is the resolution of identity for real functions that have support (i.e., are nonzero) only in the iso-energy band
[E-dE/2,E+dE/2)
. In particular,
|\,oE\,\rangle
is the
breathing mode
which is constant within
[E-dE/2,E+dE/2)
but is zero everywhere else:

\displaystyle{o_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{E}=\langle\boldsymbol{k}\,|\,oE\rangle=\frac{\delta_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}E}}{\sqrt{dN_{E}}};\;\;\;\;1=\langle oE\,|\,oE\rangle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}(o_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{E})^{2},}

(179)

As motivated in Eq. (
171
),
|\,hE\,\rangle
is both an eigenmode of the operator
\widehat{W^{mr}}
and a basis vector for the space of real-valued, smooth, iso-energy-deviating functions [Eq. (
148
)];

|\,hE\,\rangle
relates to the previously defined amplitudes
h_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{E}
as

\displaystyle{\langle\boldsymbol{k}\,|\,hE\rangle=h_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{E};\;\;\;\;1=\langle hE\,|\,hE\rangle=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}(h_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{E})^{2}.}

(180)

Just as we split
f_{\boldsymbol{k}}=[f_{\boldsymbol{k}}]+\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, we may view
Fun_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\langle\boldsymbol{k}\,|\,Fun\rangle
and split
|\,Fun\,\rangle=|\,[Fun]\,\rangle+|\,\delta Fun\,\rangle
into iso-energy-symmetric and iso-energy deviating components with corresponding projectors
\hat{P}_{[]}
and
\hat{P}_{\delta}
:

\displaystyle{|\,[Fun]\,\rangle=&\;\hat{P}_{[]}|\,Fun\,\rangle;\;\;\;\;\hat{P}_{[]}=\sum_{E}|\,oE\,\rangle\langle\,oE\,|;\;\;\;\;\langle oE\,|\,Fun\rangle=\sqrt{dN_{E}}[Fun]_{E};\;\;\;\;\langle hE\,|\,[Fun]\rangle=0;{\\
}|\,\delta Fun\,\rangle=\;\hat{P}_{\delta}|\,Fun\,\rangle;\;\;\;\;\hat{P}_{\delta}=\sum_{hE}{|\,hE\rangle\langle hE\,|};\;\;\;\;\langle oE\,|\,\delta Fun\rangle=0.}

(181)

In particular, any odd function is an iso-energy deviant, because of the time-reversal constraint
(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{-\boldsymbol{k}})
on iso-energy contours:

\displaystyle{\langle\boldsymbol{k}\,|\,Fun\rangle=-\langle-\boldsymbol{k}\,|\,Fun\rangle\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\langle oE\,|\,Fun\rangle=0.}

(182)

Iso-energy averages [Eq. (
147
)] can be equivalently expressed in terms of the energy Kronecker function:

\text{Iso-energy average}:\;\;\;\;[Fun_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=[Fun]_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}Fun_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\frac{\delta_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}}{dN_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}},

(183)

which implies for the functional derivatives:

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial f_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\partial f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;\frac{\partial[f_{\boldsymbol{k}}]}{\partial f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}=\frac{\delta_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}}{dN_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}}=\sum_{E}\frac{\delta_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}E}}{\sqrt{dN_{E}}}\frac{\delta_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}E}}{\sqrt{dN_{E}}}=\sum_{E}\langle\boldsymbol{k}\,|\,oE\rangle\langle oE\,|\,\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\rangle.}

(184)

Integration over
\boldsymbol{k}
can be equivalently expressed in terms of inner products with breathing modes:

\displaystyle{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}\langle\boldsymbol{k}\,|\,Fun\rangle=\sum_{E}dN_{E}[Fun]_{E}=\sum_{E}\sqrt{dN_{E}}\langle oE\,|\,Fun\rangle.}

(185)

Let us apply this equivalence to Eq. (
166
), which we can rewrite as

\displaystyle{\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}W^{im,a}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}}\delta_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}=0=\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}W^{im,a}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\langle\,oE\,|\widehat{W^{im,a}}=0=\widehat{W^{im,a}}|\,oE\,\rangle,}

(186)

because impurity-mediated scattering is elastic.
35
35

35

Because of this elasticity, it may be seen that (i)
\langle\,oE\,|\widehat{W^{im,a}}|\,\boldsymbol{k}\,\rangle=0
if
E\neq E_{\boldsymbol{k}}
. Conversely, integration over
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
in Eq. (
166
) gives

\displaystyle{0=\sum_{E}\sqrt{dN_{E}}\langle\,oE\,|\widehat{W^{im,a}}|\,\boldsymbol{k}\,\rangle\delta_{EE_{\boldsymbol{k}}}=\sqrt{dN_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}}\langle\,oE_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\widehat{W^{im,a}}|\,\boldsymbol{k}\,\rangle.}

(187)

Combining (i) and (ii) gives

\displaystyle{\langle\,oE\,|\widehat{W^{im,a}}|\,\boldsymbol{k}\,\rangle=0,\;\;\;\;\text{for all}\;E,\boldsymbol{k}.}

(188)

This further implies that the impurity-mediated skew-scattering only acts nontrivially on iso-energy deviant functions:

\displaystyle{\hat{P}_{[]}\widehat{W^{im,a}}=0=\widehat{W^{im,a}}\hat{P}_{[]}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\widehat{W^{im,a}}=\hat{P}_{\delta}\widehat{W^{im,a}}\hat{P}_{\delta}.}

(189)

The functional derivatives of the collisional integrals for energy [Eq. (
169
)] and momentum relaxation [Eq. (
171
)] can be viewed as linear operators:

\displaystyle{\frac{\partial I^{er}_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\partial f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}=&\;\langle\,\boldsymbol{k}\,|\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{er}}|\,\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\,\rangle;\;\;\;\;\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{er}}=-\sum_{E}\frac{|\,oE\rangle\langle oE\,|}{\tau_{er}^{E}};{\\
}\frac{\partial I^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}}}{\partial f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}=\;\langle\,\boldsymbol{k}\,|\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{mr}}|\,\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\,\rangle;\;\;\;\;\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{mr}}=\widehat{W^{mr}}=-\sum_{E,h}\frac{|\,hE\rangle\langle hE\,|}{\tau^{mr}_{hE}};}

(190)

with help from Eq. (
180
) and Eq. (
184
). Assuming none of
\{\tau^{mr}_{hE}\}
is zero or infinite, we can define an inverse operator in the restricted space of iso-energy deviants:

\displaystyle{\widehat{W^{mr}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}=\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{mr}}^{-1}=-\sum_{hE}\tau^{mr}_{hE}{|\,hE\rangle\langle hE\,|};\;\;\;\;\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{mr}}^{-1}\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{mr}}=\hat{P}_{\delta},}

(191)

with
\hat{P}_{\delta}
being the deviant projector [Eq. (
181
)]. (Warning:
\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{mr}}^{-1}
is not the inverse operator in the full space of real-valued functions of
\boldsymbol{k}
.) Thus if the inverse is applied to the momentum-relaxing collisional integral [Eq. (
171
)],

\displaystyle{\widehat{W^{mr}}^{-1}|\,I^{mr}(f^{s})\,\rangle=\widehat{W^{mr}}^{-1}\widehat{W^{mr}}|\,f^{s}\,\rangle=|\,\delta f^{s}\,\rangle.}

(192)

A.4.7
Isotropic eigensolution of the monopolar-impurity-mediated collisional integral

To develop intuition for the generalized harmonics, we consider an isotropic model in which the eigenmodes of the momentum-relaxation collision integral (
I^{mr}
) can be obtained analytically and are given by the real spherical harmonics.

Specifically, we consider the Kramers–Weyl fermion described by

H_{KW}=\hbar v\mathbf{k}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma},

(193)

subject to an overscreened monopole impurity potential, of which the matrix element in the Bloch basis is given by

V_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon}\frac{\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle}{k_{\text{scr}}^{2}}.

(194)

In the following, we show that the momentum-relaxation transition-rate matrix

W_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{mr}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}N_{\text{imp}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon k_{\text{scr}}^{2}}\right)^{2}\left(|\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle|^{2}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})-\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}|\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle|^{2}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}})\right)

(195)

can be decomposed into the harmonic form of Eq. (
171
),

\displaystyle\boxed{W_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{mr}=-\sum_{E,h}\frac{h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{\tau^{mr}_{hE}},\quad\text{with}\ h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\sqrt{4\pi}\frac{\delta_{E,E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}}{\sqrt{dN_{E}}}Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}}),\ \frac{1}{\tau^{mr}_{hE}}=a_{l}\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\frac{n_{\text{imp}}}{k_{\text{scr}}^{4}}\left(\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\rho_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}.}

(196)

Here,
Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}})
denotes the real spherical harmonics and
h=(l,m)
. Furthermore,
n_{\text{imp}}=N_{\text{imp}}/\mathcal{V}
is the impurity density,
\rho_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}=E^{2}_{\boldsymbol{k}}/(2\pi^{2}(\hbar v_{F})^{3})
is the density of states, and the coefficients are given by

a_{0}=0,\quad a_{1}=2/3,\quad a_{l\geqslant 2}=1.

(197)

The vanishing of
a_{0}
reflects particle-number conservation within each energy shell under elastic scattering. Finally,
\delta_{E,E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}
and
dN_{E}
are defined through Eqs. (
176
) and (
178
).

To rewrite Eq. (
195
) in the form of Eq. (
196
), we first note that for the Kramers–Weyl fermion described by
H_{KW}
, the Bloch-state overlap factor
|\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle|^{2}
depends only on the angle between
\boldsymbol{k}
and
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
, denoted by
\gamma_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
. Explicitly,

|\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1+\cos\gamma_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{2},\quad\cos\gamma_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\cos\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cos\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}+\sin\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}}\sin\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\cos(\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}),

(198)

where
(\theta_{\boldsymbol{k}},\varphi_{\boldsymbol{k}})
is the spherical angle of
\boldsymbol{k}
.
Using this result, the second term of
W_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{mr}
can be evaluated as

\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}|\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle|^{2}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{V}\int\frac{k^{\prime\prime 2}dk^{\prime\prime}d\Omega^{\prime\prime}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1+\cos\theta}{2}\frac{\delta(k-k^{\prime\prime})}{\hbar v}=\frac{\mathcal{V}k^{2}}{(2\pi)^{2}\hbar v}.

(199)

Furthermore, the Kronecker delta can be written as

\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\frac{(2\pi)^{3}}{\mathcal{V}}\delta^{(3)}(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}),

(200)

which implies

\frac{\mathcal{V}}{(2\pi)^{2}}\frac{k^{2}\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{\hbar v}=2\pi\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\delta^{(2)}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}).

(201)

Substituting these results into the expression for
W_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{mr}
yields

W_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{mr}=\frac{2\pi}{\hbar}\frac{N_{\text{imp}}}{k_{\text{scr}}^{4}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\left(\frac{1+\cos\gamma_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{2}-2\pi\delta^{(2)}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}}-\Omega^{\prime}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\right).

(202)

Since Eq. (
202
) depends on the spherical solid angles of
\boldsymbol{k}
and
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}
, a harmonic expansion of it should naturally involve the spherical harmonics [c.f. Eq. (
196
)]. According to the identity equation and the orthonormality of spherical harmonics:

\displaystyle\sum_{m=-l}^{m=l}Y_{lm}(\Omega)Y_{lm}(\Omega^{\prime})=\frac{2l+1}{4\pi}P_{l}(\cos\gamma),\ \sum_{l}\frac{2l+1}{4\pi}P_{l}(\cos\gamma)=\delta^{(2)}(\Omega-\Omega^{\prime})=\delta(\phi-\phi^{\prime})\delta(\cos\theta-\cos\theta^{\prime}),

(203)

\displaystyle\int d\Omega Y_{lm}(\Omega)Y_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}(\Omega)=\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}},

where
P_{l}(\cos\gamma)
is the Legendre polynomials,
it is straightforward to show that
h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
defined in Eq. (
196
) satisfy:

\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}h^{\prime E^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\delta_{h,h^{\prime}}\delta_{E,E^{\prime}},\quad\sum_{hE}h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\delta_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}

(204)

Note that

1+\cos\gamma_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=P_{0}(\cos\gamma)+P_{1}(\cos\gamma)=\sum_{l=0}^{1}\frac{4\pi}{2l+1}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}})Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}).

(205)

It is straightforward to rewrite
W_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{mr}
in Eq. (
202
) as

\displaystyle W_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{mr}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\frac{N_{\text{imp}}}{k_{\text{scr}}^{4}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\left(\sum_{l=0}^{1}\frac{4\pi}{2l+1}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}})Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})-4\pi\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}})Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\right)

(206)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\frac{N_{\text{imp}}}{k_{\text{scr}}^{4}}\left(\frac{1}{\mathcal{V}}\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)^{2}D_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\sum_{E}\frac{\delta_{E,E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\delta_{E,\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}}{dN_{E}}\sum_{l=0}^{+\infty}a_{l}\sum_{m=-l}^{l}4\pi Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}})Y_{lm}(\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=-\sum_{E,h}\frac{\pi}{\hbar}\frac{n_{\text{imp}}}{k_{\text{scr}}^{4}}\left(\frac{e^{2}}{\epsilon}\right)^{2}\rho_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}a_{l}h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}}h^{E}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}},

where

a_{l}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}2l/(2l+1)\quad\text{for}\ l=0,1\\
1\quad\text{otherwise}\end{array}\right.

(207)

A.4.8
Solution of kinetic model

Let us derive the
B
-asymmetric electron distribution that solves the kinetic equation in Eq. (
149
).

As a rule of thumb,
36
36

36

The first inequality can be justified by perturbation theory: for the linear photogalvanic effect,
I^{x,a}
is always higher-order in interaction couplings than
I^{x,s}
,
13
as elaborated for the impurity-mediated collisions in App.
A.4.4
.

the effect of skew scattering is weaker than of symmetric scattering for each scattering mechanism:

\displaystyle{\text{Rule of thumb:}\;\;\;\;[|I^{x,a}_{B}|]\ll[|I^{x,s}_{B}|];\;\;\;\;\text{steady}\;f_{B}=f_{B}^{s}+\delta f_{B}^{a};\;\;\;\;[|\delta f^{a}|]\ll[|f^{s}|].}

(208)

Because the initial distribution before photo-excitation is a thermal and symmetric [Eq. (
145
)], any
B
-asymmetric distribution
\delta f^{a}
must be dynamically generated by skew scattering [cf. second equality in Eq. (
150
)]; the smallness of
I^{a}
relative to
I^{s}
then guarantees that the steady distribution is dominated by its symmetric component [cf. last inequality in Eq. (
208
)].

The smallness of
I^{a}
relative to
I^{s}
[Eq. (
208
)] then guarantees that the steady distribution is dominated by its symmetric component, i.e.,
[|\delta f^{a}|]\ll[f^{s}]
, because
\delta f^{a}
is dynamically generated by skew scattering (
I^{im,a}_{B}
), as discussed in App.
A.4.2
. This leads to an ansatz: we first solve Eq. (
149
) for the symmetric
f^{s}
, which then determines the
B
-asymmetric
\delta f^{a}
by:

\displaystyle{\text{Ansatz}:\;\;\;\;I^{s}_{B}(f^{s})=0;\;\;\;\;\sum_{B^{\prime}}\frac{\partial I_{B}^{s}}{\partial f_{B^{\prime}}}\bigg|_{f^{s}}\delta f_{B^{\prime}}^{a}+I_{B}^{a}(f^{s})=0.}

(209)

The term proportional to
\delta f_{B^{\prime}}^{a}
in the second equation should be understood as the first-order term in a functional Taylor expansion, i.e.,
\partial I_{B}^{s}/\partial f_{B^{\prime}}|_{f^{s}}
is a functional derivative
32
evaluated at
f^{s}
that solves
I_{B}^{s}(f^{s})=0
. Eq. (
209
) corrects an error in Ref.
66
.

Combining our results in Eqs. (
168
)-(
171
), our ansatz equations in Eq. (
209
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle{0=&\;I^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(f^{s})=I^{ex,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(f^{s})+I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{er}([f])+I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{mr}(f^{s});{\\
}0=\;I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{a}(f^{s})+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{f}I^{s}\big)_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{a},}

(210)

with the band index
c
(for conduction) implicit in the above equations.
The functional derivative
\partial_{f}I^{s}
is evaluated at
f^{s}
which solves Eq. (
210
). The first ansatz equation [Eq. (
210
)] can be iso-energy averaged to give

\displaystyle{0=&\;[I^{ex,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(f^{s})]+I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{er}([f])+[I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{mr}(f^{s})],}

(211)

bearing in mind the averaging procedure does nothing to the energy-relaxing collisional integral, which is already iso-energy-symmetric [Eq. (
168
)]. The last term in Eq. (
211
) is now demonstrated to vanish, according to Eq. (
181
) and Eq. (
190
):

\displaystyle{\sqrt{dN_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}}[I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{mr}(f^{s})]=\langle oE_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,I^{mr}(f^{s})\rangle=-\sum_{hE}\frac{\langle oE_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,hE\rangle\langle\,hE\,|}{\tau^{mr}_{hE}}=0\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;[I^{ex,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(f^{s})]=-I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{er}([f]).}

(212)

Plugging this into the first ansatz equation [Eq. (
210
)] gives

\displaystyle{0=\delta I^{ex,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}(f^{s})+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}W^{mr}_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}f^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\delta I^{ex,s}=I^{ex,s}-[I^{ex,s}].}

(213)

The functional derivative
\partial_{f}I^{s}
in the second ansatz equation [Eq. (
210
)] is contributed by
I^{ex,s},I^{er}
, and
I^{mr}
, according to Eq. (
210
).
37
37

37

Don’t be misled by Eq. (
155
); one should directly differentiate Eq. (
154
).
Except for extremely intense laser pulses (with electric field strengths comparable to or greater than
10^{9}\,V/m
), the time scale for collisions with the source-produced photons is much larger than the momentum-relaxation time (
\tau_{mr}
), which is typically 100fs or less. Conversely, to describe continuous-wave laser or solar radiation experiments, it is typically safe to neglect the excitation contribution to

\displaystyle{\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{s}}|\,\delta f^{a}\,\rangle\approx&\;\big(\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{mr}}+\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{er}}\big)|\,\delta f^{a}\,\rangle=\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{mr}}|\,\delta f^{a}\,\rangle.}

(214)

In the last step, we applied that
|\,\delta f^{a}\,\rangle
is in the kernel of
\widehat{\partial_{f}I^{er}}
, in accordance with Eq. (
190
), so that our second ansatz equation [Eq. (
210
)] simplifies to

\displaystyle{0\approx I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{a}(f^{s})+\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\big(\partial_{f}I^{mr}\big)_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}^{a};\;\;\;\;\partial_{f}I^{mr}=W^{mr}.}

(215)

By applying
(W^{mr})^{-1}
[Eq. (
191
)] to both ansatz equations [Eq. (
213
) and Eq. (
215
)], we derive

\displaystyle{&B\text{-symmetric, iso-energy-deviating}\;\;\;\;\delta f^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\equiv\;f^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}-[f_{\boldsymbol{k}}]=-\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}\big(W^{mr}\big)^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}\delta I^{ex,s}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}(f^{s});{\\
}B\text{-asymmetric}\;\;\;\;\delta f^{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=-\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}\big(W^{mr}\big)^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}I^{a}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}\big(\,[f]+\delta f^{s}\,\big).}

(216)

In principle,
I^{a}=\sum_{x}I^{x,a}
is contributed by each scattering mechanism (
x=ex,pn,im,re
); in practice, we focus in this work on electron-impurity scattering (
x=im
). Eq. (
216
) is then recast as

\displaystyle{\delta f^{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}&\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{impvanish}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;-\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}\big(W^{mr}\big)^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}I^{im,a}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}(\delta f^{s})\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{Iima}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;-\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\big(W^{mr}\big)^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}\big(W^{im,a}\big)_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime\prime}}\delta f^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}{\\
}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{deltafs}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime\prime}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\big(W^{mr}\big)^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}\big(W^{im,a}\big)_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime\prime}}\big(W^{mr}\big)^{-1}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime\prime}{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}\delta I^{ex,s}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}(f^{s}).}

Assuming the nonequilibrium electron distribution is nondegenerate [Eq. (
155
)],
\delta I^{ex,s}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime\prime\prime}}
does not depend on
f^{s}
.

On one hand, to motivate the form of Eq. (
216
), an optical vortex intersecting (or proximate) to the excitation surface makes the photo-excitation rate
I^{ex,s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
anisotropic and results in a nonzero iso-energy deviant:
\delta I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{ex,s}\equiv I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{ex,s}-[I_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{ex,s}]\neq 0
. A deviating photo-excitation rate tends to create a deviating electron distribution
\delta f_{\boldsymbol{k}}^{s}
, but this is counterbalanced by momentum-relaxing transitions; namely, symmetric, impurity-mediated scattering (encoded in
W^{mr}
) tends to relax the distribution to being iso-energy-symmetric. Eq. (
216
) describes how this counterbalancing determines the steady-state deviant
\delta f^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
.

On the other hand, to motivate the form of Eq. (
A.4.8
), a deviating
\delta f^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
activates the impurity-mediated skew scattering (encoded in
W^{im,a}
). Such skew scattering tends to create an inversion-asymmetric deviant
\delta f^{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, but this is counterbalanced by the same symmetric, momentum-relaxing transitions encoded in
W^{mr}
. Eq. (
A.4.8
) describes how this counterbalancing determines the steady-state
\delta f^{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
.

Bringing both hands together, Eqs. (
216
)-(
A.4.8
) show how
\delta f^{a}
is enhanced by the intersection/proximity of optical vortices. This implies that the ballistic photocurrent, which is a linear functional of
\delta f^{a}
[Eq. (
30
)], is also vortex-enhanced.

In the momentum RTA [Eq. (
173
)], Eqs. (
216
)-(
A.4.8
) reduce to
38
38

38

To see this reduction, first note that Eq. (
191
) reduces to
(W^{mr})^{-1}\approx-\tau^{mr}_{E}\hat{P}_{\delta}
, which is simply proportional to the deviant projector. The deviant projector acts trivially on
\delta I^{ex,s}
, which is manifestly iso-energy deviant. The deviant projector also acts trivially on
W^{im,a}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}
, according to Eq. (
189
).

\displaystyle{\text{Momentum RTA:}\;\;\;\;\delta f^{s}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\approx&\;\tau^{mr}_{E_{\boldsymbol{k}}}\delta I^{ex,s}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}}(f^{s});\;\;\;\;\delta f^{a}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\approx(\tau^{mr}_{E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}})^{2}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}W^{im,a}_{{\boldsymbol{k}},{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}\delta I^{ex,s}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}^{\prime}}(f^{s}),}

(217)

bearing in mind that the energy-conserving delta functions [implicitly within
W^{im,a}
and
\delta I^{ex,s}
] restrict
E_{\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}
(the excitation energy).
The first
\approx
states that
\delta f^{s}
is just proportional to the iso-energy asymmetry of the excitation process; in contrast, the more general expression for
\delta f^{s}
in Eq. (
216
) shows that an iso-energy asymmetry of
f
can also arise from an iso-energy asymmetry of the momentum-relaxation process; this arising is artificially precluded in the momentum RTA. Thus, Eq. (
217
) should be understood as an over-simplified estimate; while it offers a useful physical insight, the insight is gained at the cost of narrowing our viewport. If greater quantitative accuracy is desired, one can just calculate Eq. (
A.4.8
) with the harmonic-dependent momentum-relaxation times.

A.5
Impurity-mediated ballistic photocurrent

Eq. (
38
) for the impurity-mediated ballistic photocurrent forms the basis for most of our model calculations in Sec.
III
. The goal is to derive not just Eq. (
38
) but also a more general formula [Eq. (
219
)] that applies beyond the momentum relaxation time approximation (RTA) [Eq. (
217
)] implicitly used in Eq. (
38
).
