are allowed.

Appendix D
Appendix to ‘Symmetry theorems for bulk photovoltaic response tensors’

The main results of this appendix are derivations of the symmetry theorems for bulk photovoltaic response tensors in Sec.
IV
[specifically the general symmetry theorems in Sec.
IV.1
and the three Hamiltonian-specific theorems in Sec.
IV.2
], as well as their applications to Dirac-Weyl fermions in Tab.
11
,
12
and
13
.

Before getting into the meat of the aforementioned derivations, a substantial digression is needed to fundamentally establish:

(App.
D.1
) What it means for a response tensor to be point-group-symmetric (with emphasis on the bulk photovoltaic tensor),

(App.
D.2
) How to view a response tensor as a calculational algorithm that inputs the one-electron distribution, Hamiltonian and collisional integral,

(App.
D.3
) How the aforementioned collisional integral transforms under point-group symmetry,

(App.
D.4
) What is a symmetry compatibility index of a response tensor.

Then we are finally ready to

(App.
D.5
) Derive the general symmetry theorems on photovoltaic tensors,

(App.
D.6
) Apply the newly-derived theorems to
to determine the symmetry-allowed photovoltaic response tensor elements, by viewing a
G
-symmetric response tensor as a
G
-equivariant linear map and applying Schur’s lemma,

(App.
D.7
) Derive the Hamiltonian-specific symmetry theorems on photovoltaic tensors,

(App.
D.8
) Apply all the preceding theorems to determine the symmetry-allowed photovoltaic tensor elements of
n
-Weyl fermions,

(App.
D.9
) Apply all the preceding theorems to determine the symmetry-allowed photovoltaic tensor elements of
n
-Dirac fermions.

D.1
What it means for a response tensor to be symmetric

While experimenting on a crystal, if a mischievous devil were to momentarily cover your eyes and to displace/rotate/reflect your spacetime rulers relative to the crystal, you wouldn’t be able to tell the difference when your eyes are open. The devil can move the rulers and fix the crystal (intervention I), or move the crystal and fix the rulers (intervention II). If, before the devil’s intervention, an electric field
{\cal E}
in the
y
direction induced a current density
\boldsymbol{j}
in the
x
direction, then you would go on believing that
{\cal E}_{y}
induces
j_{x}
, though either your notion of direction has changed (I) or your crystallographic axes have changed (II).

Symmetry can be formulated equivalently with either intervention, modulo some changes in interpretation. This Appendix chooses to formulate symmetry based on interventions of the second kind, which we refer to as
active transformations
. What exactly is an active transformation? Let
s^{e}
denote a reference closed system and
s^{g}
denote a
g
-active-transformed closed system;
e
is the identity transformation, so the reference system can be viewed as being trivially transformed. In the bulk photovoltaic context, a ‘closed system’ is the sum of subsystems: (i) the electrons (ii) a driving agent/apparatus that produces static/dynamic electric fields, and (iii) the environment (e.g., impurities,
bath of phonons and/or photons) which plays the role of a source/sink of energy or momentum or both.

We are interested in observables which are fields over spacetime, which we parametrize by the coordinate
c=(\boldsymbol{r},t)
. For instance, let
\rho^{g^{\prime}}
mean the
g^{\prime}
-active-transformed charge density associated to
s^{g^{\prime}}
.
\rho
exemplifies a

\displaystyle{\text{Time-neutral scalar field:}\;\;\;\;\rho^{g}(c)=\rho^{e}(g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\circ c);\;\;\;\;c\equiv(\boldsymbol{r},t),}

(333)

with the action
g\circ
defined in Eq. (
90
). Other types of fields transform differently:

\displaystyle{&\text{Time-oriented scalar field:}\;\;\;\;\partial_{t}\rho^{g}(c)=i_{g}\partial_{t}\rho^{e}(g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\circ c);{\\
}\text{Time-neutral vector field}:\;\;\;\;{\cal E}^{g}(c)=g^{s}\cdot{\cal E}^{e}(g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\circ c);{\\
}\text{Time-oriented vector field}:\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{j}^{g}(c)=i_{g}g^{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}^{e}(g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\circ c);{\\
}\text{Time-oriented pseudovector field}:\;\;\;\;\text{magnetic field}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{B}^{g}(c)=i_{g}\det\{g^{s}\}g^{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{B}^{e}(g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\circ c).}

(334)

All the above fields can be Fourier transformed as

\displaystyle{A(\boldsymbol{r}t)=\sum_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}A_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}e^{i\boldsymbol{q}\cdot\boldsymbol{r}-i\omega t}\in\mathbb{R}\iff A_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}=\overline{A_{-\boldsymbol{q},-\omega}}.}

(335)

In particular, the active transformations in Eqs. (
334
)-(
334
) translate in Fourier space to:

\displaystyle{&{\cal E}^{g}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}=g^{s}\cdot{\cal E}^{e}_{g^{s,{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{q},i_{g}\omega}=g^{s}\cdot\overline{{\cal E}^{e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{q},\omega}}^{g};{\\
}\boldsymbol{j}^{g}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}=g\cdot\boldsymbol{j}^{e}_{g^{s,{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{q},i_{g}\omega}=g\cdot\overline{\boldsymbol{j}^{e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\cdot\boldsymbol{q},\omega}}^{g};\;\;\;\;g\cdot=i_{g}g^{s}\cdot,}

(336)

with
\overline{A}^{g}
meaning to apply the complex conjugation on
A
iff
i_{g}=-1
.

Consider the direct current density (
\boldsymbol{j}_{\boldsymbol{q}=0,\omega=0}
) response (of a
homogeneous medium) to a dynamic electric field
{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}
(associated to a light wave) and a static homogeneous electric field
{\cal E}^{s}
:
66

\displaystyle{j_{\boldsymbol{0},0,a}=\sigma^{d}_{ab}{\cal E}^{s}_{b}+\sigma^{q}_{abc}{\cal E}^{s}_{b}{\cal E}^{s}_{c}+\Theta_{abcd}{\cal E}^{s}_{b}{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,c}\overline{{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,d}}+\Gamma_{abc}{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,b}\overline{{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,c}}+\ldots}

(337)

\{\sigma^{d/q},\Theta,\Gamma\}
are examples of response tensors. Assuming that the homogeneous medium has a crystalline lattice period which is small compared to the light wavelength, we further expand

\displaystyle{\Gamma_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{q}\omega)=&\;\Gamma^{(0)}_{ijk}(\omega)+q_{l}\Gamma^{(1)}_{iljk}(\omega)+O(q^{2}),}

(338)

with
\Gamma^{(0)}
(
\equiv
the
bulk photovoltaic tensor
) derived by setting
\boldsymbol{q}=\boldsymbol{0}
in
\Gamma^{\alpha}_{ijk}(\boldsymbol{q}\omega)
in the dipole approximation. We use
\Sigma
for the bulk photovoltaic tensor and separate it into
linear
and
circular
components, which are respectively symmetric and antisymmetric under interchange of the last two spatial indices:

\displaystyle{\text{Bulk photovoltaic tensor}\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{abc}=\Gamma^{(0)}_{abc}(\omega)=\Sigma_{abc}^{l}+\Sigma^{c}_{abc};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{abc}=\Sigma^{l}_{acb};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{c}_{abc}=-\Sigma^{c}_{acb}.}

(339)

Altogether, Eqs. (
337
)-(
339
) give a precise meaning to the shorter definition of the bulk photovoltaic tensor in Eq. (
77
).

Suppose the Devil were to actively
g
-transform the system
(\rho^{e}\rightarrow\rho^{g},{\cal E}^{e}\rightarrow{\cal E}^{g},\boldsymbol{j}^{e}\rightarrow\boldsymbol{j}^{g})
, while preserving our spacetime rulers. If
g
were a symmetry of the closed system, then we (with our short-term memory) would not be able to tell that the Devil has played a trick on us. This means we will go on to report the same response tensors while making current measurements on
s^{g}
as we had for current measurements of
s^{e}
:

\displaystyle{\text{Devil's axiom:}\;\;\;\;\text{$g$-symmetric closed system}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;R^{g}=R^{e}\;\;\;\;\text{for any response tensor}\;R.}

(340)

This is what it means for a response tensor to be symmetric.

D.2
Response tensors as algorithms

All response tensors are calculated by some type of perturbation theory. The product of this perturbation theory is an algorithm whose final output is
R
. We use the same letter to denote the algorithm:

\displaystyle{R=R(f^{0},H,I);\;\;\;\;I=I(f^{0},H).}

(341)

The inputs to the algorithm are:

(a) The one-electron distribution function
f^{0}_{B}
in the zeroth order of the perturbation theory.

(b) The Hamiltonian
H=H^{0}+V
.
H^{0}
is the electron quasiparticle/mean-field Hamiltonian in the zeroth order of the perturbation;
H^{0}=\check{H}^{0}
with
\check{H}^{0}
previously defined in App.
A.1
, but we will drop the accent for simplicity in the present appendix.
V=V_{\text{drive}}+V_{\text{env}}
is the sum of perturbations due to the drive and the environment.

(c) One-electron transition rates (induced by
V
) which can be expressed in terms of a collisional integral
I_{B}(f^{0},H)
, as exemplified in App.
A.4.3
and App.
A.4.4
. The collisional integral is the convective time derivative of the electron distribution, i.e., the rate of change of the electron distribution function
f_{B}
in the frame of the moving electron.

We assume
H^{0}
is solvable in the reference system:

\displaystyle{(H^{0,e}-E_{B}^{e})|\,B,e\,\rangle=0;\;\;\;\;}

(342)

and whatever
g
-transformed system

\displaystyle{(H^{0,g}-E_{B}^{g})|\,B,g\,\rangle=0;\;\;\;\;H^{0,g}=\hat{g}H^{0,e}\hat{g}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}.}

(343)

(
|\,B,e\,\rangle
here should be understood as
|\,\psi_{B,e}\,\rangle
in App.
A.1
; once again, we are simplifying notation.)
In general, we should not expect
|\,B,e\,\rangle=|\,B,g\,\rangle
; instead,

\displaystyle{\text{General transformation of eigenstates:}\;\;\;\;\hat{g}|\,B,e\,\rangle=|\,gB,g\,\rangle;\;\;\;\;E^{e}_{B}=E^{g}_{gB},}

(344)

with
gB
meaning the g-transformation of the quantum number
B
, e.g., for crystalline
H^{0}
,
B=(b\boldsymbol{k})
and
gB=(b,g\cdot\boldsymbol{k})
, in accordance with Eq. (
93
). We define
f^{g}_{B}
as the one-electron distribution function in system
s^{g}
; and
f^{0,g}_{B}
as the zeroth-order approximant of
f^{g}_{B}
. If
B
is occupied in
s^{e}
, then
gB
is occupied in
s^{g}
:

\displaystyle{\text{General transformation of electron distribution:}\;\;\;\;f^{g}_{gB}=f^{e}_{B}.}

(345)

If for
s^{e}
the occupation is increasing with time (in the moving frame of electron
B
) due to collisions, then for
s^{g}
(and time-inverting
g
) the occupation is decreasing in time (in the frame of electron
gB
):

\displaystyle{\text{General transformation of collisional integral:}\;\;\;\;I_{gB}^{g}=i_{g}I_{B}^{e};\;\;\;\;i_{g}=\pm 1.}

(346)

For
H
to be an input in Eq. (
341
) means that part of the algorithm (for calculating
R
) involves solving the eigenproblem of
H^{0}
, and that
R
depends on the zeroth-order energy eigenvalues and eigenstates; another part of the algorithm is to incorporate
V
perturbatively. (See App.
D.3.1
for examples.)
R=R(f^{0},H,I)
means that
R
may depend on
f^{0}
and
H
in a manner extraneous to
R
’s dependence on
I(f^{0},H)
.

If a response is understandable as a small deformation of a Fermi-Dirac distribution of electrons which existed in the undriven equilibrium state, hence the zeroth-order one-electron distribution has a Fermi-Dirac form:
f^{0}=f^{\text{FD}}
[Eq. (
145
)].
46
46

46

The intrinsic linear Hall conductivity of a metal exemplifies a response tensor that depends on
f^{0}=f^{\text{FD}}
, depends on
H
(through needing to compute the Berry curvature
\Omega_{\boldsymbol{k}}
) but not
I
:
\sigma^{d}_{xy}(f^{0},H)\propto\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}}f^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\Omega_{xy\boldsymbol{k}}
.

In some cases it is useful to have the zeroth-order
f^{0}
resemble more closely the non-equilibrium steady-state distribution, obtained by solving a Boltzmann transport equation.
16
;
83
Whether or not
f^{0}=f^{\text{FD}}
, we assume in discussions of symmetry constraints that

\displaystyle{\text{$g$-symmetric zeroth-order distribution:}\;\;\;\;f^{0,g}=f^{0,e}\;\;\;\;\text{is shorthand for}\;\;\;\;f^{0}_{gB}=f^{0}_{B}.}

(347)

This assumes a one-electron level with wavevector
g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}
exists if a one-electron level exists with wavevector
\boldsymbol{k}
, as is guaranteed by:

\displaystyle{\text{$g$-symmetric zeroth-order Hamiltonian:}\;\;\;\;H^{0,g}=H^{0,e}.}

(348)

The response tensor
R^{g}
for a g-transformed closed system is given by

\displaystyle{R^{g}=R(f^{0,g},H^{g},I^{g}),}

(349)

meaning that the calculational algorithm
R
is unchanged; what changes is the g-transformed input; this reflects that the same physical laws apply to
s^{e}
and
s^{g}
.

How does one calculate
I^{g}
? We assume
I
is a linear functional of the one-electron distribution and symmetry decomposable as

\displaystyle{I=I^{+}+I^{-};\;\;\;\;I^{\nu}_{B}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}w^{\nu}_{BB^{\prime}}f_{B^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;w^{\nu}_{BB^{\prime}}=\nu w^{\nu}_{B^{\prime}B};\;\;\;\;\nu=\pm 1.}

(350)

If the algorithm
I_{B}^{\nu}(f^{0,e},H^{e})
calculates
I_{B}^{\nu}
in system
s^{e}
:

\displaystyle{(I_{B}^{\nu})^{e}=I_{B}^{\nu}(f^{0,e},H^{e})}

(351)

then
I_{B}^{\nu}
in system
s^{g}
is calculated by

\displaystyle{(I_{B}^{\nu})^{g}=(-\nu)^{g}\,I_{B}^{\nu}(f^{0,g},H^{g});\;\;\;\;(-\nu)^{g}=-\nu\;\;\;\;\text{if $i_{g}=-1$, otherwise}\;\;\;\;(-\nu)^{g}=1.}

(352)

as will be proven below in App.
D.3
.

For the closed system to be
g
-symmetric, we require as an operational definition that

\displaystyle{&\text{g-symmetric electron distribution:}\;\;\;\;f^{g}_{B}=f_{B}^{e},\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;{\\
}\text{g-symmetric Hamiltonian:}\;\;\;\;H^{0,g}=H^{0,e}\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;V^{\text{im},g}=V^{\text{im},e}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;H^{g}=H^{e},\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;{\\
}\text{g-symmetric collisional integral:}\;\;\;\;I^{g}_{B}=I_{B}^{e}.}

f
is the exact one-electron distribution in the driven state, and
f-f^{0}
can be calculated given
H
and
I
. The last two conditions in Eq. (
D.2
), plus our assumption of
g
-symmetric
f^{0}
, jointly imply
R^{g}=R^{e}
, thus satisfying the Devil’s axiom [Eq. (
340
)]. Because
f^{0}
is independent of
g
-transformation, we will often simplify notation by omitting the
f^{0}
-dependence in algorithms:

\displaystyle{R^{g}=R(H^{g},I^{g}),\;\;\;\;(I_{B}^{\nu})^{g}=(-\nu)^{g}\,I_{B}^{\nu}(H^{g}).}

(353)

D.3
g
-transformation of collisional integrals

We largely consider collisional integrals that are linear functionals of the one-electron distribution:

\displaystyle{I_{B}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}w_{BB^{\prime}}f_{B^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;w_{BB^{\prime}}=\;\;\;\;\text{collisional operator}.}

(354)

We calculate
w=w(H)
by an algorithm (also denoted
w
) that inputs the energies and eigenstates of
H^{0}
, as well as the perturbation
V
that induces the collisions. The matrix elements of
w(H^{e})
and
w(H^{g})
are related by

\displaystyle{w_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{g})=\{w_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}(H^{e})\}^{t_{g}},}

(355)

with
A^{t_{g}}
meaning to transpose
A
if
g
inverts time.
Eq. (
355
) includes the case of
H^{T}=H^{e}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;w_{BB^{\prime}}=w_{TB^{\prime},TB}
, which is the reciprocity theorem. By splitting this collisional operator into components which are even and odd under interchange of
B\leftrightarrow B^{\prime}
[Eq. (
350
)],
Eq. (
355
) simplifies to

\displaystyle w^{\nu}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{g})=\nu^{g}w^{\nu}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}(H^{e});\;\;\;\;\nu^{g}=\nu\;\;\;\;\text{if $i_{g}=-1$, otherwise}\;\;\;\;\nu^{g}=1.

(356)

If the algorithm
w_{BB^{\prime}}^{\nu}(H^{e})
calculates
w_{BB^{\prime}}^{\nu}
in system
s^{e}
, then

\displaystyle{\text{Transformation of $w$-algorithms:}\;\;\;\;(w_{BB^{\prime}}^{\nu})^{e}=w_{BB^{\prime}}^{\nu}(H^{e})\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;(w_{BB^{\prime}}^{\nu})^{g}=(-\nu)^{g}w_{BB^{\prime}}^{\nu}(H^{g})\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{wBBp2}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;i_{g}w^{\nu}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}(H^{e}).}

(357)

How does the above factor of
(-\nu)^{g}
arise? This is necessitated by the general transformation law for the collisional integral, applied to each (a)symmetric component:

\displaystyle{\sum_{B^{\prime}}(w^{\nu}_{gBgB^{\prime}})^{g}f_{B^{\prime}}^{e}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{fgfe}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\sum_{B^{\prime}}(w^{\nu}_{gBgB^{\prime}})^{g}f_{gB^{\prime}}^{g}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}(w^{\nu}_{gBB^{\prime}})^{g}f_{B^{\prime}}^{g}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{Ilinear}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;I^{\nu,g}_{gB}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{IgIe}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;i_{g}I^{\nu,e}_{B}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{Ilinear}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;i_{g}\sum_{B^{\prime}}(w^{\nu}_{BB^{\prime}})^{e}f^{e}_{B^{\prime}},}

(358)

which implies

\displaystyle(w^{\nu}_{gBgB^{\prime}})^{g}=i_{g}(w^{\nu}_{BB^{\prime}})^{e}=i_{g}w_{BB^{\prime}}^{\nu}(H^{e})\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{wBBp2}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;(-\nu)^{g}w^{\nu}_{gB,gB^{\prime}}(H^{g}).

(359)

It follows that if the algorithm
I_{B}^{\nu}(f^{0,e},H^{e})
calculates
I_{B}^{\nu}
in system
s^{e}
:

\displaystyle{\text{Transformation of $I$-algorithms:}\;\;\;\;I_{B}^{\nu,e}=I_{B}^{\nu}(f^{0,e},H^{e})\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;I_{B}^{\nu,g}=(-\nu)^{g}I_{B}^{\nu}(f^{0,g},H^{g}).}

(360)

We exemplify these algorithms with the impurity-mediated and photo-excitation collisional integrals in App.
D.3.1
and App.
D.3.2
, respectively.

D.3.1
Impurity-mediated collisional integral as an algorithm

The impurity-mediated collisional integral
I^{im}
was introduced in App.
A.4.4
and hereby symmetry-decomposed as

\displaystyle{I^{im}=I^{im,+}+I^{im,-};\;\;\;\;I^{im,\nu}_{B}=\sum_{B^{\prime}}w^{im,\nu}_{B,B^{\prime}}f_{B^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;w^{im,\nu}_{B,B^{\prime}}=\nu w^{im,\nu}_{B^{\prime},B}.}

(361)

A direct comparison with Eqs. (
162
)-(
162
) allows to identify

\displaystyle{&w^{im,+}_{B,B^{\prime}}=W^{im,s}_{BB^{\prime}}-\delta_{BB^{\prime}}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}W^{im,s}_{BB^{\prime\prime}};\;\;\;\;W^{im,s}_{BB^{\prime}}=W^{im,s}_{B^{\prime}B}=\tfrac{2\pi}{\hbar}N_{\text{imp}}|V^{\text{im}}_{BB^{\prime}}|^{2}\delta(E_{BB^{\prime}});{\\
}w^{im,-}_{BB^{\prime}}=\tfrac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\hbar}N_{\text{imp}}\mathrm{Im}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{BB^{\prime\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime}}V^{\text{im}}_{B^{\prime}B}\delta(E_{BB^{\prime}})\delta(E_{BB^{\prime\prime}}).}

(362)

with
V^{\text{im}}_{BB^{\prime}}
being the impurity potential matrix element. Observed in system
s^{g}
,

V^{\text{im},g}_{BB^{\prime}}
is calculated by an algorithm that inputs
H^{g}
:

\displaystyle{V^{\text{im},g}_{BB^{\prime}}=V^{\text{im}}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{g})=\langle B,g\,|\,V^{\text{im},g}\,|\,B^{\prime},g\rangle.}

(363)

How does
V^{\text{im},g}_{BB^{\prime}}
relate to
V^{\text{im},e}_{BB^{\prime}}=\langle B,e\,|\,V^{\text{im},e}\,|\,B^{\prime},e\rangle
? Depending on whether
\hat{g}
is antiunitary, we transpose the matrix element:

\displaystyle V^{\text{im},g}_{BB^{\prime}}=\langle\hat{g}(g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,e)\,|\,\hat{g}V^{\text{im},e}\hat{g}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\hat{g}\,|\,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime},e\rangle=\{V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}\}^{t_{g}}=\begin{cases}V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}},&i_{g}=1;\\
V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime},g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B},&i_{g}=-1.\end{cases}

(364)

A^{t_{g}}=A^{t}
(transpose of
A
) if
g
inverts time, and otherwise
A^{t_{g}}=A
.

The general identity in Eq. (
356
) reduces in the present context to

\displaystyle{w^{\text{im},+}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{g})=&\;w^{\text{im},+}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}(H^{e});{\\
}w^{\text{im},-}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{g})=\;i_{g}w^{\text{im},-}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}(H^{e}).}

(365)

Eq. (
365
) is derivable from Eq. (
362
), Eq. (
364
) and

\displaystyle{|\{V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}\}^{t_{g}}|^{2}\delta(E_{BB^{\prime}}^{g})=|V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}|^{2}\delta(E_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}^{e}).}

(366)

Eq. (
365
) is derivable from Eq. (
362
), Eq. (
364
) and

\displaystyle{&\mathrm{Im}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}\{V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime\prime}}\}^{t_{g}}\{V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime\prime},g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}\}^{t_{g}}\{V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime},g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B}\}^{t_{g}}\delta(E^{e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}})\delta(E^{e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime\prime}}){\\
}=\;\mathrm{Im}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}\overline{V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,B^{\prime\prime}}V^{\text{im},e}_{B^{\prime\prime},g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}V^{\text{im},e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime},g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B}}^{g}\delta(E^{e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}})\delta(E^{e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,B^{\prime\prime}}).}

\overline{A}^{g}=\overline{A}
if
g
inverts time and otherwise
\overline{A}^{g}={A}
; this arises in Eq. (
D.3.1
) because interchanging
B
and
B^{\prime}
in the third-order Bargmann invariant is equivalent to taking the complex conjugate.

D.3.2
Photo-excitation collisional integral as an algorithm

The photo-excitation collisional integral was introduced in App.
A.4.3
.
Because
W^{ex}_{BB^{\prime}}=W^{ex}_{B^{\prime}B}
[Eq. (
153
)], we identify

\displaystyle{I^{ex,+}_{B}=I^{ex,s}_{B}=&\;\sum_{B^{\prime}}w^{ex,+}_{BB^{\prime}}f_{B^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;w^{ex,+}_{BB^{\prime}}=w^{ex,+}_{B^{\prime}B}=W^{ex}_{BB^{\prime}}-\delta_{BB^{\prime}}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}W^{ex}_{BB^{\prime\prime}}.}

(367)

In accordance with the general transformation law for collisional integrals [Eq. (
346
)], if system
s^{e}
is transformed to
s^{Tg^{s}}
, a photo-excitation rate at
B
[with absorption of incoming
(\boldsymbol{q},\omega)
photons] becomes a photo-relaxation rate at
gB
[with emission of outgoing
(g\cdot\boldsymbol{q},\omega)
photons]:

\displaystyle{I^{ex,+,g}_{gB}=i_{g}I^{ex,+,e}_{B},}

(368)

bearing in mind that the left-hand algorithm inputs
V^{\text{ex},g}
[Eq. (
152
)] with a
g
-transformed electric field [Eq. (
336
)].

Viewing the photo-excitation collisional operator that inputs the Hamiltonian,

\displaystyle{w^{ex,+}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{g})=w^{ex,+}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}(H^{e}),}

(369)

in accordance with the general identity in Eq. (
356
). Eq. (
369
) is derivable from
Eq. (
364
) (which applies not just to
V^{\text{im}}
but also to
V^{\text{ex}}
),
\delta_{\boldsymbol{k},\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\delta_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}\boldsymbol{k},g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\delta_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime},g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}\boldsymbol{k}}
, and

\displaystyle{\delta(E^{g}_{BB^{\prime}}-\hbar\omega)+\delta(E^{g}_{B^{\prime}B}-\hbar\omega)=\delta(E^{e}_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B,g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B^{\prime}}-\hbar\omega)+\delta(E^{e}_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B^{\prime},g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B}-\hbar\omega).}

(370)

Let us decompose the collisional operator into linear and circular components

\displaystyle{w^{ex,+}_{BB^{\prime}}=\sum_{\alpha}^{l,c}w^{\text{ex},+,\alpha}_{BB^{\prime}};\;\;\;\;w^{\text{ex},+,\alpha}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{0},V)=w^{\text{ex},+,\alpha}_{BB^{\prime},bc}(H^{0}){\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,b}\overline{{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,c}};\;\;\;\;w^{\text{ex},+,\alpha}_{BB^{\prime},bc}=s_{\alpha}w^{\text{ex},+,\alpha}_{BB^{\prime},cb};\;\;\;\;s_{l}=1;\;\;\;\;s_{c}=-1.}

(371)

Accordingly, the collisional operator also splits as

\displaystyle{I^{ex,+}_{B}=&\;\sum_{\alpha}^{l,c}I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,bc}{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,b}\overline{{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,c}};\;\;\;\;I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,bc}=s_{\alpha}I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,cb}.}

(372)

I’ll refer to
I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,bc}
as the
photo-excitation tensor
; it has the following transformation law:

\displaystyle{\text{Transformation of photo-excitation tensor:}\;\;\;\;(I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{gB,bc})^{g}=(-s_{\alpha})^{g}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}(I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,ef})^{e}.}

(373)

This follows from Eq. (
368
), with an additional transformation ‘
(s_{\alpha})^{g}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}
’ because the electric fields transform [Eq. (
336
)] as

\displaystyle{{\cal E}^{g}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,b}\overline{{\cal E}^{g}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,c}}=g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\overline{{\cal E}^{e}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,e}\overline{{\cal E}^{e}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega,f}}}^{g};}

(374)

note that complex conjugating the field Fourier coefficients (for
i_{g}=-1
) amounts to a transposition of tensor indices, hence the factor of ‘
(s_{\alpha})^{g}
’.
Similarly, we can derive

\displaystyle{I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{gB,bc}(H^{0,g})=(s_{\alpha})^{g}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,ef}(H^{0,e}).}

(375)

D.4
Symmetry compatibility index of response tensors

For symmetries that square to identity:
g^{2}=e
(including symmetries which are projectively represented:
\hat{g}^{2}=-\hat{e}
), we define

\displaystyle{\text{$g$-compatibility index of response}\;\;\;\;\text{Ind}^{g}_{R}=\pm 1;\;\;\;\;R(H^{g},I(H^{g}))=\text{Ind}^{g}_{R}\cdot R(H^{e},I(H^{e})).}

(376)

If
R(H^{e},I(H^{e}))
is not proportional to
R(H^{g},I(H^{g}))
, then it can be decomposed into two terms with well-defined indices:

\displaystyle{R(H^{e},I(H^{e}))=R^{+}+R^{-};\;\;\;\;R^{\pm}=\tfrac{R(H^{e},I(H^{e}))\pm R(H^{g},I(H^{g}))}{2}.}

(377)

We drop the superscript on
R^{\pm}
for notational simplicity. The practical value of the compatibility index is that

\displaystyle\text{Ind}^{g}_{R}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{$R$ is $(H^{e}=H^{g})$-compatible, i.e., can be nonzero if $H^{e}=H^{g}$. }\\
-1,&\text{$R$ is $(H^{e}=H^{g})$-incompatible, i.e., must be zero if $H^{e}=H^{g}$. }\end{cases}

(378)

If
g
inverts all (or none) of the spatial coordinates, then the compatibility index for
R
is independent of which tensor element
R_{ab\ldots}
is selected. For illustration, the compatibility indices of the photovoltaic tensor under
P,T,PT

are listed in Tab.
10
. If
g
inverts a strict subset of the spatial coordinates, then the compatibility index is tensor-element-dependent, as exemplified by Eqs. (
379
)-(
379
) below.

The following lemmas for the compatibility index (of the photovoltaic tensor) will be proven in Sec.
D.5.2
:

\displaystyle{&\text{$PT$-Lemma}:\;\;\;\;\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma^{\alpha}}=-\text{Ind}^{PT}_{\Sigma^{\alpha}}.{\\
}\text{$TM^{x}$-Lemma}:\;\;\;\;\text{Ind}^{TM^{x}}_{\Sigma_{abc}}=(-1)^{\text{How many of `abc' are x indices}}\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma_{abc}}.{\\
}\text{$TC^{2z}$-Lemma}:\;\;\;\;\text{Ind}^{TC^{2z}}_{\Sigma_{abc}}=-(-1)^{\text{How many of `abc' are z indices}}\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma_{abc}}.}

(379)

D.4.1
T-compatibility index for the impurity-mediated ballistic photocurrent

In this section, we use
\Sigma(H)
as a shorthand for
\Sigma(H,I(H))
.
The impurity-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor is

\displaystyle{\Sigma_{abc}^{\alpha=l,c}(H)=-\tfrac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{BB^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime\prime\prime}}v_{B,a}(w^{im,+})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{BB^{\prime}}w^{im,-}_{B^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}}(w^{im,+})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime\prime\prime}}\delta I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B^{\prime\prime\prime},bc};\;\;\;\;\delta I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,bc}(H^{0})=I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,bc}-[\delta I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,bc}],}

(380)

with
w^{im,\pm}
being the impurity-mediated collisional operators defined in App.
D.3.1
. Eq. (
380
) follows directly from Eq. (
219
) (identifying
W^{mr}=w^{im,+}
and
W^{im,a}=w^{im,-}
), the definition of the bulk photovoltaic tensor in Eqs. (
337
)-(
339
), and the definition of the photo-excitation tensor in Eq. (
372
).

From Eq. (
356
),

\displaystyle{w^{a}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{T})=-w^{a}_{TB,TB^{\prime}}(H^{e}),\;\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\;w^{s}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{T})=w^{s}_{TB,TB^{\prime}}(H^{e})\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;(w^{s})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{T})=(w^{s})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{TB,TB^{\prime}}(H^{e}),}

(381)

with
TB
meaning the one-electron label that is the time-reverse of
B
. It also holds that

\displaystyle{I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,bc}(H^{T})\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{IexcHT}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;&\;s_{\alpha}I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{TB,bc}(H^{e})\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\delta I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{B,bc}(H^{T})=s_{\alpha}\delta I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{TB,bc}(H^{e}).}

(382)

Combining Eqs. (
381
)-(
382
) with
\boldsymbol{v}_{B}(H^{T})=-\boldsymbol{v}_{TB}(H^{e})
[proven below in Eqs. (
385
)-(
386
)],

\displaystyle{\Sigma_{abc}^{\alpha}(H^{T})=-\tfrac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{BB^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime\prime\prime}}(-v_{TB,a})(w^{ex,+})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{TB,TB^{\prime}}(-w^{im,-}_{TB^{\prime},TB^{\prime\prime}})(w^{ex,+})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{TB^{\prime\prime},TB^{\prime\prime\prime}}(s_{\alpha}\delta I^{ex,+,\alpha}_{TB^{\prime\prime\prime},bc})\bigg|_{H^{e}}=s_{\alpha}\Sigma_{abc}^{\alpha}(H^{e}),}

(383)

which proves the T-compatibility index to be

\displaystyle{\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma^{\alpha}_{\text{ballistic}}}=s_{\alpha}.}

(384)

The relation
\boldsymbol{v}_{B}(H^{T})=-\boldsymbol{v}_{TB}(H^{e})
for the group velocity is a special case (
B=B^{\prime}
) of the more general relation for the velocity matrix element

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{v}_{BB^{\prime}}^{g}=\boldsymbol{v}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{g})=\langle B,g\,|\,\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}^{g}\,|\,B^{\prime},g\rangle=i_{g}g^{s}\cdot\{\boldsymbol{v}^{e}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}\}^{t_{g}},

(385)

which follows from the velocity operator
(\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}^{e}=i[H^{0,e},\check{\boldsymbol{r}}])
being a time-oriented vector observable:

\displaystyle{\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}^{g}=i[H^{0,g},\check{\boldsymbol{r}}]=i_{g}\hat{g}i[H^{0,e},\hat{g}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}\check{\boldsymbol{r}}\hat{g}]\hat{g}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}=i_{g}\hat{g}g_{s}\cdot\hat{\boldsymbol{v}}^{e}\hat{g}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}.}

(386)

D.5
General constraints on photovoltaic tensors from Hamiltonian symmetries

A point-group-symmetric [Eq. (
91
)] Hamiltonian has the following implications for the photovoltaic tensor:

\displaystyle{\text{Spatial-symmetric photovoltaic theorem}:\;\;\;\;H^{g^{s}}=&\;H^{e}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{abc}=g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{def};{\\
}\text{Magnet-symmetric photovoltaic theorem}:\;\;\;\;H^{Tg^{s}}=\;H^{e}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{abc}=\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{def}.}

(387)

The second theorem applies: (i) whether or not
H^{T}=H^{e}
, (ii) whether or not
H^{g^{s}}=H^{e}
, and (iii) to components of the bulk photovoltaic tensor with a well-defined compatibility index under time reversal:

\displaystyle{\Sigma\big(H^{T},I(H^{T})\big)=\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}\cdot\Sigma\big(H^{e},I(H^{e})\big);\;\;\;\;\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}=\pm 1.}

(388)

D.5.1
Proof of spatial-symmetric photovoltaic theorem

We begin by deriving a general property about symmetry transformations of the photovoltaic tensor:

\displaystyle{&\boldsymbol{j}^{e}=\Sigma^{\alpha,e}{\cal E}^{e}\overline{{\cal E}^{e}}{\\
}g^{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}^{e}=\boldsymbol{j}^{g^{s}}=\Sigma^{\alpha,g^{s}}{\cal E}^{g^{s}}\overline{{\cal E}^{g^{s}}}=\Sigma^{\alpha,g^{s}}(g^{s}\cdot{\cal E}^{e})\overline{g^{s}\cdot{\cal E}^{e}};{\\
}-g^{s}\cdot\boldsymbol{j}^{e}=\boldsymbol{j}^{Tg^{s}}=\Sigma^{\alpha,Tg^{s}}{\cal E}^{Tg^{s}}\overline{{\cal E}^{Tg^{s}}}=\Sigma^{\alpha,Tg^{s}}\overline{(g^{s}\cdot{\cal E}^{e})\overline{g^{s}\cdot{\cal E}^{e}}}=s_{\alpha}\Sigma^{\alpha,Tg^{s}}{(g^{s}\cdot{\cal E}^{e})\overline{g^{s}\cdot{\cal E}^{e}}}.}

Since this holds for any
\boldsymbol{j}^{e}
and
{\cal E}^{e}
, and
g^{s}_{ab}
is an orthogonal matrix,

\displaystyle{\text{General transformation:}\;\;\;\;&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha,g^{s}}_{abc}=g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha,e}_{def}\iff g^{s}_{da}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha,e}_{abc}=\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha,g^{s}}_{def}g^{s}_{eb}g^{s}_{fc}{\\
}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha,Tg^{s}}_{abc}=-s_{\alpha}g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha,e}_{def}.}

(389)

We shall speak of Eqs. (
389
)-(
389
) as
general transformation laws
that apply no matter the symmetries of
H
.

According to the symmetry transformation of the collisional integral [Eq. (
353
)], a spatial-symmetric Hamiltonian implies a spatial-symmetric collisional integral:

\displaystyle{H^{g^{s}}=&\;H^{e}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;I^{g^{s}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=I^{e}_{\boldsymbol{k}}.}

(390)

This implies that all operational conditions are satisfied for the closed system to be
g^{s}
-symmetric, and hence by the Devil’s axiom [Eq. (
340
)],
R^{g^{s}}=R^{e}
. In particular, substituting
\Sigma^{g^{s}}=\Sigma^{e}\equiv\Sigma
into Eq. (
389
), we obtain Eq. (
387
), which completes the proof.

D.5.2
Proof of magnet-symmetric photovoltaic theorem

Owing to the time-orientation of the collisional integral [Eq. (
353
)], a magnet-symmetric Hamiltonian does not generally imply a magnet-symmetric collisional integral, and therefore does not generally imply a magnet-symmetric closed system
\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;R^{Tg^{s}}=R^{e}
. In fact, even if the Hamiltonian is magnet-symmetric, most physically-interesting closed systems are not magnet-symmetric, owing to collisions which are time-oriented by the second thermodynamic law. In other words, if the Devil were to
Tg^{s}
-transform our closed system, we could almost always certainly tell that a trick has been played by measuring a decreasing entropy.

Thus with magnetic symmetries, without being able to use the Devil’s axiom, we can no longer derive a self-constraint on
\Sigma
using the same route [Sec.
D.5.1
] we had taken for spatial symmetries. An alternative route exists for irreducible
\Sigma
with a well-defined compatibility index under time reversal [Eq. (
388
)], as we now explicate. Combining the compatibility relation [Eq. (
388
)] with the general transformation law [Eq. (
389
)] for the photovoltaic tensor under time reversal:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H^{T},-s_{\nu}I^{\nu}(H)^{T})\big)\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{RgIg}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{T,\alpha}=-s_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{e,\alpha}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{RgIg}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;-s_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H^{e},I^{\nu}(H^{e})\big)\;\;\;\;\text{(matrix indices suppressed)},}

(391)

we obtain

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H^{T},-s_{\nu}I^{\nu}(H)^{T})\big)=-s_{\alpha}\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma^{\alpha}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H^{T},I^{\nu}(H^{T})\big),}

(392)

which holds whether or not
H^{T}=H^{e}
. But since the above relation holds for arbitrary
H^{T}
, we may as well drop the superscript on ‘
H^{T}
’:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H,-s_{\nu}I^{\nu}(H))\big)=-s_{\alpha}\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma^{\alpha}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H,I^{\nu}(H)\big).}

(393)

Returning to the general transformation law [Eq. (
389
)] under magnetic symmetries,

\displaystyle{-s_{\alpha}\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma^{\alpha}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H^{Tg^{s}},I^{\nu}(H^{Tg^{s}})\big)\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{magicindex}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H^{Tg^{s}},-s_{\nu}I^{\nu}(H^{Tg^{s}})\big)\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{RgIg}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;&\;\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{Tg^{s},\alpha}=-s_{\alpha}g^{s}g^{s}g^{s}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{e,\alpha}{\\
}\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{RgIg}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;-s_{\alpha}g^{s}g^{s}g^{s}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H^{e},I^{\nu}(H^{e})\big),}

which holds whether or not
H^{Tg^{s}}=H^{e}
. However, if

\displaystyle{H^{Tg^{s}}=H^{e}:\;\;\;\;\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma^{\alpha}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H,I^{\nu}(H)\big)\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{toreduce}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;g^{s}g^{s}g^{s}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{\alpha}\big(H,I^{\nu}(H)\big).}

(394)

Since the above relation is independent of
s_{\alpha}
and
s_{\nu}
, we may as well drop the
\alpha
and
\nu
superscripts, finally arriving at
Eq. (
387
) and completing the proof.

D.5.3
Symmetry compatibility index from general transformation laws

As a bonus, let us return to Eq. (
D.5.2
) and focus on
Tg^{s}=TP=PT
with
g^{s}_{ab}=P_{ab}=-\delta_{ab}
being the parity transformation (i.e., spatial inversion):

\displaystyle{\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma}\cdot\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\big(H^{TP},I(H^{TP})\big)=(-1)^{3}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\big(H^{e},I(H^{e})\big).}

(395)

From the general definition of the compatibility index [Eq. (
376
)], we identify the index under
PT
transformation as in Eq. (
379
), which is our PT-lemma.

For
Tg^{s}=TM^{x}
with
M^{x}
reflecting the x coordinate, Eq. (
D.5.2
) has different implications for
\Sigma_{abc}
depending on how many of ‘abc’ are x indices:

\displaystyle{\text{Ind}^{T}_{\Sigma_{abc}}\cdot&\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{abc}\big(H^{TM^{x}},I(H^{TM^{x}})\big)=(-1)^{N_{abc;x}}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{abc}\big(H^{e},I(H^{e})\big)\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\text{$TM^{x}$-lemma in Eq.\ (\ref{TMxlemma})}.}

(396)

The
TC^{2z}
-lemma in Eq. (
379
) is similarly proven.

D.6
Symmetric response tensors as equivariant linear maps

In applying the spatial-symmetric photovoltaic theorem [Eq. (
387
)] to concretely determine the symmetry-allowed photovoltaic response tensor elements, it is useful to view a
G
-symmetric response tensor as a
G
-equivariant linear map and apply Schur’s lemma.

Let us first develop this viewpoint of response tensors as linear maps.
Since each of
\{\boldsymbol{j},\boldsymbol{\cal E},\overline{\boldsymbol{\cal E}}\}
transforms in the three-vector representation of the point group, we introduce:

(i)
V
is a 3D vector space spanned by
|\,x\,\rangle,|\,y\,\rangle,|\,z\,\rangle
, with
|\,x\,\rangle
corresponding to either of
\{j_{x},{\cal E}_{x},\overline{{\cal E}_{x}}\}
, depending on the context.

(ii)
V^{2}=V\otimes V
is tensor-product vector space spanned by nine basis vectors:
|\,x,x\,\rangle,|\,x,y\,\rangle,\ldots

(iii)
V^{3}=V\otimes V\otimes V
is spanned by 27 basis vectors:
|\,x,x,x\,\rangle,\ldots

Reproducing Eq. (
387
) and specializing to
g\in G
(a non-magnetic point group),

\displaystyle{\Sigma_{abc}=g_{ad}g_{be}g_{cf}\Sigma_{def}}

(397)

describes an
G
-invariant tensor. This can equivalently seen as a
G
-equivariant linear map (in short,
G
-linear map) between vector spaces (
a
and
b
) equipped with representations (
\rho_{a}
and
\rho_{b}
) of
G
.

(Example 1) Let
\mathbb{R}
be a 1-dim vector space on which
G
acts as

\displaystyle{\text{1D trivial representation:}\;\;\;\;\rho_{\mathbb{R}}(g)=1,\;\;\;\;\forall g\in G.}

(398)

(Example 2) Let
V^{3}
be a 27-dim vector space on which
G
acts as
\rho_{V^{3}}(g)=\rho_{V}(g)\otimes\rho_{V}(g)\otimes\rho_{V}(g)
, with matrix representation

\displaystyle{g_{ad}=&\;\langle a\,|\,\rho_{V}(g)\,|\,d\rangle;\;\;\;\;g_{ad}g_{be}g_{cf}=\langle a,b,c\,|\,\rho_{V^{3}}(g)\,|\,d,e,f\rangle.}

(399)

Eq. (
397
) can be seen as a
G
-equivariant linear map

\displaystyle{\Sigma:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow V^{3},\;\;\;\;1\mapsto\vec{\Sigma}=\sum_{abc}\Sigma_{abc}|\,a,b,c\,\rangle,}

(400)

or equivalently
47
47

47

Both viewpoints are equivalent if
g
is orthogonal; otherwise, one should take care with distinguishing co- and contravariant indices.
as the reverse
G
-equivariant linear map

\displaystyle{\Sigma:V^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\;\;\;\;\vec{\Sigma}\mapsto 1.}

(401)

In general,
G
-equivariance of a linear map means that the linear map commutes with (or is compatible with) the
G
-action,

\displaystyle{\Sigma:A\rightarrow B,\;\;\;\;\Sigma\,\rho_{A}(g)=\rho_{B}(g)\,\Sigma,\;\;\;\;\forall g\in G,}

(402)

bearing in mind
G
acts on different vector spaces on different sides of the above equality. Then it should be seen that invariant-tensor equation [Eq. (
397
)] is the matrix representation of the equivariance condition [Eq. (
402
)] with
A=\mathbb{R}
and
B=V^{3}
. From Eq. (
400
),
\Sigma\circ 1=\vec{\Sigma}\in V^{3}
, and Eq. (
402
) implies

\displaystyle{\Sigma\circ 1\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{1Dtrivialrep}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;\Sigma\rho_{\mathbb{R}}(g)\circ 1=\rho_{V^{3}}(g)\Sigma\circ 1\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\vec{\Sigma}=\rho_{V^{3}}(g)\vec{\Sigma},}

(403)

meaning that
\vec{\Sigma}
is a
G
-invariant vector.

We can rewrite Eq. (
397
) as a
G
-linear map from
A=V^{2}
to
B=V

\displaystyle{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{da}\Sigma_{abc}=\Sigma_{def}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}_{eb}g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}_{fc}\iff\rho_{V}(g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}})\;\Sigma=\Sigma\;\rho_{V^{2}}(g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}).}

(404)

In the photovoltaic context, this has the most direct physical interpretation:
V
is the vector space of the current and
V^{2}
the vector space of the squared electric field. In general, an invariant tensor in
V^{3}
gives the same data as several types of
G
-linear map via hom-tensor adjunctions, or, in lay-speak, by various ways to split the indices (taking care of the distinction between contra-and covariance if
g
is not orthogonal).

D.6.1
Application of Schur’s lemma to response tensors

G
-linear maps between irreducible representations (irreps) are subject to Schur’s lemma. In particular, if
\rho_{A}
and
\rho_{B}
are inequivalent,
\Sigma=0
; if
\rho_{A}
and
\rho_{B}
are equivalent, then in the basis in which
\rho_{A}=\rho_{B}
,
\Sigma
is proportional to the identity map. Let us give some examples.

Parity group
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{P}=\{e,P\}
,
A=V^{2}
and
B=V

Each of
|\,x\,\rangle,|\,y\,\rangle,|\,z\,\rangle
transforms in the nontrivial irrep [namely,
\rho_{V}(e)=1=-\rho_{V}(P)
], while each of
|\,x,x\,\rangle,|\,x,y\,\rangle,\ldots
transforms in the trivial irrep [namely,
\rho_{V^{2}}(e)=1=\rho_{V^{2}}(P)
]. Schur’s lemma then implies
\Sigma_{abc}=0
for any
a,b,c
.

Parity group
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{P}=\{e,P\}
,
A=\mathbb{R}
and
B=V^{3}

\rho_{\mathbb{R}}
is trivial by definition. There are no
G
-invariant vectors
\vec{\Sigma}\in V^{3}
; after all, each of
|\,x,x,x\,\rangle,|\,x,x,y\,\rangle,\ldots
transforms in the nontrivial irrep [namely,
\rho_{V^{2}}(e)=1=-\rho_{V^{2}}(P)
]. Schur’s lemma then implies
\Sigma_{abc}=0
for any
a,b,c
. This must, of course, be consistent with the previous paragraph.

G=SO(2)
,
A=V^{3}
and
B=\mathbb{R}

m

Irrep in
V^{3}

Irrep in
\mathbb{R}

-3

|\,x^{-}x^{-}x^{-}\,\rangle

0

-2

|\,\{zx^{-}x^{-}\}\,\rangle

0

-1

|\,\{x^{\pm}x^{\mp}x^{-}\}\,\rangle

0

0

|\,zzz/\{zx^{\pm}x^{\mp}\}\,\rangle

1

1

|\,\{x^{\pm}x^{\mp}x^{+}\}\,\rangle

0

2

|\,\{zx^{+}x^{+}\}\,\rangle

0

3

|\,x^{+}x^{+}x^{+}\,\rangle

0

Table 20:
Irreps of
SO(2)
in the vector spaces
V^{3}
and
\mathbb{R}
. Under rotation by angle
\theta
,
x^{\pm}=x\pm iy\rightarrow\omega^{\pm 1}x^{\pm}
with
\omega=e^{i\theta}
. An irrep has angular momentum
m
if it transforms under
\theta
-rotation by picking up a multiplicative phase factor
e^{im\theta}
.
\{abc\}
means all permutations of the the tensor indices are included.

Only trivial maps are possible between irreps with different
m
:

\displaystyle{&|\,\{x^{\pm}x^{\mp}x^{+}\}\,\rangle\mapsto 0\cdot 1;{\\
}|\,\{x^{\pm}x^{\mp}x^{-}\}\,\rangle\mapsto 0\cdot 1;{\\
}|\,\{zzx^{\pm}\}\,\rangle\mapsto 0\cdot 1;{\\
}|\,\{zx^{\pm}x^{\pm}\}\,\rangle\mapsto 0\cdot 1;{\\
}|\,\{x^{\pm}x^{\pm}x^{\pm}\}\,\rangle\mapsto 0\cdot 1,}

(405)

which imply the following constraints:

\displaystyle{&\text{Eqs.\ (\ref{Map1})-(\ref{Map2})}:\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{xxx}=-\Sigma_{yyx}=-\Sigma_{xyy}=-\Sigma_{yxy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{yyy}=-\Sigma_{xxy}=-\Sigma_{xyx}=-\Sigma_{yxx};{\\
}\text{Eq.\ (\ref{Map3})}:\;\;\;\;0=\Sigma_{\{zzx\}}=\Sigma_{\{zzy\}};{\\
}\text{Eq.\ (\ref{Map4})}:\;\;\;\;0=\Sigma_{zxx}-\Sigma_{zyy}=\Sigma_{xzx}-\Sigma_{yzy}=\Sigma_{xxz}-\Sigma_{yyz}=\Sigma_{zxy}+\Sigma_{zyx}=\Sigma_{xzy}+\Sigma_{yzx}=\Sigma_{xyz}+\Sigma_{yxz};{\\
}\text{Eq.\ (\ref{Map5}) and Eq.\ (\ref{c3constraint})}:\;\;\;\;0=\Sigma_{xxx}=\Sigma_{yyy}.}

(406)

Altogether these are 20 constraints leaving 7 independent tensor elements (7=4 linear + 3 circular):

\displaystyle{\text{$SO(2)$-allowed photovoltaic tensor elements:}\;\;\;\;&\Sigma_{zzz};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{zxy}=-\Sigma_{zyx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{xzy}=-\Sigma_{yzx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{xyz}=-\Sigma_{yxz};{\\
}\Sigma_{zxx}=\Sigma_{zyy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{xzx}=\Sigma_{yzy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{xxz}=\Sigma_{yyz};{\\
}\text{$SO(2)$-allowed linear photovoltaic elements:}\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{zzz};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{zxx}=\Sigma^{l}_{zyy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{xyz}=-\Sigma^{l}_{yzx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{xxz}=\Sigma^{l}_{yyz}.{\\
}\text{$SO(2)$-allowed circular photovoltaic elements:}\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{c}_{zxy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{c}_{xyz}=\Sigma^{c}_{yzx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{c}_{xxz}=\Sigma^{c}_{yyz}.}

G=C_{3}
,
A=V^{3}
and
B=\mathbb{R}

m

Irrep in
V^{3}

Irrep in
\mathbb{R}

0

|\,zzz/x^{\pm}x^{\pm}x^{\pm}/\{zx^{\pm}x^{\mp}\}\,\rangle

1

1

|\,\{zzx^{+}\}/\{x^{\pm}x^{\mp}x^{+}\}\,\rangle

0

2

|\,\{zzx^{-}\}/\{zx^{\pm}x^{\pm}\}/\{x^{\pm}x^{\mp}x^{-}\}\,\rangle

0

Table 21:
Irreps of
C_{3}
in the vector spaces
V^{3}
and
\mathbb{R}
. Under three-fold rotation,
x^{\pm}=x\pm iy\rightarrow\omega^{\pm 1}x^{\pm}
with
\omega=e^{i2\pi/3}
. An irrep has angular momentum
m
(defined modulo three) if it transforms under three-fold rotation by picking up a multiplicative phase factor
\omega^{m}
.
\{abc\}
means all permutations of the the tensor indices are included.

Only trivial maps are possible between irreps with different
m
; these are the same trivial maps in Eqs. (
405
)-(
405
) but excluding Eq. (
405
). It follows that the constraints in Eqs. (
406
)-(
406
) apply, but not Eq. (
406
). Altogether these are 18 constraints leaving 9 independent tensor elements (9=6 linear + 3 circular):

\displaystyle{\text{$C_{3}$-allowed photovoltaic tensor elements:}\;\;\;\;&\Sigma_{xxx}=-\Sigma_{yyx}=-\Sigma_{xyy}=-\Sigma_{yxy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{yyy}=-\Sigma_{xxy}=-\Sigma_{xyx}=-\Sigma_{yxx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{zzz}{\\
}\Sigma_{zxy}=-\Sigma_{zyx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{xzy}=-\Sigma_{yzx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{xyz}=-\Sigma_{yxz};{\\
}\Sigma_{zxx}=\Sigma_{zyy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{xzx}=\Sigma_{yzy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma_{xxz}=\Sigma_{yyz};{\\
}\text{$C_{3}$-allowed linear photovoltaic elements:}\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{xxx}=-\Sigma^{l}_{xyy}=-\Sigma^{l}_{yxy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{yyy}=-\Sigma^{l}_{xxy}=-\Sigma^{l}_{yxx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{zzz};{\\
}\Sigma^{l}_{zxx}=\Sigma^{l}_{zyy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{xyz}=-\Sigma^{l}_{yzx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{xxz}=\Sigma^{l}_{yyz}.{\\
}\text{$C_{3}$-allowed circular photovoltaic elements:}\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{c}_{zxy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{c}_{xyz}=\Sigma^{c}_{yzx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{c}_{xxz}=\Sigma^{c}_{yyz}.}

For linearly-polarized light in a 2D system, we can assume the electric field amplitude is real and parametrized by one angle:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\cal E}_{\boldsymbol{q}\omega}=|{\cal E}|\boldsymbol{e}\in\mathbb{R};\;\;\;\;\boldsymbol{e}=(e_{x},e_{y})=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta).}

(407)

Parametrizing the two independent tensor elements (‘two’ in 2D) in Eq. (
D.6.1
) by a different angle:

\displaystyle{\Sigma^{l}_{xxx}=|\Sigma^{l}|\sin\phi;\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{xxy}=|\Sigma^{l}|\cos\phi,}

(408)

and applying Eq. (
D.6.1
), we can express the photovoltaic current as a function of the two angles:

\displaystyle{j_{x}=&\;|{\cal E}|^{2}\big(\Sigma_{xxx}(e_{x}^{2}-e_{y}^{2})+2\Sigma_{xxy}e_{x}e_{y}\big)=|\Sigma^{l}||{\cal E}|^{2}\sin(2\theta+\phi);{\\
}j_{y}=\;|{\cal E}|^{2}\big(\Sigma_{yxx}(e_{x}^{2}-e_{y}^{2})+2\Sigma_{yxy}e_{x}e_{y}\big)=|\Sigma^{l}||{\cal E}|^{2}\cos(2\theta+\phi).}

Thus the electric field transforms like it has spin one, while the current transforms like it has spin two, but
C_{3}
symmetry alone does not fix the direction in which
|j_{x}|
is maximized.

Let us enlarge the group
C_{3}
to
C_{3v}
, which has an extra generator
M_{j}
that inverts the
j
-coordinate; to remind us of this specific mirror plane, we add a superscript to
C_{3v}^{j}
. According to the spatial-symmetric photovoltaic theorem [Eq. (
387
)], the effect of
M_{j}
symmetry is to constrain
N_{abc;j}
to be even. For illustration,
M_{x}
symmetry extinguishes 4 of the 9 independent tensor elements allowed by
C_{3}
:

\displaystyle{\text{$C_{3v}^{x}$-allowed linear photovoltaic elements:}\;\;\;\;&\Sigma^{l}_{yyy}=-\Sigma^{l}_{xxy}=-\Sigma^{l}_{yxx};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{zzz};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{zxx}=\Sigma^{l}_{zyy};\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}_{xxz}=\Sigma^{l}_{yyz}.{\\
}\text{$C_{3v}^{x}$-allowed circular photovoltaic elements:}\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{c}_{xxz}=\Sigma^{c}_{yyz}.}

(409)

In particular, setting
\Sigma^{l}_{xxx}=0
in Eq. (
D.6.1
) gives the
C_{3v}^{x}
-allowed current in 2D:

\displaystyle{j_{x}=\Sigma^{l}_{xxy}|{\cal E}|^{2}\sin(2\theta);\;\;\;\;j_{y}=\Sigma^{l}_{xxy}|{\cal E}|^{2}\cos(2\theta),}

(410)

with mirror symmetry guaranteeing that
|j_{x}|
is maximized if the light polarization vector deviates from the mirror x-axis by
\pi/4
.

D.7
Hamiltonian-specific symmetry constraints on photovoltaic tensors

The two theorems in Eqs. (
387
)-(
387
) apply to all photovoltaic tensors independent of specific details of the Hamiltonian, beyond the assumption that the Hamiltonian is symmetric:
H^{g}=H^{e}
. In contrast, we introduce in this subappendix the notion of
H
-specific symmetry constraints
(short for Hamiltonian-specific), which apply to specific components of the photovoltaic tensor and depend on specific structural details of the Hamiltonian.

We shall further distinguish between
H
-specific symmetry constraints of the first and second kinds, or first- and second-kind constraints, in short. A
first-kind constraint
(resp.
second-kind constraint
) is a
H
-specific symmetry constraint that arises from the Hamiltonian being
g
-symmetric (resp.
g
-
a
symmetric:
H^{g}\neq H^{e}
), as will be discussed in App.
D.7.1
(resp. App.
D.7.2
).

D.7.1
Hamiltonian-specific symmetry constraints of the first kind

We package two first-kind constraints into two theorems, the first of which is:

\vec{H}
-polarity theorem:
For any two-band
H^{0}
with a point group
G
, if there exists no invariant vector in the Hamiltonian-vector representation of
G
, then the ballistic photocurrent (mediated by overscreened monopole impurities) vanishes.

The notion of the Hamiltonian-vector representation was introduced in App.
A.2.3
for two-band Hamiltonians of the form
H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}=\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}+\delta E_{\boldsymbol{k}}\mathbb{1}
; the related notion of invariant vectors was defined in Eq. (
118
). To be ‘mediated by overscreened monopole impurities’ means that the impurity potential responsible for long-wavelength skew scattering is that of the overscreened monopole in Eq. (
53
).

To prove this theorem, we will show that the asymmetric transition rate matrix element [Eq. (
36
)] vanishes, which would imply that the ballistic photocurrent (being a linear functional of the asymmetric transition rate [Eqs. (
37
)-(
38
)]) also vanishes. Indeed, plugging Eq. (
53
) into Eq. (
36
),

\displaystyle{(\text{Asymmetric transition rate})_{\boldsymbol{k}\leftarrow\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}=\tfrac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\hbar}N_{\text{imp}}\big(\tfrac{e^{2}}{{\cal V}\epsilon k^{2}_{\text{scr}}}\big)^{3}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}})\sum_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}{\text{Im}\;}\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\rangle\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}}\rangle\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}).}

(411)

Plugging in the
\boldsymbol{d}
-vector expression for the imaginary component of the Bargmann invariant [Eq. (
52
)], we find that the asymmetric transition rate matrix element is proportional to

\displaystyle{\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\times\hat{\mathbf{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}\cdot[{\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}}]_{E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}};\;\;\;\;[{\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}}]_{E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}}=\tfrac{\int\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}-E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}})d\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}{\int\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}}-E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}})d\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}},}

(412)

with
E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}=E_{\boldsymbol{k}}
the excitation energy and
[\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}]_{E}
meaning the iso-energy average of
\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}
. Let us change the integration variable as
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}=g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}
, with
g
an element in the point group of
G
, and
g\circ
denoting a transformation by the matrix representation of
g
in
\boldsymbol{k}
-space. (a) Because this matrix is orthogonal, the Jacobian determinant for the transformation from
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}
to
\boldsymbol{k}
is unity. (b) Because
g
is a symmetry of
H^{0}
[Eq. (
113
)], (b)
E_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}}=E_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, which is equivalent to
|\boldsymbol{d}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}}|=|\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}|
, and (c)
\boldsymbol{d}_{g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}}=\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}\cdot\boldsymbol{d}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
, with
\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}
the Hamiltonian-vector (i.e.
\boldsymbol{d}
-vector) representation of
g
. Combining (a-c),

\displaystyle{[{\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}}]_{E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}}=\tfrac{\int\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}})d\boldsymbol{k}}{\int\delta(E_{\boldsymbol{k}}-E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}})d\boldsymbol{k}}=\overleftrightarrow{\boldsymbol{g}}\cdot[{\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}}]_{E_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ex}}}}.}

(413)

Because we can in principle change the integration variable (
\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}=g\cdot\boldsymbol{k}
) for any
g\in G
, Eq. (
413
) must hold for all
g\in G
, which identifies
[{\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}}]_{E}
as an invariant vector in the Hamiltonian-vector representation of
G
[Eq. (
118
)]. If there is no invariant vector, then
[{\boldsymbol{\hat{d}}}]_{E}=\boldsymbol{0}
for any
E
, which extinguishes the ballistic photocurrent, thus proving the theorem.

As applications of the
\vec{H}
-polarity theorem, we have shown in App.
A.2.3
that there are no invariant vectors (in the Hamiltonian-vector representation of
G
) for all
n
-fold Dirac-Weyl fermions, hence the monopole-impurity-mediated
\boldsymbol{j}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\text{ballistic}}}
vanishes.

We now proceed to the second theorem:

TC_{2z}
theorem:
For 2D systems with
TC_{2z}
-symmetric Hamiltonians, (a) the linear component of the ballistic/shift current vanishes, and (b) both linear and circular components of the ballistic photocurrent (mediated by centrosymmetric impurities) vanishes.

To be ‘mediated by centrosymmetric impurities’ means that the impurity potential (responsible for long-wavelength skew scattering) is centrosymmetric:
V^{\text{im}}_{\boldsymbol{r}}=V^{\text{im}}_{-\boldsymbol{r}}
, implying that the Fourier transform is real:
\widetilde{V^{\text{im}}}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\in\mathbb{R}
. (This includes the case of the monopole impurity.) It follows that the imaginary component of the N-point self-interference amplitude [Eq. (
44
)] is proportional to the imaginary component of the N-point Bargmann invariant [Eq. (
44
)]:

\displaystyle{{\text{Im}\;}\{\text{N-point self-interference amplitude}\}\approx\widetilde{V^{\text{im}}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}-\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}\ldots\widetilde{V^{\text{im}}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{N}-\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\cdot{\text{Im}\;}{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}\ldots\boldsymbol{k}_{N}},}

(414)

with
{\mathscr{B}}_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}\ldots\boldsymbol{k}_{N}}=\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{2}}\rangle\ldots\langle u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{N}}\,|\,u_{\boldsymbol{k}_{1}}\rangle
.

Statement (a) in the
TC_{2z}
theorem follows from applying the magnet-symmetric photovoltaic theorem [Eq. (
387
)], with the recognition that the linear shift/ballistic photovoltaic tensor is compatible with time-reversal symmetry [Tab.
10
]. Statement (a) is not a
H
-specific symmetry constraint, but statement (b) is.

Let us prove statement (b). By assumption, the zeroth-order Hamiltonian
H^{0}
is
TC_{2z}
-symmetric, meaning that

\displaystyle{\widehat{TC_{2z}}\,H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}\,\widehat{TC_{2z}}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}=H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}}

(415)

holds for all
\boldsymbol{k}
, which is possible in 2D systems because
TC_{2z}\circ(k_{x},k_{y})=(k_{x},k_{y})
.
Because
\widehat{TC_{2z}}
squares to the identity operator in its action on wave functions (independent of whether
\widehat{T}^{2}=\pm\mathbb{1}
), all eigenstates (
\{|\,u_{b\boldsymbol{k}}\,\rangle\}_{b}
) of
H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
can be chosen to have real-valued wave functions,
6
implying that the N-point self-interference amplitude [Eq. (
414
)] vanishes for any
N
. In particular, our impurity-mediated ballistic photocurrent is proportional to the imaginary component of the 3-point self-interference amplitude [Eq. (
38
)] and therefore also vanishes. This holds for effective few-band
H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
as well as for the Schrödinger-type, infinite-band
H^{0}_{\boldsymbol{k}}
.

D.7.2
Hamiltonian-specific symmetry constraints of the second kind

Combining the general transformation laws [Eq. (
389
),Eq. (
D.5.2
)] and the T-compatibility index specific to the impurity-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor [Eq. (
384
)],

\displaystyle{\Sigma^{\alpha}_{abc}(H^{g})=&\;(s_{\alpha})^{g}g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\Sigma^{\alpha}_{def}(H^{e});}

(416)

with
\Sigma(H)
being a shorthand for the algorithmic function
\Sigma(H,I(H))
.
If the Hamiltonian is symmetric, the above transformation law implies:

\displaystyle{H^{g}=&\;H^{e}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\Sigma^{\alpha}_{abc}=(s_{\alpha})^{g}g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\Sigma^{\alpha}_{def},}

(417)

in accordance with the symmetry theorems in Eqs. (
387
)-(
387
).
Eq. (
417
) is a symmetry constraint that is specific to the impurity-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor, because the form of the constraint changes between the linear (
s^{l}=1
) and circular (
s^{c}=-1
) components of the tensor.

Though dipole impurity potentials are asymmetric with respect to certain
g
,
g
may nevertheless constrain the response tensor. Let us give two examples:

(i) Commonly, we may encounter
g
that is a symmetry of
H^{0}
and
V^{\text{dr}}
(the driving perturbation) but not necessarily of the impurity potentials; the latter are classified by their parity under
g
transformation:

\displaystyle{\text{$g$-parity even/odd}:\;\;\;\;V^{\text{im},g}=p_{g}V^{\text{im},e};\;\;\;\;p_{g}=\pm 1.}

(418)

We say that
V^{\text{im}}
is
g
-
antisymmetric
if it has odd
g
-parity:

\displaystyle{\text{$g$-antisymmetric $V^{\text{im}}$}:\;\;\;\;H^{g}=H^{0,g}+V^{\text{im},g}+V^{\text{dr},g}=H^{0,e}-V^{\text{im},g}+V^{\text{dr},e}\neq H^{e}=H^{0,e}+V^{\text{im},g}+V^{\text{dr},e},}

(419)

which exemplifies a
g
-asymmetric Hamiltonian
H
. A specific property of the ballistic photovoltaic tensor is that for impurity potentials with a well-defined
g
-parity (
p_{g}
),

\displaystyle{H^{0,g}=H^{0,e},\;\;\;\;V^{\text{im},g}=p_{g}V^{\text{im},e}:\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{\alpha}(H^{g})\approx p_{g}\Sigma^{\alpha}(H^{e}),}

(420)

with ‘
\approx
’ meaning equal to the lowest nontrivial order in the impurity potential
V^{\text{im}}
. Indeed, inspection of Eq. (
380
) reveals that
V^{\text{im}}
enters the formula for
\Sigma
only through the transition rate matrix elements
(w^{s})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}
and
w^{a}
;
w^{s}
is second order in
V^{\text{im}}
[Eq. (
362
)] and therefore even under
V^{\text{im}}\rightarrow-V^{\text{im}}
;
w^{a}
is third order in
V^{\text{im}}
[Eq. (
362
)] and therefore odd under
V^{\text{im}}\rightarrow-V^{\text{im}}
; this makes
\Sigma^{\alpha}
(which is a linear functional of
w^{a}
) also odd under
V^{\text{im}}\rightarrow-V^{\text{im}}
. Combining Eq. (
420
) with the general transformation law in Eq. (
416
),

\displaystyle{H^{0,g}=H^{0,e},\;\;\;\;V^{\text{im},g}=p_{g}V^{\text{im},e}:\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{\alpha}_{abc}\approx p_{g}(s_{\alpha})^{g}g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\Sigma^{\alpha}_{def},}

(421)

which is (for
p_{g}=-1
) our first example of a symmetry constraint of the second kind.

(ii) For our second example of a symmetry constraint of the second kind, we consider a hybrid impurity potential
V^{\text{im}}=V^{m}+V^{d}
that is the sum of a monopole and dipole potential, which individually have distinct
g
-parities. For small momentum scattering within a single valley of a Dirac-Weyl fermion, we assume that matrix elements (
V^{d}_{b\boldsymbol{k},b\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}}
) of the dipole potential are suppressed by a multiplicative factor
|\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}|/
(Brillouin-zone period) compared to matrix elements of the monopole potential. We therefore approximate
|V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}+V^{d}_{BB^{\prime}}|^{2}\approx|V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}|^{2}
within the symmetric transition rate matrix element [Eq. (
362
)]:

\displaystyle{&w^{s}_{BB^{\prime}}=W^{s}_{BB^{\prime}}-\delta_{BB^{\prime}}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}W^{s}_{BB^{\prime\prime}};\;\;\;\;W^{s}_{BB^{\prime}}\approx\tfrac{2\pi}{\hbar}N_{\text{imp}}|V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}|^{2}\delta(E_{BB^{\prime}}).}

(422)

We emphasize this approximation by writing
w^{s}=w^{s}(H^{0},V^{m})
as an algorithm that inputs
V^{m}
, not
V^{m}+V^{d}
.

By splitting
V^{\text{im}}_{BB^{\prime}}=V^{m}_{BB^{\prime}}+V^{d}_{BB^{\prime}}
in the asymmetric transition rate matrix element [Eq. (
362
)], we decompose this matrix element into a sum of eight terms:

\displaystyle{&w^{a}_{BB^{\prime}}=\sum_{\alpha\beta\delta}dw^{a}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{0},V^{\alpha},V^{\beta},V^{\delta});\;\;\;\;dw^{a}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{0},V^{\alpha},V^{\beta},V^{\delta})=\tfrac{(2\pi)^{2}}{\hbar}N_{\text{imp}}\mathrm{Im}\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}V^{\alpha}_{BB^{\prime\prime}}V^{\beta}_{B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime}}V^{\delta}_{B^{\prime}B}\delta(E_{BB^{\prime}})\delta(E_{BB^{\prime\prime}}),}

(423)

with all Greek indices being summed over
\{m,d\}
. Being a linear functional of
w^{a}
[Eq. (
380
)], the photovoltaic tensor also splits into eight terms:

\displaystyle{\Sigma^{l}=\sum_{\alpha\beta\delta}d\Sigma^{l,\alpha\beta\delta};\;\;\;\;d\Sigma_{abc}^{l,\alpha\beta\delta}=&\;d\Sigma_{abc}^{l,\alpha\beta\delta}\big(\,H^{0},w^{s}(H^{0},V^{m}),dw^{a}(H^{0},V^{\alpha},V^{\beta},V^{\delta})\,\big){\\
}=\;-\tfrac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{BB^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime\prime\prime}}v_{B,a}(w^{s})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{BB^{\prime}}dw^{a}_{B^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}}(w^{s})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime\prime\prime}}\delta I^{ex,s,l}_{B^{\prime\prime\prime},bc}.}

Four of these eight terms vanish for
n
-Dirac/Weyl fermions [Eqs. (
47
)-(
48
)] coupled to overscreened hybrid impurities, leading to

\displaystyle{\text{$n$-Dirac/Weyl Hamiltonians $H^{d,n}$ and $H^{w,n}$}:\;\;\;\;\Sigma^{l}=d\Sigma^{l,ddd}+d\Sigma^{l,mdd}+d\Sigma^{l,dmd}+d\Sigma^{l,ddm}.}

(424)

Namely,
d\Sigma^{l,mmm}
(and also
dw^{a}(H^{0},V^{m},V^{m},V^{m})
) vanishes by the
\vec{H}
-polarity theorem, because
n
-Dirac/Weyl fermions [Eqs. (
47
)-(
48
)] do not admit a
\vec{H}
-vector polarization. Moreover,

\displaystyle{0=&\;d\Sigma^{l,mmd}+d\Sigma^{l,mdm}+d\Sigma^{l,dmm},}

(425)

because their net contribution to the asymmetric transition rate [obtained by plugging into Eq. (
423
) the explicit impurity matrix elements in Eqs. (
53
)-(
53
) and Eqs. (
62
)-(
62
)] is proportional to

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{d}}\cdot\sum_{B^{\prime\prime}}\bigg[(\boldsymbol{k}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime})+(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime\prime}-\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime})+(\boldsymbol{k}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{k})\bigg]\langle u_{B}\,|\,u_{B}^{\prime\prime}\rangle\langle u_{B^{\prime\prime}}\,|\,u_{B}^{\prime}\rangle\langle u_{B^{\prime}}\,|\,u_{B}\rangle\delta(E_{BB^{\prime\prime}})=0.}

(426)

By
g
-transforming all the Hamiltonian inputs to
w^{s}
and
d\omega^{s}
in Eq. (
D.7.2
),

\displaystyle{&w^{s}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{0,g},V^{m,g})\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{wBBp2}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;w^{s}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}(H^{0,e},V^{m,e}){\\
}dw^{a}_{BB^{\prime}}(H^{0,g},V^{\alpha,g},V^{\beta,g},V^{\delta,g})\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{essentially}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;i_{g}dw^{a}_{g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B,g^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}B^{\prime}}(H^{0,e},V^{\alpha,e},V^{\beta,e},V^{\delta,e}).}

(427)

To simplify notation, we henceforth denote an algorithm
A
with all its Hamiltonian inputs being
g
-transformed as
A(H^{g})
, or sometimes
A|_{H^{g}}
.
Combining Eqs. (
427
)-(
427
) with previously derived relations for the velocity matrix [Eq. (
385
)] and photo-excitation tensor [Eq. (
375
)], we obtain an expression for
d\Sigma^{l}
with
g
-transformed Hamiltonian inputs:

\displaystyle{d\Sigma_{abc}^{\alpha,\alpha\beta\delta}(H^{g})=&\;-\tfrac{|e|}{{\cal V}}\sum_{BB^{\prime}B^{\prime\prime}B^{\prime\prime\prime}}\big(i_{g}g^{s}_{ad}v_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B,d}\big)(w^{s})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B,g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B^{\prime}}(i_{g}dw^{a}_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B^{\prime},g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B^{\prime\prime}})(w^{s})^{{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}}_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B^{\prime\prime},g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B^{\prime\prime\prime}}\big(g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}\delta I^{ex,s,\alpha}_{g^{\scriptscriptstyle{-1}}B^{\prime\prime\prime},ef}\big)\bigg|_{H^{e}}{\\
}=\;(s_{\alpha})^{g}g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}d\Sigma_{def}^{\alpha,\alpha\beta\delta}(H^{e}).}

This relation holds no matter the symmetry of
H
.

Let
g
be a symmetry of the zeroth-order Hamiltonian (
H^{0,g}=H^{0,e}
), and be either a symmetry or antisymmetry of the impurity potential:
V^{g}=\pm V^{e}
. Defining a
g
-parity
p_{g}^{\alpha}
[Eq. (
418
)] for each of the three impurity potentials in
dw^{a}
, we find that the response tensor
d\Sigma^{\alpha}
can be assigned a
g
-parity which is the net
g
-parity for all three impurity potentials:

\displaystyle{H^{0,g}=H^{0,e},\;\;\;\;V^{\alpha,g}=p_{g}^{\alpha}V^{\alpha,e}:\;\;\;\;d\Sigma_{abc}^{\alpha,\alpha\beta\delta}(H^{g})\;\;\;\;\begin{subarray}{c}\scriptscriptstyle{\text{Eq. }(\ref{dSigmal}})\\
=\end{subarray}\;\;\;\;(-1)^{N_{\alpha\beta\delta;g\text{-odd}}}d\Sigma_{abc}^{\alpha,\alpha\beta\delta}(H^{e});\;\;\;\;(-1)^{N_{\alpha\beta\delta;g\text{-odd}}}=p_{g}^{\alpha}p_{g}^{\beta}p_{g}^{\delta},}

(428)

with
N_{\alpha\beta\delta;g\text{-odd}}
being the number of potentials in
\{V^{\alpha},V^{\beta},V^{\delta}\}
that are
g
-antisymmetric. Combining Eq. (
428
) with Eq. (
D.7.2
),

\displaystyle{H^{0,g}=H^{0,e},\;\;\;\;V^{\alpha,g}=p_{g}^{\alpha}V^{\alpha,e}:\;\;\;\;d\Sigma_{abc}^{\alpha,\alpha\beta\delta}=(-1)^{N_{\alpha\beta\delta;g-\text{odd}}}(s_{\alpha})^{g}g^{s}_{ad}g^{s}_{be}g^{s}_{cf}d\Sigma_{def}^{\alpha,\alpha\beta\delta},}

(429)

which is our second example of a second-kind constraint.

D.8
Symmetry constraints for the photovoltaic response of
n
-Weyl fermions

Our main result is summarized in Tab.
22
and derived in the remainder of this section.

Table 22:
For
n
-Weyl fermions (
H^{w,n}
) coupled to monopolar (
V^{m}
) or x-oriented dipolar (
V^{d\shortparallel x}
) impurities or their combination, we list their symmetry groups (third column), symmetry-allowed linear/circular shift/ballistic photovoltaic tensor elements based on general symmetry constraints (fourth and fifth columns), and
H
-specific symmetry constraints of the impurity-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor (sixth column). ‘-’ means there are no nontrivial
H
-specific constraints.
d\Sigma^{\alpha,\{\beta\delta\gamma\}}
means all permutations of
(\beta\delta\gamma)
are symmetry-allowed.

The group of the Hamiltonian of
n
-Weyl fermions [App.
A.2.2
] coupled to monopolar impurities is

\displaystyle{G(H^{w,n}+V^{m})=D_{\infty}^{T}\equiv D_{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}.}

(430)

We have introduced the notation
G(H)
for a symmetry group
G
of the Hamiltonian
H
.
By assumption, monopolar impurities are
O(3)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
-symmetric. Let us separately discuss the role of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
,
D_{2}
and
SO(2)
symmetry:

(
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
) Time-reversal symmetry ensures that the circular tensor vanishes:

\displaystyle{H^{T}=&\;H^{e}\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\Sigma^{c}=0,}

(431)

according to Eq. (
417
) with
g=T,g^{s}_{ab}=\delta_{ab}
and
(s_{c})^{T}=-1.
This conclusion holds not just for
n
-Weyl fermions but also for massless
n
-Dirac fermions (which are
T
-symmetric), and not just for monopole impurities but also for dipole impurities (also
T
-symmetric). This justifies the zeros in the fifth column of Tab.
22
.
Only massive
n
-Dirac fermions break
T
symmetry and can in principle sustain a circular photovoltaic current.

(
D_{2}
) The point group
D_{2}
includes all two-fold rotations. According to to
Tab.
10
, the symmetry-allowed tensor elements have odd
N_{abc;j}
for all
j\in\{x,y,z\}
, with
N_{abc;j}
meaning the number of j indices in the subscript of
\Sigma^{l}_{abc}
. This limits us to
\Sigma_{\{xyz\}}
, which includes permutations of ‘
xyz
’.

(
SO(2)
) This symmetry allows for only one independent tensor element in the set
\Sigma_{\{xyz\}}
[Eq. (
D.6.1
)], which we write in the second row, fourth column of Tab.
22
.

(
\vec{H}
-polarization theorem) The
\vec{H}
-vector representation of
D_{\infty}^{T}\equiv D_{\infty}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
does not contain any invariant vectors, hence the impurity-mediated
\Sigma^{\text{im},l}_{\text{ballistic}}=0
[cf. second row, last column of Tab.
22
].

Let
V^{d\shortparallel z}
be the impurity potential for dipole impurity oriented parallel to the
z
axis. Such an impurity breaks
C_{2x/2y}
but preserves
SO(2)
symmetry:

\displaystyle{G(H^{w,n}+V^{d\shortparallel z})=SO(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}.}

(432)

This group restricts us to the linear photovoltaic elements in Eq. (
D.6.1
), which are reproduced in the third row of Tab.
22
.

(
\cancel{C_{2x/2y}}
) Because
C_{2x}
(and
C_{2y}
) is a symmetry of
H^{w,n}
and an antisymmetry of
V^{d\shortparallel z}
, we obtain from Eq. (
421
) two second-kind constraints:

\displaystyle{\Sigma^{l}_{abc}=-(C_{2x/2y})_{ad}(C_{2x/2y})_{be}(C_{2x/2y})_{cf}\Sigma^{l}_{def}=(-1)^{N_{abc;x/y}}\Sigma^{l}_{abc},}

(433)

which should be viewed
as separate equations for
x
and
y
. This implies that
\Sigma^{l}_{abc}=0
if the number of x indices is odd, or if the number of y indices is odd. Imposing this restriction on Eq. (
D.6.1
) does not give us new information.

Let
V^{d\shortparallel x}
be the impurity potential for dipole impurity oriented parallel to the
x
axis. Such an impurity breaks
C_{2y/2z}
but preserves
C_{2x}
symmetry:

\displaystyle{G(H^{w,n}+V^{d\shortparallel z})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{C_{2x}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T},}

(434)

with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{C_{2x}}
the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
group generated by
{C_{2x}}
.

(
C_{2x}
) According to
Tab.
10
, the symmetry-allowed tensor elements have odd
N_{abc;x}
[cf. fifth of Tab.
22
].

(
\cancel{C_{2y/2z}}
) Because
C_{2y}
(and
C_{2z}
) is a symmetry of
H^{w,n}
and an antisymmetry of
V^{d\shortparallel x}
, we obtain from Eq. (
421
) two second-kind constraints:

\displaystyle{\Sigma^{l}_{abc}=-(C_{2z/2y})_{ad}(C_{2z/2y})_{be}(C_{2z/2y})_{cf}\Sigma^{l}_{def}=(-1)^{N_{abc;z/y}}\Sigma^{l}_{abc},}

(435)

which should be viewed
as separate equations for
z
and
y
. This constraints both
N_{abc;y}
and
N_{abc;z}
to be even, while
N_{abc;x}
remains odd from the previously-explained general contraint [cf. fifth row. last column of Tab.
22
].

Let us consider the hybrid impurity potential that is a sum of monopolar and dipolar components:

\displaystyle{G(H^{w,n}+V^{m}+V^{d\shortparallel z})=SO(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}.}

(436)

This group restricts us to the linear photovoltaic elements in Eq. (
D.6.1
) [fourth row of Tab.
22
].

(
\cancel{C_{2x/2y}}
) Each of these tensor elements can be decomposed according to Eq. (
424
), with each component satisfying two second-type constraints [Eq. (
429
)]:

\displaystyle{d\Sigma_{abc}^{l,\alpha\beta\delta}=(-1)^{N_{abc;x/y}+N_{\alpha\beta\delta;C_{2x/2y}\text{-odd}}+1}d\Sigma_{abc}^{l,\alpha\beta\delta},}

(437)

which should be viewed as separate equations for
x
and
y
. For tensor elements in Eq. (
D.6.1
) with an even number of
x
or
y
indices, what’s symmetry-allowed are
d\Sigma^{l,ddd}_{zzz/zxx/zyy/xxz/yyz}
, For tensor elements in Eq. (
D.6.1
) with an odd number of
x
or
y
indices, what’s symmetry-allowed are
d\Sigma^{l,\{mdd\}}_{xyz/yzx}
, which include all permutations of
(mdd)
. This justifies the fourth row, last column of Tab.
22
.

Let us consider the hybrid impurity potential that is a sum of monopolar and dipolar components:

\displaystyle{G(H^{w,n}+V^{m}+V^{d\shortparallel x})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{C_{2x}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}.}

(438)

(
C_{2x}
) The same general constraint [Tab.
10
] allows for nonzero

\Sigma^{l}_{xxx/\{xyy\}/\{xzz\}/\{xyz\}}.

(
\cancel{C_{2y/2z}}
) Each of these tensor elements can be decomposed according to Eq. (
424
), with each component satisfying two second-type constraints [Eq. (
429
)]:

\displaystyle{d\Sigma_{abc}^{l,\alpha\beta\delta}=(-1)^{N_{abc;y/z}+N_{\alpha\beta\delta;C_{2y/2z}\text{-odd}}+1}d\Sigma_{abc}^{l,\alpha\beta\delta},}

(439)

which should be viewed as separate equations for
y
and
z
. Thus for tensor elements with an even number of
y
or
z
indices, what’s symmetry-allowed are
d\Sigma^{l,ddd/}_{xxx,\{xyy\}/\{xzz\}}
; for tensor elements with an odd number of
y
or
z
indices, what’s symmetry-allowed are
d\Sigma^{l,\{mdd\}}_{\{xyz\}}
, which include all permutations of
(mdd)
. This justifies the last entry in Tab.
22
.

D.9
Symmetry constraints for the photovoltaic response of
n
-Dirac fermions

In a ‘2D system’, we restrict current and electric fields to lie in the xy-plane, so the indices in
\Sigma_{abc}
can only be x or y indices. Let us define
N_{abc;x}
(resp.
N_{abc;y}
) as the number of ‘abc’ which are x indices (resp. y indices); because
N_{abc;x}+N_{abc;y}=3
,
N_{abc;x}
is odd if and only if
N_{abc;y}
is even. Our main result is summarized in Tab.
23
and derived in the remainder of this section.

Fermion

Impurity

Group

Shift
l
/Ballistic
l

Shift
c
/Ballistic
c

\Sigma_{\text{ballistic}}^{\text{im},l/c}

H^{d,n}

V^{m}

O(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}

0

0

-

V^{d\shortparallel x}

\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{M_{y}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}

\Sigma^{l}_{xxx/\{xyy\}}

0

-

V^{m}+V^{d\shortparallel x}

\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{M_{y}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}

\Sigma^{l}_{xxx/\{xyy\}}

H^{md,n}

V^{m}

SO(2)

0

0

-

V^{d\shortparallel x}

V^{m}+V^{d\shortparallel x}

d\Sigma^{l/c,ddd}_{\text{All}}

Table 23:
For massless (
H^{d,n}
) and massive (
H^{md,n}
)
n
-Dirac fermions, coupled to monopolar (
V^{m}
) or x-oriented dipolar (
V^{d\shortparallel x}
) impurities or their combination, we list their symmetry groups (third column), symmetry-allowed linear/circular shift/ballistic photovoltaic tensor elements based on general symmetry constraints (fourth and fifth columns), and
H
-specific symmetry constraints of the impurity-mediated ballistic photovoltaic tensor (sixth column). ‘-’ means there are no nontrivial
H
-specific constraints.
\Sigma_{\text{All}}
means that all tensor elements of
\Sigma
are symmetry-allowed. ‘0’ means all tensor elements vanish.

C_{2z}
[which maps
(x,y)
to
(-x,-y)
] is an element in both
O(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T}
and
SO(2)
, which are respectively the symmetry groups of massless and massive
n
-Dirac fermions [Tab.
16
]. This remains true if the fermions are coupled to monopole impurities:

\displaystyle{G(H^{d,n}+V^{m})=O(2)\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T};\;\;\;\;G(H^{md,n}+V^{m})=SO(2).}

(440)

According to Tab.
10
,
C_{2z}
enforces
\Sigma_{abc}=0
for any
(abc)
in a 2D system [Eq. (
387
)], and for both linear and circular components of
\Sigma
. This justifies the zeros in the second and fifth rows of Tab.
23
.

C_{2z}
symmetry is broken by any impurity with an in-plane dipole moment; the corresponding impurity potential is
C_{2z}
-antisymmetric, which implies a second-kind constraint [Eq. (
421
)] which is trivial:
\Sigma=\Sigma
. Suppose the dipole moment is aligned in the
x
direction (with corresponding impurity potential
V^{d\shortparallel x}
), then the symmetry groups of massless and massive Dirac fermions are reduced to

\displaystyle{G(H^{d,n}+V^{d\shortparallel x})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{M_{y}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T};\;\;\;\;G(H^{md,n}+V^{d\shortparallel x})=\mathbb{Z}_{1}^{e},}

(441)

with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{M_{y}}
the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
group generated by mirror reflection
M_{y}:(x,y)\mapsto(x,-y)
, and
\mathbb{Z}_{1}^{e}
being the trivial group. We discuss the two fermion classes in turn:

(Massless) The photovoltaic theorems [Eqs. (
387
)-(
387
)] imply that (i)
\Sigma^{c}=0
(due to
T
symmetry), and (ii)
\Sigma_{abc}\neq 0
requires
N_{abc;y}
to be even (due to
M_{y}
symmetry):

\displaystyle{H^{d,n}+V^{d\shortparallel x}:\;\;\;\;\text{only $\Sigma^{l}_{xxx/\{xyy\}}$ are symmetry-allowed; cf. third row of Tab.\ \ref{tab:ndiracconstraint}}.}

(442)

Because
H^{d,n}
is
M_{x}
-symmetric and
V^{d\shortparallel x}
is
M_{x}
-antisymmetric, a constraint of the second kind [Eq. (
421
)] implies that
\Sigma_{abc}\neq 0
requires
N_{abc;x}
to be odd. However, we do not gain more information than what is stated in Eq. (
442
).

(Massive) There are no general or
H
-specific first-kind constraints because the Hamiltonian is trivially symmetric. The second-kind constraint (associated to
C_{2}
being a symmetry of
H^{md,n}
and an antisymmetry of
V^{d\shortparallel x}
) is trivial, as noted earlier.

\displaystyle{H^{d,n}+V^{d\shortparallel x}:\;\;\;\;\text{all $\Sigma^{l,c}$ elements are symmetry-allowed; cf. sixth row of Tab.\ \ref{tab:ndiracconstraint}}.}

(443)

Finally let us consider the hybrid impurity potential that is a sum of monopolar and dipolar components:

\displaystyle{G(H^{d,n}+V^{m}+V^{d\shortparallel x})=\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{M_{y}}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{T};\;\;\;\;G(H^{md,n}+V^{m}+V^{d\shortparallel x})=\mathbb{Z}_{1}^{e}.}

(444)

(Massless) The same symmetry group and same general constraints apply as in Eq. (
442
). Combining these general constraints with the second-kind constraint [Eq. (
429
), with either
g=C_{2}
or
g=M_{x}
, applied to Eq. (
424
)],

\displaystyle{H^{d,n}+V^{m}+V^{d\shortparallel x}:\;\;\;\;\text{$\Sigma^{l}_{xxx/\{xyy\}}$ and $d\Sigma^{l,ddd}_{xxx/\{xyy\}}$ are symmetry-allowed; cf. fourth row of Tab.\ \ref{tab:ndiracconstraint}}.}

(445)

(Massive) Applying Eq. (
429
) (with
g=C_{2}
) to Eq. (
424
) gives a second-kind constraint:

\displaystyle{H^{md,n}+V^{m}+V^{d\shortparallel x}:\;\;\;\;\text{$\Sigma^{l,c}_{abc}$ and $d\Sigma^{l/c,ddd}_{abc}$ are symmetry-allowed, for any $(abc)$; cf. seventh row of Tab.\ \ref{tab:ndiracconstraint}.}}

(446)
